In the room. The lesson pages estimate 100 + 95 minutes for a solo learner. After the warm-up, the break and the wrap-up the room has 150, so for each lesson the room does the mental model, the live demo, one guided kit lab, and the first three steps of the lesson project.

Goes home (2 to 3 hours). Both projects' Steps 4 to 6 (the honest churn evaluation and the fair bake-off); each lesson's Hands-on Exercises 1 to 3 (Exercise 4 is a stretch); both Quick Quizzes; both journal entries; the "Before the Next Lesson" lists of both lessons. The second lab of each lesson (fraud_validation_starter.py, regularization_check_starter.py) and the two debug_*.py handouts are optional practice.

Protect, in this order: 1.2 demo Steps 2, 8 and 12 to 13 (the wobble, the flattering random split, the leak audit); 1.3 demo Steps 4, 6, 8 and 11 (one line, the pipeline, the paired comparison, the three splitters); TODO 1 to 3 of each guided lab. Cut first: 1.2 Step 7 (show the calendar table on the page instead of typing it), then 1.3 Step 10 (show the time-fold output on the page), then 1.2 Step 10 (the group split: one sentence and the two numbers), then the second half of each guided lab.

Opening Segment

0:00

Warm-up: Retrieval and Homework Check

10 min
Timing
5mFive retrieval questions from Session 1 (workbook Warm-up page): learners write, then compare with a neighbor
5mHomework check: the random_state=7 rerun and the constant=1 dummy; who finished the churn shortlist
Five retrieval questions, with answers
  1. W1 On the churn table, what does always predicting "stays" score? And "never fraud" on the payments? Answer: 0.835 on churn (16.5% churn) and 0.9912 on fraud (0.88% fraud). Every score today is read against a do-nothing baseline.
  2. W2 Which column of customers_churn.csv must never be a feature, and why? Answer: cancellation_reason: it is filled in only after a customer has left, so on prediction day it is always empty. Today Lesson 1.2 measures what it does to a model (0.848 becomes 0.941).
  3. W3 train_test_split(X, y, ...) returns four things. In which order? Answer: X_train, X_test, y_train, y_test: the X parts, then the y parts. Today's Common Mistake 1 is the wrong order.
  4. W4 Why is the scaler fitted on the training rows only, and then used to transform both sets? Answer: The test rows stand in for future customers, whose numbers could not have been seen when the model was built. Today the same rule moves inside every cross-validation fold.
  5. W5 Homework: what did DummyClassifier(strategy="constant", constant=1) score on the 1.1 test set? Answer: 0.165 (0.165): it calls everyone a churner, so it is right exactly for the 16.5% who churn. Accuracy rewards the majority class.
Homework check (from 1.1 "Before the Next Lesson")

Ask for hands: "Who reran the shortlist project with random_state=7? What moved?" The reference rerun (the 1.1 project's split, scaler, baseline and forest, seed 7 in the split only):

random_state=42  baseline 0.835  forest 0.873  top-50 hits 41
random_state= 7  baseline 0.835  forest 0.876  top-50 hits 45
  • The baseline does not move (0.835 both times): the split is stratified, so the test churn share, and with it the baseline, is pinned. That is today's second demo step.
  • The forest moves (0.873 to 0.876) and so does its shortlist (41 to 45 real churners in the top 50). Do not explain it; say: "How much of a score is luck? That is the first experiment after this warm-up." It is the bridge into 1.2.
  • The constant dummy scores 0.165 (W5). Anyone who predicted 0.835 mixed up which class it always predicts: ask them to say the strategy in words.
  • Shortlist not finished? It is not needed today (1.2 and 1.3 start fresh notebooks), but the habit is: ask them to finish it with the 1.2 project this week.
Watch-outs
  • Keep it to 10 minutes. The warm-up is retrieval, not reteaching. Anything longer than a sentence goes to the 1.2 demo, which revisits each point.
  • NOT READY machines from Session 1: check them during the warm-up (the learner runs check_environment.py while the room writes). Still not ready: pair them for today and fix it at the break.

Lesson Plans

1.2

Splits, Baselines and Leakage

75 min
Objectives (from the lesson page)
  • Measure how much a test score moves from one random split to another, and explain why stratifying helps
  • Make a train / validation / test split and use each part for its one job
  • Split by time and by group when rows are related, and show how much a careless random split flatters a model
  • Pick a baseline that fits the problem (most frequent class, mean, yesterday, same day last week)
  • Detect target leakage and train-test contamination, and remove them
Timing in the room
10mFrame: the dress rehearsal; the careless evaluation always looks better
30mLive demo: 30 splits, stratify, three parts, a time split and forecast baselines, a group split, the leak and the audit
20mGuided lab: demand_forecast_starter.py (a time split and three free baselines for next year's demand)
15mStart the lesson project: an honest churn evaluation, Steps 1 to 3
Mental model to say

Say: "A theater company rehearses for weeks, then holds one dress rehearsal run exactly like opening night: full costumes, no stopping, no script. If the dress rehearsal is easier than opening night, a prompter whispering lines or half the audience missing, it tells you nothing about the real show."

Then the rule, written on the board and left there: evaluate the model under the same conditions it will face in real use. "If it will predict next month from data up to this month, the test must be later than the training data. If it will score brand-new products, the test must hold products it has never seen. If a column will not exist at prediction time, the model must not see it in testing either."

Then the warning that makes the lesson stick: "Every section today breaks the rule in a different way, and every time, the careless evaluation looks BETTER. Nobody goes looking for a bug when the score goes up. That is why these mistakes survive."

Live demo (you type, they follow)

New notebook, lesson_1_2. The code is the lesson's own, in order, minus the plotting cells: show those charts on the page instead of typing them. Ask for a prediction before Steps 2, 5, 8, 10 and 12.

  1. Same customers, same features (1m). "We start exactly where Lesson 1.1 ended: 6,000 customers, the ten clean numeric features, 16.5% churn." 
    import numpy as np
import pandas as pd
import matplotlib.pyplot as plt
from sklearn.model_selection import train_test_split
from sklearn.preprocessing import StandardScaler
from sklearn.linear_model import LogisticRegression
from sklearn.dummy import DummyClassifier

customers = pd.read_csv("data/customers_churn.csv")
features = ["tenure_months", "orders_12m", "spend_12m", "avg_order_value", "days_since_last_order",
            "returns_12m", "return_rate", "support_tickets_12m", "discount_share", "app_user"]
X = customers[features]
y = customers["churned"]
print(X.shape, f"churn rate {y.mean():.3f}")
    (6000, 10) churn rate 0.165
  2. One split is one sample (3m). "In 1.1 logistic regression scored 0.848 on the split with seed 42. Forty-two was arbitrary. Let's ask thirty other seeds." Before running: "Write down the lowest and highest score you expect." 
    def split_and_score(seed, test_size=0.25):
    X_train, X_test, y_train, y_test = train_test_split(
        X, y, test_size=test_size, stratify=y, random_state=seed
    )
    scaler = StandardScaler().fit(X_train)
    model = LogisticRegression().fit(scaler.transform(X_train), y_train)
    return model.score(scaler.transform(X_test), y_test)

scores = pd.Series([split_and_score(seed) for seed in range(30)])
print(scores.describe().round(3))
    count    30.000
mean      0.851
std       0.007
min       0.836
25%       0.849
50%       0.852
75%       0.855
max       0.866
dtype: float64
    Show the lesson page's histogram. Say: "Same model, same data. Only the luck of which 1,500 customers landed in the test set changed, and the score moved from 0.836 to 0.866. The model's lead over the baseline is about 0.016: barely two wobbles." Write wobble ≈ 0.007 on the board; it comes back after the break.
  3. Stratify, or the yardstick moves too (2m). "Without stratify=y the churn share in the test set wobbles as well, and the baseline is one minus that share." 
    test_rates = {"random": [], "stratified": []}
for seed in range(30):
    *_, y_te = train_test_split(X, y, test_size=0.25, random_state=seed)
    test_rates["random"].append(y_te.mean())
    *_, y_te = train_test_split(X, y, test_size=0.25, stratify=y, random_state=seed)
    test_rates["stratified"].append(y_te.mean())

print(pd.DataFrame(test_rates).agg(["min", "max"]).round(3))
         random  stratified
min   0.151       0.165
max   0.181       0.165
    *_, y_te = ... keeps only the last of the four results. Rule for the board: stratify classification splits on the target, never on a feature.
  4. Three parts, three jobs (2m). "Comparing models on the test set again and again turns it into training data: you learn from it. So we carve a third part." Draw the 60/20/20 bar: train fits, validation chooses, test grades once. 
    X_rest, X_test, y_rest, y_test = train_test_split(X, y, test_size=0.20, stratify=y, random_state=42)
X_train, X_val, y_train, y_val = train_test_split(X_rest, y_rest, test_size=0.25, stratify=y_rest, random_state=42)

print(len(X_train), len(X_val), len(X_test))
print(f"churn rates: {y_train.mean():.3f} {y_val.mean():.3f} {y_test.mean():.3f}")
    3600 1200 1200
churn rates: 0.166 0.165 0.165
    Point at stratify=y_rest in the second call: you stratify on the target of the data you are splitting.
  5. Validation makes a real decision (3m). "How deep should the tree be? Training accuracy will say 'deeper'. Watch validation." 
    from sklearn.tree import DecisionTreeClassifier

val_by_depth = {}
for depth in [2, 3, 4, 5, 6, 8, 12]:
    tree = DecisionTreeClassifier(max_depth=depth, random_state=42).fit(X_train, y_train)
    val_by_depth[depth] = tree.score(X_val, y_val)
    print(f"max_depth={depth:2d}  train {tree.score(X_train, y_train):.3f}  validation {val_by_depth[depth]:.3f}")

best_depth = max(val_by_depth, key=val_by_depth.get)
print("chosen depth:", best_depth)
    max_depth= 2  train 0.858  validation 0.849
max_depth= 3  train 0.872  validation 0.864
max_depth= 4  train 0.876  validation 0.864
max_depth= 5  train 0.886  validation 0.859
max_depth= 6  train 0.898  validation 0.858
max_depth= 8  train 0.921  validation 0.848
max_depth=12  train 0.978  validation 0.823
chosen depth: 3
    Training climbs with every level; validation peaks and falls: overfitting, used to make a choice. Depths 3 and 4 tie exactly (1037 of 1,200 correct each, checked), and max keeps the first: the simpler tree, which is the right tie-break.
  6. Open the envelope, once (2m). "Now, and only now, the test set. Refit the chosen depth on train plus validation, grade it once, next to the baseline." 
    final_tree = DecisionTreeClassifier(max_depth=best_depth, random_state=42).fit(X_rest, y_rest)
baseline = DummyClassifier(strategy="most_frequent").fit(X_rest, y_rest)

print(f"baseline   test accuracy {baseline.score(X_test, y_test):.3f}")
print(f"tree (d={best_depth}) test accuracy {final_tree.score(X_test, y_test):.3f}")
    baseline   test accuracy 0.835
tree (d=3) test accuracy 0.858
    0.858 is a little below the best validation score (0.864). Ask why. (The validation winner is partly the lucky one.) "This is the number you report. If it disappoints, you do NOT go back and try more depths."
  7. A table that has a calendar (2m). "New question: daily sales per store. On opening night the model is trained on everything up to today and asked about days that have not happened yet." 
    sales = pd.read_csv("data/daily_store_sales.csv", parse_dates=["date"])
sales["store_code"] = sales["store"].map({"Asheville": 0, "Bend": 1, "Boise": 2, "Flagstaff": 3})
sales["day_index"] = (sales["date"] - sales["date"].min()).dt.days
sales["day_of_week"] = sales["date"].dt.dayofweek
sales["day_of_year"] = sales["date"].dt.dayofyear
sales["closed"] = (sales["event"] == "Closed").astype(int)

calendar = ["store_code", "day_index", "day_of_week", "day_of_year", "promo", "closed"]
print(sales[["date", "store", "sales"] + calendar].head(5))
            date      store    sales  store_code  day_index  day_of_week  day_of_year  promo  closed
0 2023-01-01  Asheville  4838.69           0          0            6            1      0       0
1 2023-01-01       Bend  4533.55           1          0            6            1      0       0
2 2023-01-01      Boise  6026.92           2          0            6            1      0       0
3 2023-01-01  Flagstaff  4217.59           3          0            6            1      0       0
4 2023-01-02  Asheville  2695.58           0          1            0            2      0       0
    Store as a code, the date as a running day count, day of week, day of year. Keep it quick: the point is the next step.
  8. Random split vs time split (3m). "Same forest, same features, same number of test days. Once on a random split, once trained on 2023 and 2024 and tested on 2025. Predict: which one looks better?" 
    from sklearn.ensemble import RandomForestRegressor
from sklearn.metrics import mean_absolute_error

def forest_mae(train, test):
    forest = RandomForestRegressor(n_estimators=200, min_samples_leaf=3, random_state=42, n_jobs=-1)
    forest.fit(train[calendar], train["sales"])
    return mean_absolute_error(test["sales"], forest.predict(test[calendar]))
is_2025 = sales["date"].dt.year == 2025
past, future = sales[~is_2025], sales[is_2025]
shuffled_train, shuffled_test = train_test_split(sales, test_size=len(future), random_state=42)

print(f"test rows: {len(future):,}")
print(f"random split MAE: ${forest_mae(shuffled_train, shuffled_test):,.0f}")
print(f"time split MAE:   ${forest_mae(past, future):,.0f}")
    test rows: 1,460
random split MAE: $613
time split MAE:   $854
    Show the Boise train/test chart from the page. Say: "The random split claims $600 a day; reality is $850. Sales have momentum, and with a random split almost every test day has its neighbors in training. Tomorrow's neighbors are never available."
  9. Is $850 good? Ask the baselines (3m). "For a forecast there are two baselines much tougher than the mean, and they cost nothing: yesterday, and the same day last week. Each is a shifted column." 
    from sklearn.dummy import DummyRegressor

sales = sales.sort_values(["store", "date"])
sales["yesterday"] = sales.groupby("store")["sales"].shift(1)
sales["same_day_last_week"] = sales.groupby("store")["sales"].shift(7)
future = sales[sales["date"].dt.year == 2025]

mean_model = DummyRegressor(strategy="mean").fit(past[calendar], past["sales"])
print(f"training mean       MAE ${mean_absolute_error(future['sales'], mean_model.predict(future[calendar])):,.0f}")
print(f"yesterday           MAE ${mean_absolute_error(future['sales'], future['yesterday']):,.0f}")
print(f"same day last week  MAE ${mean_absolute_error(future['sales'], future['same_day_last_week']):,.0f}")
    training mean       MAE $1,967
yesterday           MAE $1,350
same day last week  MAE $1,072
    The forest at $854 beats the strongest free baseline ($1,072), so it earns its place. Measured dishonestly at $613 it would have looked like a triumph. Shifted columns are legal: they only look backward.
  10. Splitting by group (3m). "Pinecrest plans to launch new products. A random split tests the model on products it already knows. GroupShuffleSplit sends every row of a product to one side." 
    from sklearn.model_selection import GroupShuffleSplit

demand = pd.read_csv("data/weekly_demand.csv")
numeric = ["week_of_year", "store_sqft", "list_price", "price", "discount_pct",
           "promo", "holiday_week", "avg_high_temp_f"]

row_train, row_test = train_test_split(demand, test_size=0.25, random_state=42)

splitter = GroupShuffleSplit(n_splits=1, test_size=0.25, random_state=42)
train_idx, test_idx = next(splitter.split(demand, groups=demand["product_id"]))
group_train, group_test = demand.iloc[train_idx], demand.iloc[test_idx]

shared = set(group_train["product_id"]) & set(group_test["product_id"])
print(f"group split: {group_test['product_id'].nunique()} test products, {len(shared)} shared with training")

for name, train, test in [("random rows", row_train, row_test), ("new products", group_train, group_test)]:
    forest = RandomForestRegressor(n_estimators=200, min_samples_leaf=3, random_state=42, n_jobs=-1)
    forest.fit(train[numeric], train["units_sold"])
    mean_guess = DummyRegressor().fit(train[numeric], train["units_sold"])
    print(f"{name:12s}  forest MAE {mean_absolute_error(test['units_sold'], forest.predict(test[numeric])):5.2f}"
          f"   mean baseline MAE {mean_absolute_error(test['units_sold'], mean_guess.predict(test[numeric])):5.2f}")
    group split: 6 test products, 0 shared with training
random rows   forest MAE 10.23   mean baseline MAE 17.03
new products  forest MAE 17.84   mean baseline MAE 20.87
    About 10 units off on known products, about 18 on new ones: barely better than the mean. "Neither number is wrong. Choosing the split IS choosing the question."
  11. The leak, counted (1m). "Session 1 warned you about cancellation_reason. Count it against the outcome." 
    customers["has_reason"] = customers["cancellation_reason"].notna().astype(int)
print(pd.crosstab(customers["has_reason"], customers["churned"]))
    churned        0    1
has_reason           
0           5008  367
1              0  625
    A zero in the stayers' column: every one of the 625 customers with a reason churned.
  12. The leak, in a model (2m). "Add one flag to the ten honest features and retrain the 1.1 model on the 1.1 split." Ask for a guess first. 
    def logreg_accuracy(columns):
    X_tr, X_te, y_tr, y_te = train_test_split(
        customers[columns], y, test_size=0.25, stratify=y, random_state=42
    )
    scaler = StandardScaler().fit(X_tr)
    model = LogisticRegression().fit(scaler.transform(X_tr), y_tr)
    return model.score(scaler.transform(X_te), y_te)

print(f"10 honest features:         {logreg_accuracy(features):.3f}")
print(f"10 features + has_reason:   {logreg_accuracy(features + ['has_reason']):.3f}")
    10 honest features:         0.848
10 features + has_reason:   0.941
    "Nothing else in this course will lift a churn model this much, and that is the clue. In real use the column is always empty."
  13. The single-feature audit (3m). "Leaks rarely have a name like this. Two habits catch most of them: ask of every column 'known at prediction time?' (the lesson's flowchart), and fit a tiny model on each feature alone." 
    X_tr, X_te, y_tr, y_te = train_test_split(
    customers[features + ["has_reason"]], y, test_size=0.25, stratify=y, random_state=42
)

single = {}
for column in features + ["has_reason"]:
    stump = DecisionTreeClassifier(max_depth=2, random_state=42).fit(X_tr[[column]], y_tr)
    single[column] = stump.score(X_te[[column]], y_te)

print(pd.Series(single).sort_values(ascending=False).head(4).round(3))
print(f"baseline: {1 - y_te.mean():.3f}")
    has_reason               0.941
days_since_last_order    0.842
tenure_months            0.835
spend_12m                0.835
dtype: float64
baseline: 0.835
    Real signals are weak one at a time (recency alone reaches 0.842). One column alone at 0.941 is the alarm. "From here on, has_reason and cancellation_reason stay out of every model."
Three mistakes to expect, and how to coach each
  • The split unpacked in the wrong order, or stratified on a number (the two loud ones). X_train, y_train, X_test, y_test = ... fails at fit with ValueError: y should be a 1d array, got an array of shape (1500, 10) instead.. stratify=demand["units_sold"] fails with ValueError: The least populated classes in y have only 1 member, which is too few. ... and a screen of numbers. Coach: "Read the first sentence of the last line. What does it say y holds?" Fix: the X parts first; stratify only a classification target (or bins of a regression target, or split by time).
  • A whole number as the test size (silent). test_size=25 means 25 rows, not 25%: 5975 25. A 25-customer test set wobbles enormously; in the kit's debug_leaky_split.py it even reports a perfect 1.000. Coach: "How many customers is your score based on?" Have them print len(X_test) after every split this week.
  • Something learns from all the rows before the split (silent, and the one to fear). Selecting 20 of 2,000 noise columns on all the rows, then splitting: select, then split: 0.78, versus split, then select: 0.47 (a coin flip). A centered 7-day average on daily sales: time split MAE with a peeking feature: $504 with an honest time split. Coach with the board question: "Could I have computed this the evening before, from the training rows only?" Fix: fit every step that learns (scaler, imputer, selector) on the training rows; build time features from the past (shift(1) before rolling).
Guided lab
  • Lab A (20m, everyone): labs/module-02/l1_2-splits-and-leakage/demand_forecast_starter.py; reference labs/module-02/l1_2-splits-and-leakage/demand_forecast_solution.py. The planning team wants next year's weekly units for every product. Learners make the honest time split (2024 train, 2025 test), a random split of the same size, grade the same forest both ways, and then build three free baselines: the training mean, each store and product's 2024 average, and the same week last year. New practice on all four 1.2 moves (time split, random split for contrast, forecast baselines, report against the strongest), on data the lesson used only for the group split.
  • Run it first (1m), TODO 1 together (3m), TODO 2 and 3 in pairs (6m), TODO 4 and 5 (8m), debrief (2m). TODO 1 has a planted surprise: week_start.dt.year == 2024 holds 53 weeks (2024-12-30 is a Monday of 2024) and 2025 only 51. Ask "104 weeks, so 52 and 52?" before they print it. Checking where the line falls is part of every time split.
  • Expected: 5,088 train rows (last week 2024-12-30), 4,896 test rows (first week 2025-01-06), 53 and 51 weeks; half the random split's training rows come from 2025; forest MAE 10.45 on the random split vs 10.27 on the time split; baselines: each product's 2024 average 12.95, same week last year 13.81, training mean 17.13; the forest beats the strongest baseline.
  • The honest surprise (debrief): here the random split does NOT flatter the forest (10.45 vs 10.27), unlike daily sales ($613 vs $854). Weekly demand carries little momentum from one week to the next once season and price are known, so a test week's neighbors help little. Say: "You only know by measuring. The time split is still the right dress rehearsal, because it is how the model will be used, whatever it costs." Lesson 1.3 meets the same effect with folds (9.84 vs 10.17). Second point: the strongest free baseline is each product's usual level, not last year's same week (a single noisy week); a forecast that cannot beat 12.95 is not worth deploying.
  • Circulate for: past/future swapped (a model trained on 2025); shift(52) without sorting by store, product and week first (the shift then crosses products: ask them to print three rows); the per-product average computed on ALL rows, 2025 included (a leak: ask "which rows may the baseline learn from?").
  • Fast finishers: labs/module-02/l1_2-splits-and-leakage/fraud_validation_starter.py (train / validation / test for a fraud review queue; reference labs/module-02/l1_2-splits-and-leakage/fraud_validation_solution.py) and the handout labs/module-02/l1_2-splits-and-leakage/debug_leaky_split.py: two bugs that stop it, two that do not.
Start the lesson project

The lesson project is an honest churn evaluation: a leak audit, a sealed test set, a choice made on validation, one test score next to the baseline, and a short report (evaluation_report.txt). In the room, Steps 1 to 3 (15 minutes).

  • Read the brief aloud ("a colleague's churn model is '94% accurate'") and ask: "Where could 94% come from?" Step 1 together: split the whole table three ways so the IDs stay lined up. Done = 3600 1200 1200. From here, test is not touched, not even .head().
  • Step 2 on their own: the single-feature audit on training and validation rows. Done = suspects: ['has_reason'] with has_reason at 0.942 against a 0.835 baseline. Step 3: drop it; done = 10 features kept and baseline validation accuracy 0.835.
  • Goes home: Step 4 (choose on validation: logistic regression 0.850, tree 0.864, forest 0.870), Step 5 (open the test set once: forest 0.866, baseline 0.835) and Step 6 (evaluation_report.txt). Tell them the numbers so they can check themselves.
Checks for understanding

Ask these out loud during the demo and the lab:

  • Why stratify on the target and never on a feature? Answer: it pins the class share in every part, so the baseline (one minus the churn rate) stays fixed; a feature does nothing for that, and a continuous one crashes.
  • Validation said 0.864, the test set said 0.858. Which do you report, and may you now try another depth? Answer: report 0.858; no: after one look the test set has been used.
  • You forecast tomorrow's sales. Which baseline must the model beat? Answer: the strongest free one, same day last week ($1,072 here), not the training mean ($1,967).
  • Pinecrest will score products it has never sold. Which split? Answer: a group split by product: every row of a product on one side.
  • A new column lifts accuracy from 0.85 to 0.94. What two things do you do before celebrating? Answer: score it alone against the baseline, and find out when and how it is recorded (known at prediction time?).

Workbook self-check, items Q1 to Q6 (learners do them at the end of the block or at home).

Answers: Q1 C) A group split, GroupShuffleSplit(..., groups=demand["product_id"]), so that no test product appears in training; Q2 600 200 200; Q3 B) A 7-day average built with rolling(7, center=True); Q4 D) ValueError: y should be one-dimensional; the second result is the 1,500 test rows of X; Q5 A) Report 0.858 and keep depth 3: the test set has spoken once; Q6 30.0. Full set with explanations in the Answer Key.

Differentiation

Support: give each pair one sentence to say before every run in the demo: "This evaluation is easier / harder / the same as real use, because ...". In the lab, let a struggling pair skip TODO 2 and 3 (the random split) and go straight to the baselines: the time split plus one baseline is the core skill.

Stretch: fraud_validation_starter.py (a validation set chooses between four models by frauds in the top 100; validation said 47, the test set says 41: the winner was partly lucky); then the lesson's Exercise 4, a reusable leak detector, and the Bonus Challenge: honest lag features for the daily forecast.

1.3

Cross-Validation

75 min
Objectives (from the lesson page)
  • Explain why one train/validation split gives a noisy score, and how k-fold cross-validation averages that noise away
  • Run cross_val_score and cross_validate with a stratified, shuffled splitter, and report a mean with its spread
  • Keep preprocessing inside the folds by cross-validating a make_pipeline instead of pre-scaled data
  • Compare two models fold by fold on identical folds, and get out-of-fold predictions with cross_val_predict
  • Choose TimeSeriesSplit or GroupKFold when rows are related by time or by group
Timing in the room
10mFrame: one exam or five quizzes; cross-validation scores a recipe
30mLive demo: the loop by hand, one line, shuffled and stratified folds, a pipeline, train vs validation, paired folds, out-of-fold predictions, time and group folds
20mGuided lab: fraud_folds_starter.py (which folds tell the truth about next month's payments?)
15mStart the lesson project: a fair model bake-off, Steps 1 to 3
Mental model to say

Say: "A teacher who grades a student on one exam learns something, but a bad night's sleep or a lucky choice of questions can swing it. Five shorter quizzes, each on different material, give a steadier picture, and the spread of the five grades tells the teacher how much to trust the average. Cross-validation gives your model five quizzes instead of one exam, and it does so without using the final exam at all."

Point at the wobble you wrote on the board before the break (about 0.007 for a test set, about two points between ten splits): "Before the break we measured the noise. Now we average it away, and report its size." Then the two sentences that prevent the most confusion: "Cross-validation evaluates a recipe, not a model: it fits five models and throws them away. When you like the recipe, you fit it once on all the training rows." And: "In scikit-learn's names, 'test' inside cross-validation means the validation fold. Our real test set stays locked."

Live demo (you type, they follow)

New notebook, lesson_1_3. The lesson's code, in order, without the strip-plot cell (show it on the page). Ask for a prediction before Steps 5, 7, 8 and 11.

  1. One split is one opinion (2m). "Recall the wobble from before the break. Here it is again for a depth-4 tree and ten seeds." (Say once: this loop touches the whole table, fine for a thought experiment, never in real work.) 
    import numpy as np
import pandas as pd
from sklearn.model_selection import train_test_split
from sklearn.tree import DecisionTreeClassifier

customers = pd.read_csv("data/customers_churn.csv")
features = ["tenure_months", "orders_12m", "spend_12m", "avg_order_value", "days_since_last_order",
            "returns_12m", "return_rate", "support_tickets_12m", "discount_share", "app_user"]
X = customers[features]
y = customers["churned"]

scores = []
for seed in range(10):
    X_a, X_b, y_a, y_b = train_test_split(X, y, test_size=0.25, stratify=y, random_state=seed)
    tree = DecisionTreeClassifier(max_depth=4, random_state=42).fit(X_a, y_a)
    scores.append(tree.score(X_b, y_b))

print(np.round(scores, 3))
print(f"lowest {min(scores):.3f}   highest {max(scores):.3f}")
    [0.859 0.862 0.879 0.868 0.869 0.867 0.863 0.866 0.874 0.858]
lowest 0.858   highest 0.879
    About two points between luckiest and unluckiest. "If two models truly differ by one point, one split can crown either."
  2. Lock the test set (1m). "Everything from here happens inside the 4,500 training customers. The 1,500 test customers are the final exam; we do not touch them." 
    X_train, X_test, y_train, y_test = train_test_split(
    X, y, test_size=0.25, stratify=y, random_state=42
)
print(X_train.shape, X_test.shape)
    (4500, 10) (1500, 10)
  3. The fold loop, by hand, once (4m). "Five folds of 900. Five rounds: hold one fold out, train on the other four, score. Every training row is validated exactly once." Draw the 5 × 5 grid from the lesson page. 
    from sklearn.model_selection import StratifiedKFold

cv = StratifiedKFold(n_splits=5, shuffle=True, random_state=42)

fold_scores = []
for fold, (train_idx, val_idx) in enumerate(cv.split(X_train, y_train), start=1):
    tree = DecisionTreeClassifier(max_depth=4, random_state=42)
    tree.fit(X_train.iloc[train_idx], y_train.iloc[train_idx])
    score = tree.score(X_train.iloc[val_idx], y_train.iloc[val_idx])
    fold_scores.append(score)
    print(f"fold {fold}: train {len(train_idx)}, validate {len(val_idx)}, "
          f"churn rate {y_train.iloc[val_idx].mean():.3f}, accuracy {score:.3f}")

print(f"mean {np.mean(fold_scores):.3f}, std {np.std(fold_scores):.3f}")
    fold 1: train 3600, validate 900, churn rate 0.164, accuracy 0.859
fold 2: train 3600, validate 900, churn rate 0.166, accuracy 0.866
fold 3: train 3600, validate 900, churn rate 0.166, accuracy 0.866
fold 4: train 3600, validate 900, churn rate 0.166, accuracy 0.873
fold 5: train 3600, validate 900, churn rate 0.166, accuracy 0.850
mean 0.863, std 0.008
    Point at .iloc (positions, not labels) and at the FRESH tree inside the loop: reusing a fitted model across rounds would leak one round into the next.
  4. The same thing in one line (2m). "cross_val_score clones the model for every fold, fits, scores, and hands back the five numbers." 
    from sklearn.model_selection import cross_val_score

tree = DecisionTreeClassifier(max_depth=4, random_state=42)
scores = cross_val_score(tree, X_train, y_train, cv=cv)

print(scores.round(3))
print(f"accuracy {scores.mean():.3f} +/- {scores.std():.3f}")
    [0.859 0.866 0.866 0.873 0.85 ]
accuracy 0.863 +/- 0.008
    The same five numbers as the loop, because the same splitter object made the same folds. Report the mean plus or minus the spread. Then the warning box: it fitted five models and threw them all away. It scored a recipe. Afterward you fit the recipe once on all 4,500 rows.
  5. Why we build the splitter ourselves (3m). "cv=5 works, but it does not shuffle. Watch what that does to a table that happens to be sorted by the target." 
    from sklearn.model_selection import KFold

sorted_train = X_train.assign(churned=y_train).sort_values("churned")
X_sorted, y_sorted = sorted_train[features], sorted_train["churned"]

for name, splitter in [("KFold(5)", KFold(5)),
                       ("StratifiedKFold", StratifiedKFold(5, shuffle=True, random_state=42))]:
    rates = [y_sorted.iloc[val].mean() for _, val in splitter.split(X_sorted, y_sorted)]
    acc = cross_val_score(tree, X_sorted, y_sorted, cv=splitter)
    print(f"{name:16s} churn rate per fold {np.round(rates, 2)}  accuracy {acc.mean():.3f}")
    KFold(5)         churn rate per fold [0.   0.   0.   0.   0.83]  accuracy 0.796
StratifiedKFold  churn rate per fold [0.16 0.17 0.17 0.17 0.17]  accuracy 0.863
    Unshuffled KFold builds folds with no churners and one fold of mostly churners. The course habit for the board: StratifiedKFold(5, shuffle=True, random_state=42) for classification, KFold(5, shuffle=True, random_state=42) for regression.
  6. Preprocessing goes inside the folds (3m). "If I scale X_train first and then cross-validate, every validation fold helped compute the mean it was scaled with. Same leak as scaling with the test set, smaller and better hidden. make_pipeline glues scaler and model into one estimator that is refitted in every round." 
    from sklearn.pipeline import make_pipeline
from sklearn.preprocessing import StandardScaler
from sklearn.linear_model import LogisticRegression

logreg = make_pipeline(StandardScaler(), LogisticRegression())
print(logreg)

scores = cross_val_score(logreg, X_train, y_train, cv=cv)
print(f"logistic regression accuracy {scores.mean():.3f} +/- {scores.std():.3f}")
    Pipeline(steps=[('standardscaler', StandardScaler()),
                ('logisticregression', LogisticRegression())])
logistic regression accuracy 0.852 +/- 0.009
    Show the lesson's flow diagram. Here the leak would be tiny; keep the habit anyway, because steps that learn more (selection, encodings) leak a lot. Lesson 2.2 is all about pipelines.
  7. Train scores next to validation scores (3m). "cross_validate gives several metrics at once and, if you ask, the score on the training folds." Introduce ROC AUC in one breath: how well the model RANKS churners above stayers, 0.5 random, 1.0 perfect; Lesson 1.4 explains it. 
    from sklearn.model_selection import cross_validate

for depth in [4, None]:
    results = cross_validate(
        DecisionTreeClassifier(max_depth=depth, random_state=42), X_train, y_train, cv=cv,
        scoring=["accuracy", "roc_auc"], return_train_score=True,
    )
    summary = pd.DataFrame(results).drop(columns=["fit_time", "score_time"]).mean()
    print(f"max_depth={depth}")
    print(summary.round(3).to_string())
    max_depth=4
test_accuracy     0.863
train_accuracy    0.875
test_roc_auc      0.839
train_roc_auc     0.857
max_depth=None
test_accuracy     0.815
train_accuracy    1.000
test_roc_auc      0.663
train_roc_auc     1.000
    The unlimited tree: 1.000 on its own training folds, 0.663 AUC on validation. It memorizes. Depth 4: train and validation close. "Comparing the two columns is the cheapest overfitting check you have."
  8. A fair comparison: same folds, paired (4m). "Two rules: the same splitter for every model, so the scores are paired; and enough rounds to see the noise. Three repeats of five folds give 15 paired scores." Predict first: which wins, by how much? 
    from sklearn.model_selection import RepeatedStratifiedKFold

rcv = RepeatedStratifiedKFold(n_splits=5, n_repeats=3, random_state=42)

models = {
    "tree (depth 4)": DecisionTreeClassifier(max_depth=4, random_state=42),
    "logistic regression": logreg,
}
auc = pd.DataFrame({name: cross_val_score(model, X_train, y_train, cv=rcv, scoring="roc_auc")
                    for name, model in models.items()})

print(auc.agg(["mean", "std"]).round(3))
diff = auc["tree (depth 4)"] - auc["logistic regression"]
print(f"tree ahead in {(diff > 0).sum()} of {len(diff)} folds; mean lead {diff.mean():+.3f}")
          tree (depth 4)  logistic regression
mean           0.834                 0.83
std            0.017                 0.02
tree ahead in 12 of 15 folds; mean lead +0.004
    Show the strip plot on the lesson page. "12 of 15 sounds convincing. But the average lead is 0.004 and one model's folds spread over about 0.07. These two rank customers about equally well; prefer the simpler."
  9. Out-of-fold predictions (3m). "Sometimes you want predictions, not scores. cross_val_predict gives every training customer a probability from the fold model that did NOT train on them." 
    from sklearn.model_selection import cross_val_predict

oof_proba = cross_val_predict(logreg, X_train, y_train, cv=cv, method="predict_proba")[:, 1]
print(oof_proba.shape)

oof = pd.DataFrame({"churn_probability": oof_proba, "churned": y_train.to_numpy()})
oof["risk_decile"] = pd.qcut(oof["churn_probability"].rank(method="first"), 10, labels=range(1, 11))
print(oof.groupby("risk_decile", observed=True)["churned"].mean().round(3).to_string())
    (4500,)
risk_decile
1     0.022
2     0.033
3     0.033
4     0.040
5     0.056
6     0.089
7     0.167
8     0.258
9     0.380
10    0.576
    The same decile story as 1.1's test-set chart (top decile about 58%), without touching the test set. "Lesson 1.4 picks a decision threshold from exactly these probabilities."
  10. Time-ordered folds (2m). "Weekly demand has 96 rows per week, so we split the sorted list of 104 weeks and turn each pair of week lists into row positions. A list of (train, validation) pairs is a fine cv= argument." 
    from sklearn.model_selection import TimeSeriesSplit
from sklearn.ensemble import HistGradientBoostingRegressor

demand = pd.read_csv("data/weekly_demand.csv")
demand_features = ["price", "list_price", "discount_pct", "promo", "holiday_week",
                   "avg_high_temp_f", "store_sqft", "week_of_year"]
X_d, y_d = demand[demand_features], demand["units_sold"]

weeks = np.sort(demand["week_start"].unique())
time_folds = []
for train_w, val_w in TimeSeriesSplit(n_splits=4, test_size=13).split(weeks):
    train_rows = np.flatnonzero(demand["week_start"].isin(weeks[train_w]))
    val_rows = np.flatnonzero(demand["week_start"].isin(weeks[val_w]))
    time_folds.append((train_rows, val_rows))
    print(f"train {weeks[train_w][0]}..{weeks[train_w][-1]}  validate {weeks[val_w][0]}..{weeks[val_w][-1]}")
    train 2024-01-01..2024-12-23  validate 2024-12-30..2025-03-24
train 2024-01-01..2025-03-24  validate 2025-03-31..2025-06-23
train 2024-01-01..2025-06-23  validate 2025-06-30..2025-09-22
train 2024-01-01..2025-09-22  validate 2025-09-29..2025-12-22
    The training window grows every quarter, like a forecaster who refits each quarter. Do not dwell on the loop's mechanics; the output is the point.
  11. Three splitters, three questions (3m). "Same model, three kinds of folds." Before running: "Which one gives the biggest error?" Start it, then talk: gradient boosting on 13 fits takes some seconds on a laptop. 
    from sklearn.model_selection import GroupKFold

hgb = HistGradientBoostingRegressor(random_state=42)
splitters = {
    "shuffled KFold": dict(cv=KFold(5, shuffle=True, random_state=42)),
    "time-ordered": dict(cv=time_folds),
    "new products": dict(cv=GroupKFold(n_splits=4), groups=demand["product_id"]),
}
for name, kwargs in splitters.items():
    mae = -cross_val_score(hgb, X_d, y_d, scoring="neg_mean_absolute_error", **kwargs)
    print(f"{name:15s} MAE per fold {mae.round(1)}  mean {mae.mean():.2f}")
    shuffled KFold  MAE per fold [ 9.7  9.9  9.9 10.   9.6]  mean 9.84
time-ordered    MAE per fold [ 7.5 10.9 11.7 10.5]  mean 10.17
new products    MAE per fold [18.7 15.  10.7 19.9]  mean 16.07
    Shuffled 9.84, time-ordered 10.17 (little week-to-week memory in this data, so only slightly optimistic; the season shows in the per-fold errors), new products 16.07. "Ask: when this model is used for real, what will be new to it? Customers drawn from the same population: shuffled stratified folds. Next season: time-ordered. A new product, store, patient: group folds." Write that question on the board.
Three mistakes to expect, and how to coach each
  • A step that learns from data runs before cross-validation (silent). Pre-scaling X_train is the small version; the lesson's Common Mistake 1 is the large one: selecting 20 of 5,000 noise columns on all 200 rows, then cross-validating, gives leaky 0.825 against honest 0.595 honestly. Coach: "Which line learned something from the data, and did it see the validation fold?" Fix: put every learning step inside the pipeline you cross-validate.
  • Splitter and scorer errors (loud). KFold(n_splits=5, random_state=42): ValueError: Setting a random_state has no effect since shuffle is False. You should leave random_state to its default (None), or set shuffle=True. A misspelled scorer ("mean_absolute_error", "roc-auc") raises InvalidParameterError with a list of about 60 valid names. Coach: read the last line, then sklearn.metrics.get_scorer_names(); error metrics exist only as neg_... scorers, so flip the sign when you print.
  • Reporting the luckiest number, or cross-validating with the test rows inside (silent). best fold 0.873 honest estimate 0.863 +/- 0.008: the best fold is the single-split problem again. Cross-validating on all 6,000 rows and then "confirming" on the test set is no confirmation (the kit's debug_cross_validation.py makes the 0.829 estimate look like 0.850 this way). Coach: "How were the folds made, and where were the test rows while you chose?" Fix: report mean plus or minus spread of all folds, on X_train only.
Guided lab
  • Lab A (20m, everyone): labs/module-02/l1_3-cross-validation/fraud_folds_starter.py; reference labs/module-02/l1_3-cross-validation/fraud_folds_solution.py. The fraud model is retrained every month and used on the next. June is locked away; on January to May learners compare what unshuffled KFold and StratifiedKFold put in each fold, cross-validate a baseline, a pipeline and a tree with training scores, compare two models fold by fold, build next-month folds by hand, and open June once. New practice on every 1.3 move, on a rare class and a time question the lesson does not cover.
  • Run it first (1m), TODO 1 together (3m), TODO 2 and 3 in pairs (8m), TODO 4 and 5 (6m), debrief (2m). TODO 1 is the predict moment: the rows are in time order and the fraud rate drifts down (1.2 Exercise 3), so unshuffled blocks hold 83 frauds in January's block and 61 in May's.
  • Expected: frauds per fold [83, 84, 71, 75, 61] (KFold) vs [75, 75, 75, 74, 75] (stratified); validation AUC baseline 0.500, logistic regression 0.854 ± 0.023 (train 0.857), tree 0.776 ± 0.020; the tree ahead in 0 of 5 folds (mean -0.078); next-month AUC 0.864, 0.844, 0.860, 0.881 (mean 0.862); June AUC 0.874.
  • Debrief (2m): the time-ordered estimate (0.862) is closer to June (0.874) than the shuffled one (0.854), and both are within one spread of it: on this data the model's ranking holds up month to month. The tree loses every paired fold, a lead (here a deficit) that IS bigger than the noise. And the baseline's 0.500 is the yardstick for AUC, as 0.9912 was for accuracy.
  • Circulate for: cv=5 instead of cv=strat (works, but not shuffled: ask what is in fold 1); the scaler fitted outside the pipeline; time_folds with labels instead of positions (flatnonzero gives positions); June used before TODO 5.
  • Fast finishers: labs/module-02/l1_3-cross-validation/regularization_check_starter.py (reference labs/module-02/l1_3-cross-validation/regularization_check_solution.py): Session 1's Lab B said C=0.01 beat C=1.0 (0.853 vs 0.848 on one test split); 15 paired folds say otherwise. Then labs/module-02/l1_3-cross-validation/debug_cross_validation.py: two loud bugs, three silent ones.
Start the lesson project

The lesson project is a fair model bake-off: four churn models on identical repeated folds, a winner chosen on the training data alone, one look at the test set, and cv_leaderboard.csv. In the room, Steps 1 to 3 (15 minutes).

  • Read the brief aloud ("evidence that will survive a skeptical review"). Steps 1 and 2 together: the standard split (4500 1500) and the four candidates; point out the scaler inside the logistic regression pipeline and strategy="prior" (AUC exactly 0.5: the ranking yardstick).
  • Step 3 on their own: one RepeatedStratifiedKFold, two metrics, all four candidates. It fits 60 models (the forest dominates: 10 to 40 seconds on a laptop). Done = the leaderboard with random forest on top (accuracy 0.871, ROC AUC 0.867 ± 0.018) and the baseline at 0.835 / 0.500.
  • Goes home: Step 4 (the forest ahead of the tree in 15 of 15 paired folds, mean lead +0.033), Step 5 (the test set once: forest 0.865, tree 0.841) and Step 6 (cv_leaderboard.csv and the two-line recommendation). They keep this notebook: Lesson 1.4 starts from the same split and models.
Checks for understanding

Ask these out loud during the demo and the lab:

  • How many models does cross_val_score(tree, X_train, y_train, cv=5) fit, and which one do you keep? Answer: five; none. You refit the recipe once on all of X_train.
  • Why build StratifiedKFold(5, shuffle=True, random_state=42) instead of passing cv=5? Answer: cv=5 does not shuffle; on rows sorted in any meaningful order the folds are distorted (0% and 83% churners).
  • Why does the scaler go into make_pipeline? Answer: so it is refitted on the training folds of every round and never sees the validation fold.
  • The tree beats logistic regression in 12 of 15 folds by 0.004 AUC. Verdict? Answer: a tie: the lead is far smaller than the fold-to-fold spread (about 0.02). Prefer the simpler or cheaper model.
  • A model will forecast next quarter. What will be new to it, and which folds match? Answer: the future; time-ordered folds (train on the past, validate on the period right after).

Workbook self-check, items Q7 to Q12 (learners do them at the end of the block or at home).

Answers: Q7 8; Q8 B) ValueError: setting a random_state has no effect since shuffle is False; Q9 D) Each validation fold helped compute the median that filled it: put the filling step inside a pipeline so it is refitted on the training folds of every round; Q10 C) GroupKFold(n_splits=5) with groups=customer_id; Q11 [-3. -0.5 -3. ]; Q12 A) The estimate held: report 0.865 and keep the forest. Full set with explanations in the Answer Key.

Differentiation

Support: a card with the three splitter questions ("new customers? next period? new group?") and the three function names (cross_val_score for one score, cross_validate for several plus train scores, cross_val_predict for predictions). In the lab, a struggling pair may stop after TODO 3; TODO 4's index-building is mechanics, and the output is what matters.

Stretch: regularization_check_starter.py; then the lesson's Exercise 4 (nested cross-validation) and the Bonus Challenge: 50 paired folds for forest vs logistic regression and a histogram of the differences, then a different random_state for the splitter. Does the winner change?

Closing

Exit ticket (3 questions, no notes, 4 minutes)

Put the three questions on the slide. Learners answer in the workbook's Exit Ticket box or on a sticky note. Sort the notes into "got it" and "not yet" before Session 3: question 3 tells you whether the Session 3 warm-up needs a minute on pipelines inside folds before Lesson 1.4 uses cross_val_predict on a pipeline.

  1. Name the three parts of a three-way split and the one job of each. Which part is opened only once? Answer: Training fits every candidate; validation compares candidates and chooses; test grades the final choice, opened once at the very end (or cross-validation on the training rows replaces the fixed validation set).
  2. A sales forecast scores MAE $613 on a random split and $854 when trained on 2023 and 2024 and tested on 2025. Which number do you report, and why is the other one lower? Answer: $854, the time split: it is how the forecast will be used (trained on the past, asked about the future). The random split is lower because almost every test day has its neighbors, the day before and the day after, in training, and sales have momentum; tomorrow's neighbors are never available.
  3. Why do you cross-validate make_pipeline(StandardScaler(), LogisticRegression()) instead of scaling X_train first and cross-validating the model? Answer: So the scaler is refitted inside every round on the training folds only. Pre-scaling lets every validation fold help compute the mean and standard deviation it is scaled with: a leak, tiny for a scaler and large for steps that learn more (selecting features, encoding categories).
Homework (about 2 to 3 hours)
  • Finish both lesson projects. 1.2 Steps 4 to 6 (validation chooses the forest at 0.870; test 0.866 against 0.835; evaluation_report.txt). 1.3 Steps 4 to 6 (15 of 15 paired folds, mean lead +0.033; test AUC 0.865 for the forest, 0.841 for the tree; cv_leaderboard.csv).
  • Lesson exercises. 1.2: Exercises 1 to 3 (bigger test sets wobble less; a time split with shuffle=False; the June fraud rate); Exercise 4 (a leak detector) is a stretch. 1.3: Exercises 1 to 3 (3, 5 or 10 folds; the cross-validated overfitting curve; a new store with GroupKFold); Exercise 4 (nested cross-validation) is a stretch.
  • Both Quick Quizzes on the lesson pages (five questions each; answers in the Answer Key).
  • Journal: both entries in journal.md. 1.2: the dress rehearsal for the problem you framed in Lesson 0.1. 1.3: "one exam or five quizzes" for a colleague, and the splitter your own project needs.
  • Before the Next Lesson (1.2): look through data/README.md for any column, in any file, that might be recorded after the event it would help predict, and note it in the journal; rerun the 1.2 project with random_state=7 in both splits (does the same model win on validation, and how far does the test score move?).
  • Before the Next Lesson (1.3): keep the bake-off notebook; rerun Step 3 with scoring=["accuracy", "roc_auc", "f1"] and write down how the baseline's F1 compares with its accuracy, and guess why; think of one dataset from your own work where rows are related by time, person or place, and which splitter you would use.
  • Optional practice: labs/module-02/l1_2-splits-and-leakage/fraud_validation_starter.py and labs/module-02/l1_3-cross-validation/regularization_check_starter.py if you did not reach them; the two debug_*.py handouts.

Instructor Kit · Facilitator Guide

Session 3: Metrics and Preprocessing

How to teach Session 3 of Machine Learning Foundations. The first half turns "84.8% accurate" into the numbers a business can act on: the confusion matrix, precision and recall, a threshold chosen from costs on training data only, ranking metrics, and honest regression errors. The second half brings the rest of the churn table into the model: gaps filled (and remembered), units scaled, categories encoded, all in one ColumnTransformer learned from the training rows. Learners leave knowing that the same model can earn $2,280 or $3,555 depending only on its threshold, and that preparing all 16 columns lifts this linear model's ROC AUC only from 0.823 to 0.828: an honest result they could finally measure.

2 lessons 3 hrs session Lessons 1.4–2.1 Session 3 of 11

How to Use This Guide

  • Same rhythm for both lessons: frame (the goal and one mental model) → live demo (you type, they type along) → guided lab (a kit lab, new practice on the lesson's skills) → start the lesson's own project. The lesson exercises, quiz and journal are homework.
  • Type, do not paste. Every code block in a demo script below was run to produce the output printed under it (Python 3.12, pandas 3.0.6, scikit-learn 1.9.1 on 4 CPU threads). The demos follow the lesson pages' own code, so the numbers match the pages exactly; ask for a prediction before every run that has a number in it.
  • The lessons are the reference. Keep evaluation_metrics.html and preprocessing_feature_engineering.html open in browser tabs for the pictures (the confusion-matrix layout, the side-by-side matrices at 0.5 and 0.2, the ROC and precision-recall curves, the profit curve, the three histograms, the ColumnTransformer diagram). Labs live in labs/module-03/: _starter.py files are handouts, _solution.py files are yours.
  • Lesson 1.4 is the densest page of Module 1 (105 minutes for a solo learner). The room does not try to cover all of it: the multiclass section is an optional demo step, and Exercises 1 to 4 go home.
  • Teal blocks are hands-on, amber blocks are watch-outs. Lesson codes (1.4, 2.1) match the course site.
The #1 risk of Session 3: the threshold gets chosen on the test set

Lesson 1.4 gives learners a powerful new dial, and the natural thing to do with a dial is to turn it until the test score looks best. Every time someone in the room picks a threshold from a test-set table, say it out loud: "That is tuning on the test set." The demo shows the honest routes (out-of-fold probabilities, TunedThresholdClassifierCV), the lab practices one, and the project is built on it. The same risk returns after the break in a quieter form: fit_transform on the test rows. It raises no error. The checks in Step 4 of the 2.1 project (training columns centered exactly, test columns only close to center) and the debug_preprocess.py handout are there to make it visible. Second risk: the pace. Two dense lessons; protect the order below and cut from the bottom of the list.

Before the Session

  • ☐ Run the kit check once: python3 labs/check_labs.py module-03 from this kit's folder. Every line should say PASS (8 files, about 30 seconds). The two debug_*.py handouts are deliberately broken and are not run.
  • ☐ Files to hand out: labs/module-03/ without the *_solution.py files (the starters plus debug_metrics.py and debug_preprocess.py). Learners put it inside labs/ in their pinecrest-ml folder, next to module-01 and module-02. Nothing new to install and nothing to download: everything today is scikit-learn 1.9 (FixedThresholdClassifier, TunedThresholdClassifierCV and root_mean_squared_error need 1.5 or newer; the environment check from Session 1 already confirmed 1.9).
  • ☐ Projector machine: your own pinecrest-ml, (.venv) active, JupyterLab started from that folder, two empty notebooks ready: lesson_1_4 and lesson_2_1. Run the 1.4 demo once before class: the forest and TunedThresholdClassifierCV take a few seconds each, and you want to know how long.
  • ☐ Board, before learners arrive: the empty 2×2 confusion matrix (rows: really stayed / really churned; columns: predicted stays / predicted churns); the line "Profit = 60 × TP − 15 × (TP + FP)"; and, for after the break, three bowls: "gaps → imputer, units → scaler, words → encoder".
  • ☐ Browser tabs: evaluation_metrics.html (scrolled to the side-by-side confusion matrices), preprocessing_feature_engineering.html (the ColumnTransformer diagram), and the 1.3 project page in case anyone needs cross_val_predict again.
  • ☐ Print or share: the workbook pages for Session 3, and sticky notes for help flags and the exit ticket. Collect the Session 2 exit tickets if you have not reviewed them: the warm-up builds on them.
Known environment and code traps
SymptomCauseFix
ImportError: cannot import name 'FixedThresholdClassifier' or 'root_mean_squared_error'scikit-learn older than 1.5 (usually a notebook kernel that is not .venv)Check sklearn.__version__; select the .venv kernel. The course pins 1.9.
AttributeError: 'float' object has no attribute 'round'scikit-learn 1.9 metric functions return plain Python floatsround(x, 3) or an f-string, not .round(3).
ValueError: y should be a 1d array, got an array of shape (1500, 2)Both probability columns passed to a metricpredict_proba(X)[:, 1].
A precision that equals the recall you expected (no error)Arguments swapped: every metric is (y_true, y_pred)Truth first; or keyword arguments y_true=..., y_pred=....
An AUC much lower than expected (0.615 instead of 0.823; no error)AUC computed from predict labelsPass scores: predict_proba(X)[:, 1].
UndefinedMetricWarning: Precision is ill-definedThe model never predicts the positive classNothing is broken; pass zero_division=0 when you expect it.
TunedThresholdClassifierCV cell seems to hangIt fits the model 5 times; with a forest, several seconds on a laptopWait. Run it once before class to know the time.
