Warm-up

0:00

Warm-up: Metrics and Preprocessing

10 min
Timing
6mFive retrieval questions (workbook Warm-up box, no notes), then reveal and discuss
4mHomework check: both Session 3 projects, exercises, quizzes, journals
Questions (on the slide and in the workbook)
W1
Pinecrest's care team calls 150 customers and 103 of them really were about to churn. Which metric is 103 / 150, and what does it answer? (Lesson 1.4)
W2
What does this print? (Lesson 1.4)
from sklearn.metrics import precision_score, recall_score
y_true = [1, 1, 1, 0, 0, 0, 0, 0]
y_pred = [1, 1, 0, 1, 0, 0, 0, 0]
print(round(precision_score(y_true, y_pred), 3), round(recall_score(y_true, y_pred), 3))
W3
Lesson 1.4's project chose a threshold of 0.26. Why from out-of-fold probabilities on the training rows, and never from the test set? (Lesson 1.4)
W4
What does this print? (Lesson 2.1)
from sklearn.preprocessing import OneHotEncoder
enc = OneHotEncoder(sparse_output=False, handle_unknown="ignore").fit([["Bend"], ["Boise"], ["Online"]])
print(enc.transform([["Reno"], ["Boise"]]))
W5
Lesson 2.1 asked: to cross-validate its churn model, which objects must be refitted inside every fold, and in which order? (Lesson 2.1)
Answers
  1. W1 Precision (at 150): of the customers we contacted, how many were real churners? Recall would divide by all real churners instead.
  2. W2 0.667 0.667: two of the three flagged are churners (precision 2/3) and two of the three churners were flagged (recall 2/3).
  3. W3 The threshold is a setting learned from data. Choosing it on the test set lets the test set help build the model, so its score is no longer an honest preview. Out-of-fold probabilities give every training customer a score from a model that did not see them.
  4. W4 Reno is unknown, so with handle_unknown="ignore" it becomes a row of zeros; Boise is the second learned category. The category list was learned in fit.
  5. W5 The whole ColumnTransformer (imputer, scalers, ordinal and one-hot encoders), then the model, on the training folds only; then transform-and-predict on the validation fold. Doing that by hand is error-prone: a Pipeline does exactly this, which is today's first lesson.
Homework check and how to read it
  • Show of hands, one per item: Lesson 1.4 project finished (threshold 0.26, $5,415 test profit, retention_calls.csv with 150 rows); Lesson 2.1 project finished (preprocess turns 16 columns into 40; new_customer_scores.csv with N1 0.886, N2 0.009, N3 0.467); exercises 1 to 3 of each; both quizzes; both journals; the one churn-dashboard metric they chose (take two answers: most will say recall or precision at a fixed number of calls, a fair choice).
  • W5 is the bridge. Whoever answered it well has already understood half of Lesson 2.2. Write their answer on the board under the heading "What a pipeline automates".
  • Most likely to be missed: W1 and W3. Precision and recall swap places for many people; give the smoke-alarm sentence from 1.4 again ("of the alarms, how many were fires" vs "of the fires, how many rang"). Do not reteach thresholds: Lesson 2.5 (Session 5) returns to them.
  • Anyone without the 2.1 project: they do not need it today. Lesson 2.2 rebuilds the same preprocessor in its first cells; Lesson 2.3 starts from the CSV.

Lesson Plans

2.2

Pipelines

75 min
Objectives (from the lesson page)
  • Build a Pipeline with make_pipeline or with named steps, and explain what fit and predict do to each step
  • Reach inside a fitted pipeline (named_steps, indexing, slicing) to read learned values
  • Cross-validate a whole pipeline, and show with a target-based encoding why preprocessing outside the folds leaks
  • Change any setting with the step__parameter naming, swap a whole step, and run a first small grid search
  • Add a custom step with FunctionTransformer, and save and reload a fitted pipeline with joblib
Timing in the room
10mFrame: the coffee machine; fit and predict through every step; the one rule
30mLive demo: the steps below (protect 7 and 8)
20mGuided lab: fraud_pipeline_starter.py (everyone), then leak_size_starter.py (pairs)
15mStart the lesson project: the deployable churn pipeline, Steps 1 to 3
Mental model to say

Say: "You could make espresso by hand: grind, weigh, tamp, heat, time the shot. Or you use a machine that does every step in order, the same way every time. Beans in, coffee out, and nobody can skip the grinder. A pipeline is that machine: transformers in order, one model at the end. It has fit and predict like any single model, so everything that works with a model (cross-validation, grid search, saving to a file) works with the whole machine. And it knows the one rule by heart: learn from training data, apply to everything else."

Then the stakes, pointing at the warm-up's W5 on the board: "In Session 3 you followed the rule by hand: fit_transform, then fit, then transform, then predict. Every rule you have to remember is a rule you will one day forget, at 6 p.m., in a cell you ran twice. From today, every model that needs preprocessing is a pipeline."

Live demo (you type, they follow)

New notebook lesson_2_2. Ask for a prediction before Steps 2, 7, 12 and 14. Step 7 is the heart of the lesson: let the 0.930 sit for a moment before you explain it.

  1. Same data, same columns as Lesson 2.1 (1m). "Same split, same 16 columns as the 2.1 project. Nothing new until the next cell." 
    import numpy as np
import pandas as pd
from sklearn.model_selection import train_test_split
pd.set_option("display.width", 150)
customers = pd.read_csv("data/customers_churn.csv")
complete = ["tenure_months", "orders_12m", "spend_12m", "avg_order_value", "days_since_last_order",
            "returns_12m", "return_rate", "support_tickets_12m", "discount_share", "app_user"]
nominal = ["home_store", "region", "age_group", "preferred_category"]
features = complete + ["email_opens_90d", "loyalty_tier"] + nominal
train, test = train_test_split(customers, test_size=0.25, stratify=customers["churned"], random_state=42)
X_train, y_train = train[features], train["churned"]
X_test, y_test = test[features], test["churned"]
print(X_train.shape, X_test.shape)
    (4500, 16) (1500, 16)
  2. The smallest pipeline (3m). "Two steps, one object. Before I run it: what accuracy will it print?" (Someone should say 0.848, Lesson 1.1's number.) 
    from sklearn.linear_model import LogisticRegression
from sklearn.pipeline import make_pipeline
from sklearn.preprocessing import StandardScaler
simple = make_pipeline(StandardScaler(), LogisticRegression())
simple.fit(X_train[complete], y_train)
print(simple)
print(f"test accuracy: {simple.score(X_test[complete], y_test):.3f}")
    Pipeline(steps=[('standardscaler', StandardScaler()),
                ('logisticregression', LogisticRegression())])
test accuracy: 0.848
    Say: "One fit replaced three lines, one score replaced two, and the order can no longer be wrong." Draw the fit/predict diagram from the lesson on the board: fit = fit_transform on every step but the last, then fit; predict = transform only, then predict.
  3. Looking inside (2m). "A pipeline is a box you can open: by name, by position, or sliced." 
    print(list(simple.named_steps))
print("scaler means:", simple.named_steps["standardscaler"].mean_[:3].round(2))
print("model weights:", simple[-1].coef_.shape)
print("preprocessing only:", simple[:-1].transform(X_test[complete]).shape)
    ['standardscaler', 'logisticregression']
scaler means: [ 41.48   5.   594.13]
model weights: (1, 10)
preprocessing only: (1500, 10)
    simple[-1] is the fitted model; simple[:-1] is a pipeline of everything before it: the data exactly as the model sees it.
  4. Name your own steps (1m). "For anything bigger, choose short names. model survives swapping the model for another class." 
    from sklearn.pipeline import Pipeline
named = Pipeline([
    ("scale", StandardScaler()),
    ("model", LogisticRegression()),
])
named.fit(X_train[complete], y_train)
print(list(named.named_steps), round(named.score(X_test[complete], y_test), 3))
    ['scale', 'model'] 0.848
  5. The full churn pipeline (4m). "Lesson 2.1's preprocessor is itself a transformer, so it can be a step. Sixteen raw columns, text and gaps included, in; a probability out." 
    from sklearn.compose import ColumnTransformer
from sklearn.impute import SimpleImputer
from sklearn.metrics import roc_auc_score
from sklearn.preprocessing import OneHotEncoder, OrdinalEncoder
preprocess = ColumnTransformer([
    ("scale", StandardScaler(), complete),
    ("email", make_pipeline(SimpleImputer(strategy="median", add_indicator=True), StandardScaler()),
     ["email_opens_90d"]),
    ("tier", make_pipeline(OrdinalEncoder(categories=[["Basic", "Silver", "Gold"]]), StandardScaler()),
     ["loyalty_tier"]),
    ("onehot", OneHotEncoder(sparse_output=False, handle_unknown="ignore"), nominal),
])
churn_pipe = Pipeline([
    ("prep", preprocess),
    ("model", LogisticRegression()),
])
churn_pipe.fit(X_train, y_train)
proba = churn_pipe.predict_proba(X_test)[:, 1]
print(f"test ROC AUC: {roc_auc_score(y_test, proba):.3f}")
    test ROC AUC: 0.828
    Same 0.828 as Lesson 2.1. Then the payoff at prediction time:
  6. A raw record in, a probability out (1m). "Double brackets: a one-row table, not a Series." 
    one_customer = X_test.iloc[[0]]
print(one_customer[["days_since_last_order", "email_opens_90d", "home_store", "age_group"]])
print("churn probability:", churn_pipe.predict_proba(one_customer)[:, 1].round(3))
          days_since_last_order  email_opens_90d home_store age_group
2111                     53              5.0  Asheville     25-34
churn probability: [0.061]
  7. A leak you could write on real data (4m). "A colleague proposes a clever feature: for each signup date, the churn rate of customers who joined that day. Prediction first: is the signup date a strong signal?" Then run it. 
    from sklearn.model_selection import StratifiedKFold, cross_val_score
cv = StratifiedKFold(n_splits=5, shuffle=True, random_state=42)
date_rate = y_train.groupby(train["signup_date"]).mean()
X_date = pd.DataFrame({"signup_churn_rate": train["signup_date"].map(date_rate)})
leaky = cross_val_score(LogisticRegression(), X_date, y_train, cv=cv, scoring="roc_auc")
print(f"leaky CV ROC AUC: {leaky.mean():.3f}")
    leaky CV ROC AUC: 0.930
    Let the room react to 0.930, higher than the full 16-column model. Ask: "How many customers joined on a typical date?" (Most of the 2,150 dates have one or two.) "So what is 'the churn rate of your signup date' for most customers?" (Their own label.)
  8. The honest version (3m). "TargetEncoder computes the same kind of rate, but as a transformer: inside a pipeline it is refitted on the training folds of every round." 
    from sklearn.preprocessing import TargetEncoder
honest_pipe = make_pipeline(TargetEncoder(cv=cv), LogisticRegression())
honest = cross_val_score(honest_pipe, train[["signup_date"]], y_train, cv=cv, scoring="roc_auc")
print(f"honest CV ROC AUC: {honest.mean():.3f}")
honest_pipe.fit(train[["signup_date"]], y_train)
test_auc = roc_auc_score(y_test, honest_pipe.predict_proba(test[["signup_date"]])[:, 1])
print(f"test ROC AUC     : {test_auc:.3f}")
    honest CV ROC AUC: 0.586
test ROC AUC     : 0.571
    Write on the board: 0.930 leaky / 0.586 honest CV / 0.571 test. The honest CV score and the test agree; the leaky number was a broken measurement, not a better model. Then: "Steps that learn from y leak the most. You do not need to judge each case: put every learning step in the pipeline."
  9. Cross-validate the whole pipeline (2m). "One call; every fold refits the imputer, both scalers, both encoders and the model." 
    from sklearn.dummy import DummyClassifier
baseline_pipe = Pipeline([("prep", preprocess), ("model", DummyClassifier(strategy="prior"))])
for name, pipe in [("baseline", baseline_pipe), ("logistic regression", churn_pipe)]:
    scores = cross_val_score(pipe, X_train, y_train, cv=cv, scoring="roc_auc")
    print(f"{name:20s} CV ROC AUC {scores.mean():.3f} +/- {scores.std():.3f}")
    baseline             CV ROC AUC 0.500 +/- 0.000
logistic regression  CV ROC AUC 0.833 +/- 0.021
    cross_val_score clones the pipeline per fold, so the fitted churn_pipe is untouched.
  10. Every setting has an address (3m). "Step name, two underscores, setting; deeper levels the same way. Read it like a file path." 
    params = churn_pipe.get_params()
for key in ["model__C", "model__max_iter", "prep__email__simpleimputer__strategy", "prep__onehot__handle_unknown"]:
    print(f"{key:40s} {params[key]!r}")
print(len(params), "settings in total")
    model__C                                 1.0
model__max_iter                          100
prep__email__simpleimputer__strategy     'median'
prep__onehot__handle_unknown             'ignore'
73 settings in total
    Then the loop:
  11. Turn a knob from the top (2m). "set_params uses the same names. The loop ends at the default, so the pipeline is back where it started." 
    for c_value in [0.01, 1.0]:
    churn_pipe.set_params(model__C=c_value)
    scores = cross_val_score(churn_pipe, X_train, y_train, cv=cv, scoring="roc_auc")
    print(f"C={c_value:<5} CV ROC AUC {scores.mean():.3f}")
    C=0.01  CV ROC AUC 0.835
C=1.0   CV ROC AUC 0.833
  12. Swap a whole step (2m). "A step name can take a whole new object. clone gives an unfitted copy, so churn_pipe keeps its logistic regression." (Boosting takes 10 to 30 seconds here: start it, then talk.) 
    from sklearn.base import clone
from sklearn.ensemble import HistGradientBoostingClassifier
boost_pipe = clone(churn_pipe).set_params(model=HistGradientBoostingClassifier(random_state=42))
scores = cross_val_score(boost_pipe, X_train, y_train, cv=cv, scoring="roc_auc")
print(f"gradient boosting CV ROC AUC {scores.mean():.3f} +/- {scores.std():.3f}")
    gradient boosting CV ROC AUC 0.861 +/- 0.013
    About 0.03 better than logistic regression on identical preprocessing: the true churn patterns are not a simple weighted sum. Module 5 investigates; Lesson 2.3, after the break, attacks it from the other side.
  13. A first grid search (2m). "Every combination, cross-validated, best one refitted. It receives the whole pipeline, so every candidate is leak-free." 
    from sklearn.model_selection import GridSearchCV
grid = GridSearchCV(
    churn_pipe,
    param_grid={
        "model__C": [0.01, 0.1, 1.0],
        "prep__email__simpleimputer__strategy": ["median", "mean"],
    },
    cv=cv, scoring="roc_auc",
)
grid.fit(X_train, y_train)
print(grid.best_params_)
print(f"best CV ROC AUC {grid.best_score_:.3f}")
    {'model__C': 0.01, 'prep__email__simpleimputer__strategy': 'median'}
best CV ROC AUC 0.835
    Six combinations × five folds = 30 fits + 1 refit. The point: a hundredfold change in C and two fill strategies move the score in the third decimal. Knowing what does not matter is a result. Lesson 6.2 does tuning properly.
  14. A custom step, measured honestly (2m). "FunctionTransformer turns a plain function into a step. Lesson 2.1's histograms looked nicer after a log. Does the model agree?" 
    from sklearn.preprocessing import FunctionTransformer
skewed = ["spend_12m", "avg_order_value", "days_since_last_order"]
others = [c for c in complete if c not in skewed]
log_prep = ColumnTransformer([
    ("log", make_pipeline(FunctionTransformer(np.log1p, feature_names_out="one-to-one"), StandardScaler()), skewed),
    ("scale", StandardScaler(), others),
    ("email", make_pipeline(SimpleImputer(strategy="median", add_indicator=True), StandardScaler()),
     ["email_opens_90d"]),
    ("tier", make_pipeline(OrdinalEncoder(categories=[["Basic", "Silver", "Gold"]]), StandardScaler()),
     ["loyalty_tier"]),
    ("onehot", OneHotEncoder(sparse_output=False, handle_unknown="ignore"), nominal),
])
log_pipe = Pipeline([("prep", log_prep), ("model", LogisticRegression())])
scores = cross_val_score(log_pipe, X_train, y_train, cv=cv, scoring="roc_auc")
print(f"with log1p on 3 skewed columns: CV ROC AUC {scores.mean():.3f}")
print(log_pipe.fit(X_train, y_train)[:-1].get_feature_names_out()[:4])
    with log1p on 3 skewed columns: CV ROC AUC 0.812
['log__spend_12m' 'log__avg_order_value' 'log__days_since_last_order'
 'scale__tenure_months']
    Worse: 0.812 against 0.833. Churn jumps near 150 days of silence and the log squeezes exactly that region. The method is the lesson: one cross-validation call settled it. Lesson 2.3 builds the threshold directly.
  15. Save it as one file (1m). "Everything needed to score a customer (fill values, means, category lists, weights) in one file." 
    import joblib
joblib.dump(churn_pipe, "churn_pipeline.joblib")
loaded = joblib.load("churn_pipeline.joblib")
same = np.allclose(loaded.predict_proba(X_test)[:, 1], churn_pipe.predict_proba(X_test)[:, 1])
print("identical predictions after reloading:", same)
    identical predictions after reloading: True
    Say the warning out loud: a joblib file can run code when loaded. Only load files you or your team made. Lesson 12.1 shows skops and version pinning.
Three mistakes to expect, and how to coach each
  • A setting without its step name. ValueError: Invalid parameter 'C' for estimator Pipeline(steps=[('standardscaler', StandardS... Coach: "The pipeline has only four settings of its own; where does C live?" Then list(pipe.get_params()). The variant in the self-check (Q3): model__C on a make_pipeline pipeline, whose step is called logisticregression.
  • The model in the middle. TypeError: All intermediate steps should be transformers and implement fit and transform or be the string 'pas... (it also happens when a learner appends a scaler after the model to "scale the predictions"). Coach: "Which steps need a transform?" Transformers first, exactly one model last. Also expect ValueError: Estimator names must not contain __: got ['my__scaler'] when someone names a step my__scaler.
  • Believing the pipeline guards the columns too. A make_column_selector pointed at the whole table takes every text column, cancellation_reason included: CV ROC AUC 0.937, no error (the lesson's Common Mistake 5). Coach: "Which leak does a pipeline prevent: between rows, or from the future?" Rows only. The feature list is still the learner's job. A missing column at prediction time is the opposite case: ValueError: columns are missing: {'email_opens_90d'}, fixed by sending the column (empty if unknown).
Guided lab
  • Lab A (12m, everyone): labs/module-04/l2_2-pipelines/fraud_pipeline_starter.py; reference labs/module-04/l2_2-pipelines/fraud_pipeline_solution.py. The lesson's moves on a new problem: card fraud, nine numeric columns plus the text category. Build prep + model, compare with a baseline pipeline by cross-validation (ROC AUC and average precision), reach inside, turn model__C, test once, save to OUTPUT, reload, score three raw payments, one in a category Pinecrest does not sell.
  • Expected, Lab A: baseline CV ROC AUC 0.500 / average precision 0.009; logistic regression 0.867 / 0.535; 10 categories learned, the largest weight onehot__category_Gift Cards 0.95; model__C=0.01 gives 0.518, 1.0 gives 0.535; test ROC AUC 0.843, average precision 0.463, 56 frauds among the 100 riskiest; reloaded steps ['prep', 'model'], 10 columns; probabilities [0.999, 0.0, 0.002].
  • Circulate in Lab A for: cross_validate results read as res["roc_auc"] (the key is test_roc_auc); named_transformers without the trailing underscore (the unfitted originals, no categories_); forgetting to put C back to 1.0. Debrief: "Why did the Bikes payment get a score?" handle_unknown="ignore" made it a row of zeros in the one-hot block. And: "Why is the test AUC (0.843) below the CV estimate (0.867)?" With 131 test frauds, a few hard ones move the number; the folds' spread is wide too. Lesson 2.5 returns to this data.
  • Lab B (8m, pairs): labs/module-04/l2_2-pipelines/leak_size_starter.py; reference labs/module-04/l2_2-pipelines/leak_size_solution.py. Three steps, each done before cross-validation and inside a pipeline: a fraud rate per dollar amount (learns from y, few rows per value), a scaler (learns from X), np.log1p (learns nothing). The partner who is not typing predicts each gap out loud first.
  • Expected, Lab B: 1,016 distinct amounts, 434 of them 5 times or fewer; target rate 0.913 before CV, 0.583 inside the pipeline, 0.568 on the test set; scaler 0.8613 both ways (difference -0.00001); log1p 0.8617 both ways (+0.00000). Fill in the board's "Leak size" box from it: huge / tiny / none.
  • Fast finishers: labs/module-04/l2_2-pipelines/debug_pipeline.py: a demand pipeline with three loud bugs (alpha without its step name, a step called one__hot, a missing column at prediction time) and one silent one (the "test" rows are the training year). Fixed, it prints 2025 test MAE: 11.75 units (11.02 while the silent bug remains).
Start the lesson project

The lesson project is a deployable churn pipeline: chosen by cross-validation, tested once, saved, reloaded and used on raw records. In the room, Steps 1 to 3 (15 minutes).

  • Read the brief aloud ("one file that takes raw rows and returns probabilities"). Step 1 is the demo's setup plus cv: done = (4500, 16) (1500, 16).
  • Step 2: the preprocessor once, a churn_pipeline(model) function, three candidates. Step 3: the same folds for all three. Done in the room = baseline 0.500, logistic regression 0.833 ± 0.021, gradient boosting 0.861 ± 0.013, chosen: gradient boosting (the boosting CV takes up to a minute on a laptop).
  • Goes home: Step 4 (test ROC AUC 0.863, 42 real churners in the top 50 against 8.3 at random), Step 5 (churn_pipeline.joblib, 16 columns expected), Step 6 (two raw records: N1 0.866, N2 0.002; monthly_churn_scores.csv). Tell them to KEEP the .joblib file: later lessons compare new models against it.
Checks for understanding

Ask these out loud during the demo and the lab:

  • What does pipe.fit call on the scaler, and what does pipe.predict call? Answer: fit_transform during fit (then passes the result on); transform only during predict.
  • Why did the signup-date rate score 0.930 in CV and about 0.57 on test? Answer: it was computed with every training label, including each validation row's own; most dates have one or two customers, so the feature was mostly the answer.
  • Which name sets C in Pipeline([("prep", ...), ("model", LogisticRegression())])? Answer: model__C.
  • Can a pipeline stop cancellation_reason from leaking? Answer: no: it separates rows, not columns. Keeping future columns out of the feature list is your job.
  • Why is FunctionTransformer(np.log1p) safe even outside the pipeline? Answer: it is stateless: it learns nothing from the data.

Workbook self-check, items Q1 to Q6 (learners do them at the end of the block or at home).

Answers: Q1 B) transform only, with the means and standard deviations it learned during fit; Q2 D) prep__num__simpleimputer__strategy; Q3 A) ValueError: Invalid parameter 'model': make_pipeline named the step logisticregression; Q4 C) FunctionTransformer(np.log1p); Q5 [[4.605]]; Q6 D) ValueError: columns are missing: {'email_opens_90d'}: send the column, empty (NaN) where unknown. Full set with explanations in the Answer Key.

Differentiation

Support: give anyone lost in the names a printed list(churn_pipe.get_params()) and have them find the address of three settings before the lab. In Lab A, let a struggling pair copy the ColumnTransformer from the demo notebook: the goal is the comparison by cross-validation, not retyping branches.

Stretch: the lesson's Exercise 4 (one grid that swaps model families: best boosting with learning rate 0.05, CV 0.866) and the Bonus Challenge (a Winsorizer class with fit learning percentiles). Ask them why the percentiles must be learned in fit, not in transform.

2.3

Creating Features

75 min
Objectives (from the lesson page)
  • Explain why a linear model cannot use a threshold, a U-shape or an interaction unless you build it as a feature
  • Create flags, ratios and interaction terms with pandas, choosing them from the training data only
  • Extract calendar parts and cyclical (sine and cosine) features from a date column
  • Build lag and rolling-window features per group with shift, so no row sees its own future
  • Put feature creation inside a Pipeline with FunctionTransformer and measure the lift with cross-validation
Timing in the room
10mFrame: the interviewer and the calculator; the feature loop
30mLive demo: the steps below (protect 2 to 6)
20mGuided lab: night_features_starter.py (everyone), then daily_sales_features_starter.py (pairs)
15mStart the lesson project: the weekly demand forecaster, Steps 1 to 3
Mental model to say

Say: "Think of a model as a very fast calculator with a fixed shape. Logistic regression can only add up weighted columns: more of a column always pushes the same way, by the same amount. It cannot say 'nothing much happens until 150 days, then risk jumps', and it cannot say 'tickets matter, but only without the app'. You are the interviewer who decides which questions the calculator gets. Ask 'days since the last order?' and it answers with a straight line. Also ask 'more than 150 days?' and it can express a jump."

Then the loop, on the board: explore the TRAINING rows → write a feature function → put it in the pipeline → cross-validate against the plain model → keep it or drop it. "Most ideas will not help. An idea that does not help is a cheap experiment, not a failure: you will record two of them today."

Live demo (you type, they follow)

New notebook lesson_2_3. Steps 2 to 4 are three groupbys: before each, ask the room to sketch the shape they expect (a line, a step, a U). Steps 9 to 14 switch to the weekly demand table.

  1. The standard split, ten numeric columns (1m). "Lesson 1.1's ten columns. Everything we explore today uses the training rows only." 
    import numpy as np
import pandas as pd
from sklearn.model_selection import train_test_split
pd.set_option("display.width", 150)
customers = pd.read_csv("data/customers_churn.csv")
base_features = ["tenure_months", "orders_12m", "spend_12m", "avg_order_value", "days_since_last_order",
                 "returns_12m", "return_rate", "support_tickets_12m", "discount_share", "app_user"]
train, test = train_test_split(customers, test_size=0.25, stratify=customers["churned"], random_state=42)
X_train, y_train = train[base_features], train["churned"]
X_test, y_test = test[base_features], test["churned"]
print(X_train.shape, X_test.shape)
    (4500, 10) (1500, 10)
  2. Is risk a straight line in recency? (3m). "A logistic regression assumes every extra day of silence adds the same risk. Prediction: does it?" 
    recency = pd.cut(train["days_since_last_order"], bins=[-1, 30, 90, 150, 250, 364])
print(train.groupby(recency, observed=True)["churned"].agg(["mean", "size"]).round(3))
                            mean  size
days_since_last_order             
(-1, 30]               0.088  1516
(30, 90]               0.096  1591
(90, 150]              0.158   646
(150, 250]             0.415   431
(250, 364]             0.560   316
    9 to 10% for three months, 16% by 150 days, then over 40%. Something happens around five months. One weight has to compromise between the flat part and the jump.
  3. A U-shape (2m). "Tenure." 
    tenure = pd.cut(train["tenure_months"], bins=[0, 5, 12, 36, 72, 96])
print(train.groupby(tenure, observed=True)["churned"].agg(["mean", "size"]).round(3))
                    mean  size
tenure_months             
(0, 5]         0.320   930
(5, 12]        0.109   238
(12, 36]       0.076   973
(36, 72]       0.099  1397
(72, 96]       0.215   962
    New customers churn most, the middle is safe, veterans drift away. "The model in Lesson 1.1 gave tenure a tiny weight. It was not saying tenure does not matter; it was saying 'I cannot draw this shape'."
  4. An interaction (3m). "Tickets, split by the app." 
    tickets = train["support_tickets_12m"].clip(upper=4)
print(train.pivot_table(index=tickets, columns="app_user", values="churned", aggfunc="mean").round(3))
    app_user                 0      1
support_tickets_12m              
0                    0.141  0.110
1                    0.214  0.116
2                    0.266  0.112
3                    0.331  0.120
4                    0.464  0.038
    Without the app every ticket raises churn, to 46%; with the app, flat. One weight for tickets is the average of a strong effect and no effect.
  5. Five questions as five columns (4m). "Each pattern becomes one column. Write them as ONE function: easy to test, reuse and put in a pipeline. df.copy() keeps it pure." 
    def add_churn_features(df):
    df = df.copy()
    df["lapsed_150"] = (df["days_since_last_order"] > 150).astype(int)
    df["new_customer"] = (df["tenure_months"] < 6).astype(int)
    df["veteran"] = (df["tenure_months"] > 72).astype(int)
    df["heavy_discount"] = (df["discount_share"] > 0.35).astype(int)
    df["tickets_no_app"] = df["support_tickets_12m"] * (1 - df["app_user"])
    return df
engineered = add_churn_features(X_train)
new_columns = ["lapsed_150", "new_customer", "veteran", "heavy_discount", "tickets_no_app"]
print(engineered[["tenure_months", "new_customer", "veteran"]].head(3))
print(engineered[["support_tickets_12m", "app_user", "tickets_no_app"]].head(3))
print(engineered[new_columns].mean().round(3))
          tenure_months  new_customer  veteran
1556              1             1        0
965              44             0        0
5145              4             1        0
      support_tickets_12m  app_user  tickets_no_app
1556                    3         0               3
965                     2         1               0
5145                    0         0               0
lapsed_150        0.166
new_customer      0.207
veteran           0.214
heavy_discount    0.210
tickets_no_app    0.532
dtype: float64
    Check two rows by hand against the raw columns. The means say how common each flag is: none is almost always 0 or 1.
  6. Measure the lift, inside a pipeline (4m). "FunctionTransformer wraps the function; it learns nothing, so fit does nothing and transform calls it. Prediction first: logistic regression with five more columns against boosting on the raw ones." (Boosting takes a while: start it, talk.) 
    from sklearn.ensemble import HistGradientBoostingClassifier
from sklearn.linear_model import LogisticRegression
from sklearn.model_selection import StratifiedKFold, cross_val_score
from sklearn.pipeline import make_pipeline
from sklearn.preprocessing import FunctionTransformer, StandardScaler
cv = StratifiedKFold(n_splits=5, shuffle=True, random_state=42)
candidates = {
    "logistic, raw features": make_pipeline(StandardScaler(), LogisticRegression()),
    "logistic, + engineered": make_pipeline(FunctionTransformer(add_churn_features), StandardScaler(),
                                            LogisticRegression()),
    "boosting, raw features": HistGradientBoostingClassifier(random_state=42),
}
cv_auc = {}
for name, pipe in candidates.items():
    scores = cross_val_score(pipe, X_train, y_train, cv=cv, scoring="roc_auc")
    cv_auc[name] = scores.mean()
    print(f"{name:24s} CV ROC AUC {scores.mean():.3f} +/- {scores.std():.3f}")
    logistic, raw features   CV ROC AUC 0.829 +/- 0.025
logistic, + engineered   CV ROC AUC 0.883 +/- 0.018
boosting, raw features   CV ROC AUC 0.854 +/- 0.013
    0.829 → 0.883, a bigger step than the folds' spread, and past boosting (0.854), which had to find the same shapes alone. Same data, same model, better questions.
  7. What the model sees now (2m). "Ask the whole pipeline for pandas output and read the weights by name." 
    churn_pipe = candidates["logistic, + engineered"].set_output(transform="pandas")
churn_pipe.fit(X_train, y_train)
model = churn_pipe[-1]
weights = pd.Series(model.coef_[0], index=model.feature_names_in_)
print(weights[new_columns + ["days_since_last_order", "tenure_months", "support_tickets_12m"]].round(2))
    lapsed_150               0.60
new_customer             0.92
veteran                  0.49
heavy_discount           0.73
tickets_no_app           0.64
days_since_last_order    0.58
tenure_months            0.15
support_tickets_12m     -0.07
dtype: float64
    Every new column carries a big weight; support_tickets_12m itself turned slightly negative, because tickets_no_app took over the ticket effect. The features now say what the tables said.
  8. One test-set check (1m). "The choice is made; now one look." 
    from sklearn.metrics import roc_auc_score
plain_pipe = candidates["logistic, raw features"].fit(X_train, y_train)
for name, pipe in [("raw features", plain_pipe), ("+ engineered", churn_pipe)]:
    auc = roc_auc_score(y_test, pipe.predict_proba(X_test)[:, 1])
    print(f"{name:14s} test ROC AUC {auc:.3f}  accuracy {pipe.score(X_test, y_test):.3f}")
    raw features   test ROC AUC 0.823  accuracy 0.848
+ engineered   test ROC AUC 0.880  accuracy 0.876
    0.823 → 0.880 on the test set, in line with cross-validation: what honest feature choice looks like.
  9. Dates are not numbers yet (2m). "Second dataset: two years of weekly sales. parse_dates, then the .dt accessor." 
    demand = pd.read_csv("data/weekly_demand.csv", parse_dates=["week_start"])
print(demand["week_start"].dtype)
print(demand[["week_start", "store", "product_id", "category", "units_sold"]].head(3))
demand["month"] = demand["week_start"].dt.month
demand["quarter"] = demand["week_start"].dt.quarter
demand["day_of_year"] = demand["week_start"].dt.dayofyear
print(demand[["week_start", "month", "quarter", "day_of_year"]].drop_duplicates("month").head(4))
    datetime64[us]
  week_start      store product_id     category  units_sold
0 2024-01-01  Asheville   ACC-2001  Accessories          20
1 2024-01-01  Asheville   ACC-2002  Accessories          15
2 2024-01-01  Asheville   ACC-2003  Accessories          14
     week_start  month  quarter  day_of_year
0    2024-01-01      1        1            1
480  2024-02-05      2        1           36
864  2024-03-04      3        1           64
1248 2024-04-01      4        2           92
  10. Months on a circle (2m). "Is December far from January? As numbers, 11 apart. On a circle, neighbors." 
    demand["month_sin"] = np.sin(2 * np.pi * demand["month"] / 12)
demand["month_cos"] = np.cos(2 * np.pi * demand["month"] / 12)
circle = demand.drop_duplicates("month").set_index("month")[["month_sin", "month_cos"]].sort_index()
def gap(a, b):
    return np.linalg.norm(circle.loc[a] - circle.loc[b])
print(f"Dec to Jan: number gap {12 - 1}, circle gap {gap(12, 1):.2f}")
print(f"Jun to Jul: number gap {7 - 6}, circle gap {gap(6, 7):.2f}")
    Dec to Jan: number gap 11, circle gap 0.52
Jun to Jul: number gap 1, circle gap 0.52
    Both columns are needed: sine alone gives two different months the same value (quiz question 5).
  11. Every category has its own season (3m). "The most important table of this half." 
    season = (demand[demand["year"] == 2024]
          .groupby(["month", "category"])["units_sold"].mean()
          .unstack()[["Snow", "Tents", "Camp Kitchen", "Apparel"]])
print(season.loc[[1, 4, 7, 10, 12]].round(1))
demand["category_month"] = demand["category"] + "-" + demand["month"].astype(str)
print(demand["category_month"].nunique(), "levels, for example:", demand["category_month"].unique()[:4].tolist())
    category  Snow  Tents  Camp Kitchen  Apparel
month                                       
1         30.3    4.9          15.0     26.6
4          3.2   15.0          29.5     18.8
7          0.2   30.4          65.6     14.8
10        10.2    9.6          23.2     27.7
12        41.8    8.8          25.5     36.3
108 levels, for example: ['Accessories-1', 'Apparel-1', 'Climbing-1', 'Footwear-1']
    Snow in winter, tents in summer: one shared effect per month averages opposite curves into nothing. A category × month column gives each category its own season. The project measures what it is worth (MAE 11.23 → 9.39).
  12. History: lags and rolling windows (3m). "Last week's tents in Bend, not last week's socks in Boise: per series, past only. shift(1) keeps the current week out." 
    demand = demand.sort_values(["store", "product_id", "week_start"])
by_series = demand.groupby(["store", "product_id"])["units_sold"]
demand["lag_1"] = by_series.shift(1)
demand["rolling_4"] = by_series.transform(lambda s: s.shift(1).rolling(4).mean())
print(demand[["week_start", "store", "product_id", "units_sold", "lag_1", "rolling_4"]].head(6))
demand = demand.sort_index()
        week_start      store product_id  units_sold  lag_1  rolling_4
0   2024-01-01  Asheville   ACC-2001          20    NaN        NaN
96  2024-01-08  Asheville   ACC-2001          39   20.0        NaN
192 2024-01-15  Asheville   ACC-2001          13   39.0        NaN
288 2024-01-22  Asheville   ACC-2001          21   13.0        NaN
384 2024-01-29  Asheville   ACC-2001          30   21.0      23.25
480 2024-02-05  Asheville   ACC-2001          37   30.0      25.75
    Follow one row by hand: January 29, lag 21, rolling mean of 20, 39, 13, 21 = 23.25. Ask the question of the day: "On the day this forecast is made, would this number exist?"
  13. An aggregate from the training rows (2m). "Same product, store and month last year: computed from 2024, looked up for 2025." 
    from sklearn.metrics import mean_absolute_error
train_2024 = demand[demand["year"] == 2024]
test_2025 = demand[demand["year"] == 2025]
keys = ["product_id", "store", "month"]
same_month_avg = train_2024.groupby(keys)["units_sold"].mean().rename("avg_same_month_2024")
test_2025 = test_2025.join(same_month_avg, on=keys)
overall_mean = train_2024["units_sold"].mean()
print(f"predict the 2024 mean   MAE {mean_absolute_error(test_2025['units_sold'], np.full(len(test_2025), overall_mean)):.2f}")
print(f"same month last year    MAE {mean_absolute_error(test_2025['units_sold'], test_2025['avg_same_month_2024']):.2f}")
    predict the 2024 mean   MAE 17.13
same month last year    MAE 11.36
    One honest group average cuts the error by a third.
  14. Two silent leaks (2m). "Neither raises an error. Both look like better features." Run the rolling window without shift, then the average over both years. 
    demand = demand.sort_values(["store", "product_id", "week_start"])
by_series = demand.groupby(["store", "product_id"])["units_sold"]
demand["rolling_4_leaky"] = by_series.transform(lambda s: s.rolling(4).mean())
demand = demand.sort_index()
in_2025 = demand["year"] == 2025
for col in ["rolling_4", "rolling_4_leaky"]:
    print(f"{col:16s} used as the forecast: MAE {mean_absolute_error(demand.loc[in_2025, 'units_sold'], demand.loc[in_2025, col]):.2f}")
leaky_avg = demand.groupby(keys)["units_sold"].mean()
leaky_forecast = test_2025.join(leaky_avg.rename("leaky_avg"), on=keys)["leaky_avg"]
print(f"average from 2024 only   MAE {mean_absolute_error(test_2025['units_sold'], test_2025['avg_same_month_2024']):.2f}")
print(f"average from both years  MAE {mean_absolute_error(test_2025['units_sold'], leaky_forecast):.2f}")
    rolling_4        used as the forecast: MAE 12.21
rolling_4_leaky  used as the forecast: MAE 9.28
average from 2024 only   MAE 11.36
average from both years  MAE 9.87
    12.21 → 9.28 and 11.36 → 9.87, both fake: the window contains the week being predicted, the average contains the 2025 answers. Fix: shift first, then roll; aggregate the training rows only.
Three mistakes to expect, and how to coach each
  • Dates read as text. AttributeError: Can only use .dt accessor with datetimelike values Coach: "What is the column's dtype?" parse_dates when loading.
  • A ratio that divides by zero. About 40% of customers have no support tickets, so returns_12m / support_tickets_12m quietly makes inf and NaN, and the error appears only inside the model: ValueError: Input X contains infinity or a value too large for dtype('float64'). Coach: "Which rows have 0 in the denominator?" Divide by something that cannot be 0 (+ 1), or replace on purpose.
  • A feature that knows the answer. A rolling window without shift(1) (MAE 12.21 → 9.28), an average that includes the test year (11.36 → 9.87), or a threshold picked while looking at the test set. None raises anything. Coach with the one question: "On the day this prediction is made, would this number exist?" Write it on the board.
Guided lab
  • Lab A (12m, everyone): labs/module-04/l2_3-creating-features/night_features_starter.py; reference labs/module-04/l2_3-creating-features/night_features_solution.py. Closes a loop from Session 1, where the fraud model gave hour a backwards (negative) weight. Learners look at fraud by hour band and night × new device on the training rows, write add_fraud_features (night, night_new_device, log_amount), put it in the pipeline with FunctionTransformer, and compare by cross-validation.
  • Expected, Lab A: night (0 to 5) 6.71% fraud, about 0.65% in every other band; night with a new device 0.425 vs 0.022 or less elsewhere; CV ROC AUC 0.867 → 0.864 (flat) but CV average precision 0.535 → 0.564; the hour weight -0.28 → 0.01, night_new_device 0.19; test average precision 0.463 → 0.504, frauds in the top 100: 56 → 61.
  • Circulate in Lab A for: pipe[:-1].get_feature_names_out() raising (a FunctionTransformer without feature_names_out cannot name its output: ask the prep step, as the TODO says); a feature function without df.copy(); computing flags on X_train by hand outside the pipeline and then forgetting them for the test set. Debrief: "ROC AUC did not move. Is the feature worthless?" No: average precision and the top 100 improved, which is where a review team works; AUC averages over the whole ranking. Choosing the metric that matches the job is Lesson 1.4 again.
  • Lab B (8m, pairs): labs/module-04/l2_3-creating-features/daily_sales_features_starter.py; reference labs/module-04/l2_3-creating-features/daily_sales_features_solution.py. Daily store sales, where the day of the week matters: calendar parts, lag_1, lag_7, a shifted rolling_7 per store, each used as a forecast, then three Ridge pipelines; TODO 5 (a leaky window) can go home.
  • Expected, Lab B: mean sales Mon..Sun 4,639 / 4,270 / 4,512 / 4,750 / 6,024 / 8,209 / 6,988; as forecasts lag_1 MAE 1,350, lag_7 1,072, rolling_7 1,451; Ridge: calendar only 996, + lag_7 + rolling_7 708, + store × day + lag_1 586; the leaky window 551 (better, and useless).
Start the lesson project

The lesson project is the weekly demand forecaster: a linear model for 2025 built from 2024, one idea at a time, with a scoreboard. In the room, Steps 1 to 3 (15 minutes).

  • Read the brief aloud. Step 1 builds every feature on the full table before splitting (lags must reach back from January 2025 into December 2024), using only the past. Done = 5088 4896 0 missing lags in test, baseline MAE 17.13.
  • Step 2: the evaluate helper and two models: raw columns 11.28, + month 11.23. Ask: "Why did month do almost nothing?" (The season table: opposite seasons average out.) Step 3: + category × month 9.39.
  • Goes home: Step 4 (log target 7.92), Step 5 (lags: still 7.92, a genuine non-result), Step 6 (boosting on raw columns 8.61, the scoreboard chart, demand_scoreboard.csv).
Checks for understanding

Ask these out loud during the demo and the lab:

  • Why did the logistic regression give tenure a small weight? Answer: the effect is a U-shape; a straight line through a U is nearly flat.
  • What is support_tickets_12m * (1 - app_user) for app users? Answer: 0. It gives non-app tickets their own weight: an interaction.
  • Which rows may you look at to choose the 150-day threshold? Answer: the training rows only.
  • Why do cyclical encodings need both sine and cosine? Answer: either one alone gives two different months the same value.
  • A rolling mean improved a forecast dramatically. First question? Answer: does the window include the row being predicted (no shift(1))?

Workbook self-check, items Q7 to Q12 (learners do them at the end of the block or at home).

Answers: Q7 [nan, 5.0, nan, 9.0, 7.0]; Q8 C) A straight line through a U-shape is nearly flat; flags such as new_customer and veteran let the model express it; Q9 A) Give support tickets their own weight for customers without the app; Q10 [ 0. -1. 1.]; Q11 B) The 2025 weeks being predicted helped compute their own feature; Q12 C) [0.5, nan, inf]. Full set with explanations in the Answer Key.

Differentiation

Support: for learners slowed by pandas, hand them the finished add_churn_features from the lesson and ask them to check two rows by hand against the raw columns instead of typing it; in Lab B, do TODO 1 and 2 together on the projector.

Stretch: the lesson's Exercise 3 (SplineTransformer on three columns: CV 0.870 with no hand-picked cut-offs) and Exercise 4 (give boosting the same ideas: MAE 8.37 vs the log-linear 7.92), then the Bonus Challenge (temperature × category interactions).

Closing

Exit ticket (3 questions, no notes, 4 minutes)

Put the three questions on the slide. Learners answer in the workbook's Exit Ticket box or on a sticky note. Before Session 5, sort the notes: question 1 tells you whether to spend the Session 5 warm-up on "inside the folds" (Lesson 2.4's selection leak and Lesson 2.5's SMOTE leak are the same idea).

  1. Name two things a Pipeline does that you had to remember by hand in Lesson 2.1, and one kind of leak it cannot prevent. Answer: it fits every step on the training rows and only transforms at prediction time; cross-validation and grid search refit every step inside each fold (and one file saves it all). It cannot stop a column from the future (cancellation_reason) that you put in the feature list.
  2. The signup-date churn rate scored 0.930 in cross-validation and 0.571 on the test set. Why, and what fixed the measurement? Answer: the rates were computed from every training label before the folds, so each validation row's own label was in its feature. TargetEncoder inside a pipeline refits per fold: honest CV 0.586, matching the test.
  3. Give one feature you built today, the shape or interaction it lets a linear model express, and how you knew it helped. Answer (examples): lapsed_150 (a jump at 150 days), new_customer/veteran (a U-shape), tickets_no_app or night_new_device (an interaction), category × month (a season per category); measured with cross-validation against the plain model (0.829 → 0.883; MAE 11.23 → 9.39).

Instructor Kit · Facilitator Guide

Session 5: Feature Selection and Imbalanced Classes

How to teach the fifth session of Machine Learning Foundations: feature selection (filters, wrappers, L1, and the tolerance rule that cuts Pinecrest's churn model from 16 inputs to 6) and imbalanced classes (why accuracy fails on 0.9% fraud, what class weights and samplers really do, and how to set a threshold from the price of each mistake). Both lessons share one warning: a step that looks at the labels must happen inside each training fold, or cross-validation reports 80% on pure noise and AP 0.91 on fiction.

2 lessons 3 hrs session Lessons 2.4–2.5 Session 5 of 11

How to Use This Guide

  • Same rhythm for both lessons: frame (the goal and one mental model) → live demo (you type, they type along) → guided lab (a kit lab, new practice on the lesson's skills) → start the lesson project. Exercises, the quiz and the journal are homework.
  • Both lessons are about the same trap in two forms: a step that looks at the labels (choosing columns, rebalancing) must happen inside each training fold, or cross-validation lies. Say it at 0:10 and at 1:35.
  • Type, do not paste, except where a step says "start the cell, then talk": forward selection and the samplers take 10 to 30 seconds on a laptop. Every code block below was run to produce the output printed under it (Python 3.12, pandas 3.0, scikit-learn 1.9, imbalanced-learn, 4 CPU threads).
  • Keep dimensionality_feature_selection.html and imbalanced_learning.html open in browser tabs for the charts you show but do not type: the RFECV curve, the forward-selection path, the side-by-side sampler table and the cost curve. Labs live in labs/module-05/: _starter.py files are handouts, _solution.py files are yours.
  • Session 6 opens with Checkpoint 1 on Modules 1 and 2. Mention it in the warm-up and again at the wrap-up so nobody is surprised.
The #1 risk of Session 5: numbers that are too good

Both lessons have a moment where a wrong method produces an impressive number: about 80% accuracy on pure noise, AP 0.91 from SMOTE before cross-validation. Learners who miss the point leave thinking selection or SMOTE "improves the model". Protect the noise experiment (2.4 Step 9) and the SMOTE leak (2.5 Step 11) even if you cut elsewhere, and in both labs ask the same question: "Which number would you have reported if you had not known the truth?"

Before the Session

  • ☐ Run the kit check: python3 labs/check_labs.py module-05 from this kit's folder. Expect 8 passed, 0 failed (four lab pairs; debug_imbalance.py is not run) in under a minute.
  • ☐ Copy the starters only to the shared folder: labs/module-05/l2_4-feature-selection/*_starter.py, labs/module-05/l2_5-imbalanced-classes/*_starter.py and debug_imbalance.py. Keep every *_solution.py on your machine.
  • ☐ Downloads: none. imbalanced-learn (import imblearn) is in the course requirements and check_environment.py already checks it.
  • ☐ Projector machine: (.venv) active in your pinecrest-ml, JupyterLab started from that folder, two empty notebooks lesson_2_4 and lesson_2_5, font 18 pt or more. Run demo Steps 6 to 7 of 2.4 and Step 7 of 2.5 once beforehand to know how long they take on your machine.
  • ☐ Board: three habits for 2.4 (decide with CV · selection inside the pipeline · smallest set within a tolerance); for 2.5 the smoke detector, RANK | DECIDE, and cost = $10 × flagged + $375 × missed.
  • ☐ Print or share: the workbook pages for Session 5 and sticky notes for the exit ticket.

Opening Segment

0:00

Warm-up: Pipelines and Features

10 min
Timing
6mFive retrieval questions (workbook, Session 5 warm-up box), answered alone, then compared with a neighbor
4mReveal and discuss; homework check: who has churn_pipeline.joblib and demand_scoreboard.csv?
Questions and answers
  • W1. A pipeline is make_pipeline(SimpleImputer(), StandardScaler(), LogisticRegression()). What does pipe.predict(X_new) do to each of the three steps? Answer: The imputer and the scaler only transform the new rows, with the medians, means and standard deviations they learned during fit; the model then predicts. Nothing is refitted. If someone says "it refits the scaler", ask what would happen with one new customer: a scaler fitted on one row.
  • W2. In Lesson 2.2 the signup-date churn rate scored CV ROC AUC 0.930 when computed with pandas, and 0.586 as a TargetEncoder inside the pipeline. Which is honest, and why was the other so high? Answer: 0.586. The pandas rates used every training row's label, including the rows that later sat in each validation fold; with one or two customers per date, "the rate on your date" was mostly your own label. This is today's selection leak in another costume: any step that looks at y belongs inside the folds.
  • W3. What does this print? (code on the slide) Answer: ['logisticregression__C'] make_pipeline names each step after its class in lowercase; step__parameter reaches any setting. Today you swap a whole step the same way: set_params(logisticregression=...).
  • W4. add_churn_features builds tickets_no_app = support_tickets_12m * (1 - app_user). What is it for an app user with 3 tickets, and why build it? Answer: 0. Tickets raise churn for customers without the app but not for app users; a linear model cannot express that from the two raw columns, so the product gives non-app tickets their own weight. Keep this pair in mind: in today's demo forward selection keeps both raw columns because they matter together.
  • W5. Why can FunctionTransformer(add_churn_features) be fitted outside the folds without leaking, while TargetEncoder cannot? Answer: The feature function is stateless: its thresholds are fixed numbers and it learns nothing from the rows it sees. TargetEncoder learns rates from y, so fitting it on validation rows hands them their own answers. Feature selection and resampling, today's two subjects, are both "learn from y" steps.
Reading the room
  • W2 and W5 wrong means the room does not yet feel why some steps must live inside the folds. Spend an extra minute on 2.4 Step 9 and 2.5 Step 11: both are that idea again.
  • W3 wrong is harmless: today you will use set_params(logisticregression=...) and pipe[-1], and the demo narrates them.
  • No saved pipeline or scoreboard? Not needed today; both projects start from the data file. They do need their Lesson 2.3 notebook later (Exercise 4 of 2.4 reuses add_churn_features).
  • The redundant-column guesses (Session 4 homework): take three or four and write them on the board. Do not judge them now: 2.4 demo Steps 2 and 3 check them (no pair is a copy by correlation, yet spend_12m, orders_12m and avg_order_value rebuild one another). Tick the guesses that survive.
  • The Module 1 model rewritten as a pipeline (also Session 4 homework): one hand up is enough; ask that learner which steps went inside and why. Anyone who skipped it gets the same practice in today's labs.

Lesson Plans

2.4

Feature Selection

75 min
Objectives (from the lesson page)
  • Explain what feature selection buys you (simpler, cheaper, sturdier models) and what it usually does not (big accuracy gains)
  • Find redundant features with correlations, exact relationships and cross-tabulations
  • Use filter (SelectKBest), wrapper (RFECV, SequentialFeatureSelector) and embedded (L1, SelectFromModel) methods, and say what each can miss
  • Keep selection inside a Pipeline so cross-validation stays honest, and show what happens when it is not
  • Choose the smallest feature set whose cross-validated score is within a tolerance of the best
Timing in the room
10mFrame: the ultralight backpack; three families of methods and their blind spots; the three habits
30mLive demo: redundancy by hand, two filters, RFECV, forward selection, the L1 sweep, the noise experiment
20mGuided lab: lean_fraud_starter.py (everyone), then vendor_columns_starter.py in pairs
15mStart the lesson project: the lean churn model, Steps 1 to 3
Mental model to say

"Experienced hikers weigh every item before a long trip. Two stoves? One goes home: a redundant feature. The camp chair you never sit in? Home: an irrelevant feature. But the water filter stays, even though you use it twice a day. The goal is not the lightest pack; it is the lightest pack that still gets you to the end of the trail. In model terms: the smallest feature set that keeps the score."

Then the honest promise, in one breath: "Selection rarely makes a good model much more accurate. It makes it simpler to collect, explain and monitor. On Pinecrest's churn table it will cut 16 inputs to 6 at no cost." Draw three columns on the board, Filter · Wrapper · Embedded, with one line each: scores columns alone (fast, blind to the model and to teamwork) · tries subsets with the real model (slow, sees teamwork) · selects while training (one fit, tied to one model type).

Close with the three habits, written under the columns and left there: decide with cross-validation · keep selection inside the pipeline · prefer the smallest set within a tolerance of the best.

Live demo (you type, they follow)

New notebook lesson_2_4. Ask for a prediction before every number. The two moments that matter most are Step 6 (the plateau: read the curve, not the winner) and Step 9 (80% on pure noise).

  1. Load the churn table and the standard split (2m). "Same customers, same split as every churn lesson, but today all 11 numeric columns, including email_opens_90d with its gaps. Every pipeline imputes it." 
    import numpy as np
import pandas as pd
from sklearn.model_selection import train_test_split

customers = pd.read_csv("data/customers_churn.csv")
numeric = ["tenure_months", "orders_12m", "spend_12m", "avg_order_value", "days_since_last_order",
           "returns_12m", "return_rate", "support_tickets_12m", "discount_share", "app_user", "email_opens_90d"]
train, test = train_test_split(customers, test_size=0.25, stratify=customers["churned"], random_state=42)
X_train, y_train = train[numeric], train["churned"]
X_test, y_test = test[numeric], test["churned"]
print(X_train.shape, X_test.shape)
    (4500, 11) (1500, 11)
  2. Which columns move together? (3m). "Cheapest check first: correlations, each pair once." 
    corr = X_train.corr()
upper = corr.where(np.triu(np.ones(corr.shape, dtype=bool), k=1)).stack()
print(upper[upper.abs() > 0.3].sort_values(key=abs, ascending=False).round(2).head(4))
    returns_12m  return_rate              0.68
orders_12m   spend_12m                0.57
spend_12m    avg_order_value          0.49
orders_12m   days_since_last_order   -0.41
dtype: float64
    Ask: "Is any pair a copy?" (No: nothing near 1.) "So no redundancy?" Take answers, then Step 3.
  3. A relationship correlation cannot see (2m). "Lesson 2.3 found this one: spend is orders times average order value." 
    rebuilt = X_train["orders_12m"] * X_train["avg_order_value"]
print("largest gap between spend and orders x AOV:", (X_train["spend_12m"] - rebuilt).abs().max().round(2))
    largest gap between spend and orders x AOV: 0.09
    Nine cents of rounding. Any one of the three can be rebuilt from the other two. Say: "The fastest selection method is asking how a column was made." Mention that region repeats home_store for store shoppers and loyalty_tier is assigned from spend and tenure (lesson page, no need to type).
  4. Two filters disagree (3m). "A filter scores each column alone against the target. F compares the churners' mean with the stayers'; mutual information sees any kind of dependence." 
    from sklearn.feature_selection import f_classif, mutual_info_classif
from sklearn.impute import SimpleImputer

X_filled = SimpleImputer(strategy="median").set_output(transform="pandas").fit_transform(X_train)
f_scores, _ = f_classif(X_filled, y_train)
mi_scores = mutual_info_classif(X_filled, y_train, random_state=42)
filters = pd.DataFrame({"F": f_scores, "MI": mi_scores}, index=numeric)
filters["F_rank"] = filters["F"].rank(ascending=False).astype(int)
filters["MI_rank"] = filters["MI"].rank(ascending=False).astype(int)
print(filters.sort_values("F_rank").round(4))
                                  F      MI  F_rank  MI_rank
days_since_last_order  735.4249  0.0641       1        1
discount_share         196.5015  0.0118       2        6
spend_12m              174.5598  0.0190       3        4
orders_12m             156.1053  0.0206       4        3
app_user                64.2232  0.0145       5        5
support_tickets_12m     41.8948  0.0000       6       10
email_opens_90d         37.1523  0.0000       7       10
returns_12m             15.5411  0.0064       8        8
tenure_months           14.9306  0.0329       9        2
avg_order_value          8.5355  0.0106      10        7
return_rate              1.1013  0.0000      11       10
    Point at tenure_months: rank 9 for F, rank 2 for MI. Ask: "Which lesson showed why?" (2.3: the U-shape; the means are close.) Then support_tickets_12m: MI rounds it to 0 although it matters, for non-app customers.
  5. A filter inside the pipeline (3m). "SelectKBest keeps the k best; in a pipeline it is refitted on every training fold. Predict: does picking the best six beat using all eleven?" 
    from functools import partial
from sklearn.feature_selection import SelectKBest
from sklearn.linear_model import LogisticRegression
from sklearn.model_selection import StratifiedKFold, cross_val_score
from sklearn.pipeline import make_pipeline
from sklearn.preprocessing import StandardScaler

cv = StratifiedKFold(n_splits=5, shuffle=True, random_state=42)
mi = partial(mutual_info_classif, random_state=42)

def churn_pipe(*steps):
    return make_pipeline(SimpleImputer(strategy="median"), StandardScaler(), *steps, LogisticRegression())

for name, pipe in [("all 11 columns", churn_pipe()),
                   ("best 6 by F", churn_pipe(SelectKBest(f_classif, k=6))),
                   ("best 6 by MI", churn_pipe(SelectKBest(mi, k=6)))]:
    scores = cross_val_score(pipe, X_train, y_train, cv=cv, scoring="roc_auc")
    print(f"{name:15s} CV ROC AUC {scores.mean():.3f}")
    all 11 columns  CV ROC AUC 0.833
best 6 by F     CV ROC AUC 0.824
best 6 by MI    CV ROC AUC 0.813
    Both lose: F by 0.009, MI by 0.020. MI keeps tenure, whose U-shape a logistic regression cannot use. "A filter knows nothing about the model that comes after it."
  6. RFECV and its plateau (4m). "Recursive elimination drops the column with the smallest weight, refits, repeats; RFECV scores every count with cross-validation." 
    from sklearn.feature_selection import RFECV

rfecv = RFECV(LogisticRegression(), cv=cv, scoring="roc_auc")
rfe_pipe = make_pipeline(SimpleImputer(strategy="median"), StandardScaler(), rfecv).set_output(transform="pandas")
rfe_pipe.fit(X_train, y_train)
curve = pd.Series(rfecv.cv_results_["mean_test_score"], index=rfecv.cv_results_["n_features"])
print("RFECV keeps", rfecv.n_features_, "columns")
print(curve.round(4).to_dict())
    RFECV keeps 11 columns
{1: 0.7215, 2: 0.8063, 3: 0.8116, 4: 0.8226, 5: 0.8263, 6: 0.8328, 7: 0.8328, 8: 0.8323, 9: 0.8332, 10: 0.8333, 11: 0.8333}
    It keeps all 11, won by a ten-thousandth. Show the lesson's curve chart. Say: "Read the curve, not n_features_. From six on it is flat, and the folds disagree by far more than these differences." Write the rule on the board: smallest set within a tolerance of the best.
  7. Forward selection finds teamwork (4m). "A wrapper adds, one at a time, the column that raises the cross-validated score most. tol=0.002 stops it when the next column adds less." Start this cell, then talk while it runs (about 10 to 20 seconds on a laptop). 
    from sklearn.feature_selection import SequentialFeatureSelector

sfs = SequentialFeatureSelector(LogisticRegression(), n_features_to_select="auto", tol=0.002,
                                direction="forward", cv=cv, scoring="roc_auc")
sfs_pipe = make_pipeline(SimpleImputer(strategy="median"), StandardScaler(), sfs).set_output(transform="pandas")
sfs_pipe.fit(X_train, y_train)
forward_six = list(sfs.get_feature_names_out())
print(forward_six)
scores = cross_val_score(churn_pipe(SequentialFeatureSelector(LogisticRegression(), n_features_to_select="auto",
                                                              tol=0.002, cv=cv, scoring="roc_auc")),
                         X_train, y_train, cv=cv, scoring="roc_auc")
print(f"forward selection inside CV: {scores.mean():.3f}")
    ['orders_12m', 'days_since_last_order', 'support_tickets_12m', 'discount_share', 'app_user', 'email_opens_90d']
forward selection inside CV: 0.832
    Six columns, the full model's score. It kept support_tickets_12m AND app_user, the pair that matters together (warm-up W4). Point at the second number: it cross-validates the whole selection procedure, the honest estimate.
  8. L1: selection while training (4m). "An L1 penalty charges for the total size of the weights; the cheapest way to pay is zeros. Smaller C, heavier penalty, more zeros. liblinear because the default solver refuses L1." 
    rows, zeroed = [], {}
for C in [0.005, 0.01, 0.02, 0.05, 1.0]:
    l1_pipe = churn_pipe().set_params(logisticregression=LogisticRegression(l1_ratio=1, solver="liblinear", C=C))
    auc = cross_val_score(l1_pipe, X_train, y_train, cv=cv, scoring="roc_auc").mean()
    weights = l1_pipe.fit(X_train, y_train)[-1].coef_[0]
    zeroed[C] = [col for col, w in zip(numeric, weights) if w == 0]
    rows.append({"C": C, "kept": int((weights != 0).sum()), "cv_auc": round(auc, 4)})
print(pd.DataFrame(rows).to_string(index=False))
print("zero at C=0.05:", zeroed[0.05])
        C  kept  cv_auc
0.005     2  0.7955
0.010     6  0.8175
0.020     8  0.8301
0.050     8  0.8333
1.000    11  0.8334
zero at C=0.05: ['avg_order_value', 'returns_12m', 'return_rate']
    Read from the bottom: at C = 0.05 three columns drop with no loss, and they are the redundant ones from Steps 2 and 3. At C = 0.01 useful columns start to go and the score falls.
  9. The most important experiment: pure noise (4m). "200 rows, 5,000 random columns, a random target. Honest accuracy is a coin flip. Watch what selecting first, then cross-validating, reports." 
    def noise_experiment(seed):
    rng = np.random.default_rng(seed)
    X_noise = rng.normal(size=(200, 5000))
    y_noise = rng.integers(0, 2, size=200)
    chosen = SelectKBest(f_classif, k=20).fit(X_noise, y_noise)
    leaky = cross_val_score(LogisticRegression(), chosen.transform(X_noise), y_noise, cv=cv).mean()
    honest = cross_val_score(make_pipeline(SelectKBest(f_classif, k=20), LogisticRegression()),
                             X_noise, y_noise, cv=cv).mean()
    return {"seed": seed, "select first, then CV": leaky, "select inside CV": honest}

noise = pd.DataFrame([noise_experiment(seed) for seed in range(42, 47)]).set_index("seed")
print(noise.round(3))
print(noise.mean().round(3).to_string())
          select first, then CV  select inside CV
seed                                         
42                    0.825             0.595
43                    0.780             0.545
44                    0.820             0.515
45                    0.770             0.520
46                    0.795             0.460
select first, then CV    0.798
select inside CV         0.527
    About 80% on pure noise, five times out of five, against 53% when selection is a pipeline step. Say: "Same data, same model, one line of difference. Any choice made with the labels is part of the model."
  10. One silent mistake: L1 without a scaler (1m). "No error, and that is the problem." 
    l1 = LogisticRegression(l1_ratio=1, solver="liblinear", C=0.02)
for name, pipe in [("scaled", make_pipeline(SimpleImputer(strategy="median"), StandardScaler(), l1)),
                   ("unscaled", make_pipeline(SimpleImputer(strategy="median"), l1))]:
    weights = pipe.fit(X_train, y_train)[-1].coef_[0]
    print(f"{name:9s} keeps discount_share: {weights[numeric.index('discount_share')] != 0}, "
          f"keeps avg_order_value: {weights[numeric.index('avg_order_value')] != 0}")
    scaled    keeps discount_share: True, keeps avg_order_value: False
unscaled  keeps discount_share: False, keeps avg_order_value: True
    Unscaled, L1 drops discount_share (0 to 1, needs a big weight) and keeps redundant avg_order_value (dollars, tiny weight). It selects by units.
Three mistakes to expect, and how to coach each
  • Selecting before the split or outside the pipeline. Symptom: a CV score that rises when they select (often with SelectKBest(...).fit_transform(X_train, y_train) and then cross_val_score on the result). Coach: "Which rows did the selector see? Did the validation folds help choose the columns?" Point at the noise experiment's two numbers.
  • L1 or RFE on unscaled columns, or the default solver. Symptoms: ValueError: Solver lbfgs supports only 'l2' or None penalties, got l1 penalty., or an L1 model that keeps avg_order_value and drops discount_share. Coach: "What does the penalty charge for? Is a weight of 3 on a 0-to-1 column the same as 3 on dollars?"
  • Reading the winner instead of the curve, and "dropped" as "irrelevant". Symptoms: "RFECV says keep all 11", or "L1 shows average order value doesn't matter". Coach: "How far apart are the scores on the plateau, and how far apart are the folds?" and "Dropped given what else is in the model?"
Guided lab
  • Lab A (12m, everyone): labs/module-05/l2_4-feature-selection/lean_fraud_starter.py; reference labs/module-05/l2_4-feature-selection/lean_fraud_solution.py. The lesson's tools on fraud: nine raw columns plus five engineered ones (night, night on a new device, gift cards, log of the amount, brand-new accounts), judged by cross-validated average precision because fraud is rare. TODO 1 to 4 in the room; TODO 5 (tolerance rule and test) can finish at home.
  • Expected, Lab A: amount and log_amount correlate 0.88 (the only pair above 0.5); the F-test ranks night_new_device first (4,344); CV AP 0.5512 ± 0.0618 with all 14, 0.5202 with the raw 9; forward selection adds velocity_24h, night_new_device, log_amount, card_attempts, ...; within 0.01 of the best: 8 columns; test AP raw 9 0.444, all 14 0.505, lean 8 0.505.
  • Circulate in Lab A for: forward selection judged on the test set ("which rows chose the columns?"); a tolerance smaller than the fold spread treated as meaningful; surprise that the F-test's winner is not forward selection's first pick (velocity_24h alone ranks better; the interaction shines next to it). Debrief: "Engineering moved AP from 0.444 to 0.505; selection then dropped six columns for free. Create generously, keep what earns its place."
  • Lab B (8m, pairs): labs/module-05/l2_4-feature-selection/vendor_columns_starter.py; reference labs/module-05/l2_4-feature-selection/vendor_columns_solution.py. A data vendor's 1,000 extra columns (simulated as pure noise) on a 300-customer pilot. One types, one predicts each number.
