Start the lesson project

The lesson project is the lean churn model: the smallest set of the 16 candidate columns whose CV ROC AUC is within 0.005 of the best, confirmed once on the test set. In the room, Steps 1 to 3 (15 minutes).

  • Read the brief from the lesson page: every input column is a data feed someone must maintain. Step 1 together: 16 candidate columns; {'email_opens_90d': 419, 'age_group': 359}.
  • Step 2 (build_model for any list of columns, numeric and categorical) is the step that matters: walk the room. Done = all 16 columns: CV ROC AUC 0.8324.
  • Step 3 on their own: dropping the four derived columns gives 0.8305.
  • Goes home: Step 4 (forward selection by hand, about a minute to run, starting with days_since_last_order), Step 5 (tolerance rule: 6 columns) and Step 6 (test ROC AUC 0.826 full against 0.824 lean; lean_features.txt).
Checks for understanding

Ask these out loud during the demo and the lab:

  • Why can't correlation find that spend is orders times average order value? Answer: correlation looks at two columns and straight lines; this is a product of two columns.
  • RFECV's best count is 11, but 6 scores 0.8328 against 0.8333. What do you keep? Answer: 6: the gap is far smaller than the spread between folds; prefer the smaller set within a tolerance.
  • Why does L1 need a scaler? Answer: it charges for weight size, and a column's unit decides how big its weight must be.
  • Name one step besides selection that must live inside the folds. Answer: anything that looks at y: target encoding (2.2), resampling (2.5), tuning (6.2).

Workbook self-check, items Q1 to Q6 (learners do them at the end of the block or at home).

Answers: Q1 C) A filter scores each column on its own; the wrapper judges each column next to the ones already chosen, so it sees that the two matter together; Q2 ['score']; Q3 B) Put the selector inside a pipeline and cross-validate the whole pipeline, expecting a lower number; Q4 D) discount_share runs from 0 to 1, so it needs a large weight, and the penalty charges for the size of each weight; Q5 A) 6; Q6 B) Given spend and orders, average order value adds nothing new to this model. Full set with explanations in the Answer Key.

Differentiation

Support: give Lab A's TODO 1 and 2 to the whole room on the projector, then let pairs do TODO 3 and 4 with cv_ap already written. For anyone lost in method names, the board's three columns are the map: ask "which column is this method in?" before each demo step.

Stretch: the lesson's Exercise 2 (backward selection reaches the same six), then the Bonus Challenge (how stable is the lean set across CV shuffles and half the data?). In Lab A, ask them to try SelectFromModel with the L1 model at a few values of C and compare with their forward path.

2.5

Imbalanced Classes

75 min
Objectives (from the lesson page)
  • Explain why accuracy fails on rare classes and judge a model with recall, precision and average precision against the right baseline
  • Apply class_weight="balanced" and the imbalanced-learn samplers (random under- and oversampling, SMOTE) inside a leak-proof pipeline
  • Show that weights and resampling mostly move the decision threshold, and explain what they do to predicted probabilities
  • Choose a threshold from the cost of each kind of mistake, by formula and with TunedThresholdClassifierCV
  • Check who carries the burden of false alarms before a model goes live
Timing in the room
10mFrame: the smoke detector; ranking against deciding; mistakes have prices
30mLive demo: the accuracy paradox, AP, weights and samplers, cost by threshold, the formula, the tuned threshold, the SMOTE leak
20mGuided lab: retention_threshold_starter.py (everyone), then rebalance_tree_starter.py in pairs
15mStart the lesson project: the June review queue, Steps 1 to 3
Mental model to say

"A smoke detector that never beeps is right 364 days a year, and worthless. One that beeps every time you make toast catches every fire, and soon gets its battery pulled. A useful detector sits between the two, and where exactly depends on what a false alarm costs compared with a missed fire."

Then split the board in two: RANK (does the model put frauds near the top? average precision against the base rate) and DECIDE (where do we cut? cost, capacity or a recall target). "Today's big surprise: class weights and resampling, the fixes every tutorial reaches for, mostly change DECIDE, not RANK, and you can change DECIDE directly by moving the threshold." Write cost = $10 × flagged + $375 × missed under DECIDE.

Live demo (you type, they follow)

New notebook lesson_2_5. The two predictions that matter: "what does 'all legitimate' score?" (Step 2) and "what AP does SMOTE-before-CV report?" (Step 11).

  1. How rare is fraud? (1m). "Six months of online payments." 
    import numpy as np
import pandas as pd

tx = pd.read_csv("data/transactions.csv")
print(tx.shape)
print(tx["is_fraud"].value_counts().to_dict())
print(f"fraud rate: {tx['is_fraud'].mean():.3%}")
    (50000, 13)
{0: 49562, 1: 438}
fraud rate: 0.876%
    Fewer than one in a hundred. Write 0.876% on the board.
  2. The accuracy paradox (3m). "Stratified split, because with 438 frauds a random split could leave the test set short. Predict: what does 'all legitimate' score?" 
    from sklearn.model_selection import train_test_split
from sklearn.dummy import DummyClassifier
from sklearn.metrics import recall_score

num_cols = ["account_age_days", "amount", "hour", "items", "device_new",
            "billing_shipping_mismatch", "country_mismatch", "card_attempts", "velocity_24h"]
cat_cols = ["category"]
X = tx[num_cols + cat_cols]
y = tx["is_fraud"]
X_train, X_test, y_train, y_test = train_test_split(X, y, test_size=0.3, stratify=y, random_state=42)
print(f"train: {len(y_train):,} rows, {y_train.sum()} frauds")
print(f"test : {len(y_test):,} rows, {y_test.sum()} frauds")
dummy = DummyClassifier(strategy="most_frequent").fit(X_train, y_train)
print(f"'all legitimate' accuracy: {dummy.score(X_test, y_test):.4f}")
print(f"frauds caught: {recall_score(y_test, dummy.predict(X_test)):.0%}")
    train: 35,000 rows, 307 frauds
test : 15,000 rows, 131 frauds
'all legitimate' accuracy: 0.9913
frauds caught: 0%
    99.1% accurate, catches nothing. Draw the smoke detector that never beeps.
  3. Judge the ranking: AP against its baseline (3m). "A pipeline from Lesson 2.2, built by a function so no two pipelines share one fitted ColumnTransformer. Average precision's baseline is the fraud rate, not 0.5." 
    from sklearn.compose import ColumnTransformer
from sklearn.preprocessing import StandardScaler, OneHotEncoder
from sklearn.pipeline import make_pipeline
from sklearn.linear_model import LogisticRegression
from sklearn.metrics import roc_auc_score, average_precision_score

def make_preprocess():
    return ColumnTransformer([
        ("num", StandardScaler(), num_cols),
        ("cat", OneHotEncoder(handle_unknown="ignore"), cat_cols),
    ])

plain = make_pipeline(make_preprocess(), LogisticRegression())
plain.fit(X_train, y_train)
p_plain = plain.predict_proba(X_test)[:, 1]
print(f"ROC AUC {roc_auc_score(y_test, p_plain):.3f}   average precision {average_precision_score(y_test, p_plain):.3f}")
print(f"average precision of a random ranking: {y_test.mean():.4f}")
    ROC AUC 0.843   average precision 0.463
average precision of a random ranking: 0.0087
    0.46 against 0.009: a good ranking.
  4. Then the decisions at 0.5 (2m). "A helper that prints what a fraud team cares about." 
    from sklearn.metrics import confusion_matrix

def report(name, y_true, y_pred):
    tn, fp, fn, tp = confusion_matrix(y_true, y_pred, labels=[0, 1]).ravel()
    print(f"{name:16s} flagged {tp + fp:5d}  caught {tp:3d}  missed {fn:3d}  "
          f"recall {tp / (tp + fn):.2f}  precision {tp / max(tp + fp, 1):.2f}")

report("plain @ 0.5", y_test, plain.predict(X_test))
    plain @ 0.5      flagged    44  caught  41  missed  90  recall 0.31  precision 0.93
    44 flags, 41 real, 90 frauds missed. "The ranking is good; the decision rule is timid." Write two words on the board: RANK | DECIDE.
  5. Class weights (3m). "Each fraud mistake now counts about 57 times." 
    from sklearn.utils.class_weight import compute_class_weight

print(compute_class_weight("balanced", classes=np.array([0, 1]), y=y_train).round(2))
weighted = make_pipeline(make_preprocess(), LogisticRegression(class_weight="balanced"))
weighted.fit(X_train, y_train)
p_weighted = weighted.predict_proba(X_test)[:, 1]
print(f"ROC AUC {roc_auc_score(y_test, p_weighted):.3f}   average precision {average_precision_score(y_test, p_weighted):.3f}")
report("balanced @ 0.5", y_test, weighted.predict(X_test))
    [ 0.5 57. ]
ROC AUC 0.848   average precision 0.438
balanced @ 0.5   flagged  1863  caught  90  missed  41  recall 0.69  precision 0.05
    Recall 0.69, but 1,863 flags for 90 frauds, and AUC and AP barely moved (AP even fell). Ask: "Did it learn fraud better, or say fraud more readily?"
  6. What the weights did to the probabilities (2m). "Compare the average probability with the real rate." 
    from scipy.stats import spearmanr

print(f"real fraud rate          : {y_test.mean():.4f}")
print(f"mean probability, plain  : {p_plain.mean():.4f}")
print(f"mean probability, weighted: {p_weighted.mean():.4f}")
print(f"rank correlation of the two scores: {spearmanr(p_plain, p_weighted).statistic:.3f}")
    real fraud rate          : 0.0087
mean probability, plain  : 0.0085
mean probability, weighted: 0.2671
rank correlation of the two scores: 0.961
    The plain model is calibrated (0.0085 against 0.0087); the weighted one averages 0.27. Same order (rank correlation 0.96), probabilities of nothing real. "Hold that thought for Step 8."
  7. Three samplers, inside an imblearn pipeline (4m). "Samplers change the training rows. imblearn's pipeline runs them during fit and skips them during predict, so test rows keep the real balance." Start the cell, then talk through the three samplers while it runs. 
    from imblearn.under_sampling import RandomUnderSampler
from imblearn.over_sampling import RandomOverSampler, SMOTE
from imblearn.pipeline import make_pipeline as make_imb_pipeline

samplers = {
    "undersample": RandomUnderSampler(random_state=42),
    "oversample": RandomOverSampler(random_state=42),
    "SMOTE": SMOTE(random_state=42),
}
for name, sampler in samplers.items():
    pipe = make_imb_pipeline(make_preprocess(), sampler, LogisticRegression())
    pipe.fit(X_train, y_train)
    proba = pipe.predict_proba(X_test)[:, 1]
    print(f"{name:12s} ROC AUC {roc_auc_score(y_test, proba):.3f}   AP {average_precision_score(y_test, proba):.3f}")
    report(f"{name} @ 0.5", y_test, pipe.predict(X_test))
    undersample  ROC AUC 0.843   AP 0.419
undersample @ 0.5 flagged  2172  caught  93  missed  38  recall 0.71  precision 0.04
oversample   ROC AUC 0.848   AP 0.437
oversample @ 0.5 flagged  1866  caught  90  missed  41  recall 0.69  precision 0.05
SMOTE        ROC AUC 0.845   AP 0.428
SMOTE @ 0.5      flagged  1940  caught  89  missed  42  recall 0.68  precision 0.05
    Show the lesson's side-by-side table: four fixes, the same place: about 1,900 flags, recall near 0.7, precision near 0.05, ranking no better. "For a linear model they shift scores across 0.5. You can do that directly."
  8. Price the mistakes (3m). "A review costs $10 of analyst time; a missed fraud costs about $375. Cost = $10 per flag plus $375 per miss." 
    REVIEW_COST, FRAUD_LOSS = 10, 375

def fraud_cost(y_true, y_pred):
    tn, fp, fn, tp = confusion_matrix(y_true, y_pred, labels=[0, 1]).ravel()
    return int(REVIEW_COST * (tp + fp) + FRAUD_LOSS * fn)

print(f"review nothing   : ${fraud_cost(y_test, np.zeros(len(y_test), dtype=int)):,}")
print(f"review everything: ${fraud_cost(y_test, np.ones(len(y_test), dtype=int)):,}")
for threshold in [0.5, 0.2, 0.1, 0.05, 0.02, 0.01]:
    pred = (p_plain >= threshold).astype(int)
    print(f"threshold {threshold:<5} cost ${fraud_cost(y_test, pred):,}")
    review nothing   : $49,125
review everything: $150,000
threshold 0.5   cost $34,190
threshold 0.2   cost $30,100
threshold 0.1   cost $27,455
threshold 0.05  cost $25,395
threshold 0.02  cost $27,250
threshold 0.01  cost $33,230
    Both do-nothing options are expensive, so is 0.5. Lowering the cut to 0.05 saves almost $9,000 with the same model.
  9. The formula, and why not to pick on the test set (3m). "Flag when p × $375 > $10: p > 10/375. That works because the plain model's probabilities are honest." 
    print(f"formula threshold: {REVIEW_COST} / {FRAUD_LOSS} = {REVIEW_COST / FRAUD_LOSS:.3f}")
thresholds = np.geomspace(0.003, 0.9, 80)
costs = [fraud_cost(y_test, (p_plain >= t).astype(int)) for t in thresholds]
best = thresholds[int(np.argmin(costs))]
print(f"cheapest threshold on the test set: {best:.3f}  (cost ${min(costs):,})")
    formula threshold: 10 / 375 = 0.027
cheapest threshold on the test set: 0.043  (cost $25,025)
    Show the lesson's cost-curve chart (log scale). Then: "0.043 was found by peeking at the test set: fine for a picture, wrong for a choice."
  10. TunedThresholdClassifierCV (3m). "Cross-validation tries many thresholds on the training folds, keeps the cheapest, then refits." 
    from sklearn.metrics import make_scorer
from sklearn.model_selection import TunedThresholdClassifierCV

cost_scorer = make_scorer(fraud_cost, greater_is_better=False)
tuned = TunedThresholdClassifierCV(make_pipeline(make_preprocess(), LogisticRegression()),
                                   scoring=cost_scorer, cv=5)
tuned.fit(X_train, y_train)
print(f"threshold chosen by 5-fold CV: {tuned.best_threshold_:.3f}")
print(f"test cost: ${fraud_cost(y_test, tuned.predict(X_test)):,}")
report("tuned threshold", y_test, tuned.predict(X_test))
    threshold chosen by 5-fold CV: 0.040
test cost: $25,295
tuned threshold  flagged   392  caught  74  missed  57  recall 0.56  precision 0.19
    0.040, close to the formula, chosen without the test set: 392 flags, 74 frauds caught, $25,295 against $34,190 at 0.5.
  11. The leak, on purpose (3m). "Resample first, then cross-validate. Predict the AP." 
    from sklearn.model_selection import StratifiedKFold, cross_val_score

cv = StratifiedKFold(n_splits=5, shuffle=True, random_state=42)
X_train_enc = make_preprocess().fit_transform(X_train)
X_smote, y_smote = SMOTE(random_state=42).fit_resample(X_train_enc, y_train)
leaky = cross_val_score(LogisticRegression(), X_smote, y_smote, cv=cv, scoring="average_precision")
honest = cross_val_score(make_imb_pipeline(make_preprocess(), SMOTE(random_state=42), LogisticRegression()),
                         X_train, y_train, cv=cv, scoring="average_precision")
print(f"SMOTE first, then CV : AP {leaky.mean():.3f}")
print(f"SMOTE inside the CV  : AP {honest.mean():.3f}")
    SMOTE first, then CV : AP 0.912
SMOTE inside the CV  : AP 0.495
    0.912 is fiction: validation folds full of synthetic near-copies, at a 50/50 balance production never has. Honest: 0.495. Then say one sentence about Mistake 1 (a sampler in a scikit-learn pipeline raises TypeError) and Mistake 3 (Step 6's inflated probabilities cut at the formula threshold flag all 15,000 payments, the most expensive option).
Three mistakes to expect, and how to coach each
  • A sampler in a scikit-learn pipeline, or resampling before cross-validation. Symptoms: TypeError: All intermediate steps should be transformers and implement fit and transform or be the string 'passthrough...; or a CV AP near 0.9. Coach: "Which pipeline did you import? Did the validation folds keep the real fraud rate?"
  • Judging by accuracy or by recall at 0.5. Symptoms: "the balanced model is better, recall 0.69"; cross_val_score without scoring=. Coach: "How many payments did it flag to get that recall? What did AP do?"
  • The formula threshold on weighted or resampled probabilities, or a threshold chosen on the test set. Symptoms: a model that flags everything; a "best threshold" read off the test-set cost curve. Coach: "Are these probabilities honest? Compare their mean with the real rate." and "Which rows chose the threshold?"
Guided lab
  • Lab A (12m, everyone): labs/module-05/l2_5-imbalanced-classes/retention_threshold_starter.py; reference labs/module-05/l2_5-imbalanced-classes/retention_threshold_solution.py. The lesson's cost reasoning on a different decision: a $15 retention offer against $60 lost per churner nobody contacts, on the canonical churn split with 14 columns.
  • Expected, Lab A: offer nobody $14,880, everyone $22,500; ROC AUC 0.827, AP 0.496 (random 0.165); at 0.5: 112 offers, $12,540; the sweep is cheapest at 0.2 to 0.25 ($10,830 to $10,935); formula 15/60 = 0.25; balanced weights at 0.5: 471 offers, $10,965, but their probabilities average 0.377 against a real 0.165, so the formula on them costs $14,070; tuned threshold 0.234: 343 offers, 153 of 248 churners reached, $10,845.
  • Circulate in Lab A for: a cost function with the ravel() order wrong (tn, fp, fn, tp); the formula applied to the balanced model; thresholds chosen from the test sweep and then reported as the result. Debrief: "With churn at 16.5% the 0.5 cut still costs $1,700 more than a cut chosen from the prices. Same lesson as fraud, milder imbalance."
  • Lab B (8m, pairs): labs/module-05/l2_5-imbalanced-classes/rebalance_tree_starter.py; reference labs/module-05/l2_5-imbalanced-classes/rebalance_tree_solution.py. Tests the lesson's claim that SMOTE can help a deep tree: a depth-6 decision tree on fraud, five candidates compared by CV AP inside imblearn pipelines.
  • Expected, Lab B: CV AP plain 0.335, balanced 0.269, undersample 0.044, oversample 0.271, SMOTE 0.182; at 0.5 plain flags 44 (recall 0.27, precision 0.80), the others 1,517 to 2,242 (recall near 0.65, precision 0.04 to 0.06); SMOTE first, then CV: AP 0.873 on folds that are 50% fraud.
  • Debrief Lab B (1m): "Here every rebalancing hurt the ranking, and undersampling (614 training rows) wrecked it. On this data the claim fails: try it, do not assume it. The leak still reports 0.87."
  • Fast finishers: labs/module-05/l2_5-imbalanced-classes/debug_imbalance.py: two bugs that stop it (TypeError from a scikit-learn pipeline, ValueError: could not convert string to float: 'Gift Cards' from SMOTE before the encoder) and two that do not (accuracy reported as AP; the ravel() order). Fixed, it prints AP 0.535 and 0.495 and a cost of $25,985.
Start the lesson project

The lesson project is a fraud review queue for June: train on January to May, choose model and threshold on those months only, run June once. In the room, Steps 1 to 3 (15 minutes).

  • Read the brief aloud. Step 1 together: the split by date, no shuffling. Done = train Jan-May: 41,738 payments, 374 frauds (0.90%) and test June : 8,262 payments, 64 frauds (0.77%).
  • Step 2: the cost function and June's do-nothing costs, $24,000 and $82,620. "Any model has to beat $24,000."
  • Step 3: three candidates by CV AP with SMOTE inside an imblearn pipeline: plain 0.506, balanced 0.481, SMOTE 0.472. Keep the plain model.
  • Goes home: Step 4 (tuned threshold 0.051 against the formula's 0.027), Step 5 (June once: 166 flags, 41 of 64 caught, $10,285) and Step 6 (fraud_review_queue.csv, no is_fraud column).
Checks for understanding

Ask these out loud during the demo and the lab:

  • What is AP's baseline here, and why not 0.5? Answer: the fraud rate, about 0.009: that is what a random ranking scores.
  • The balanced model's recall jumped and its AP did not. What changed? Answer: where the 0.5 cut falls; the ranking is about the same.
  • Review $10, missed fraud $375: formula threshold, and which model may use it? Answer: 0.027, only a model with calibrated probabilities (the plain one).
  • Why must the sampler sit inside the pipeline? Answer: so validation folds keep the real class balance and contain no synthetic rows.
  • Who carries the cost of a false alarm? Answer: an honest customer whose order is held; check whether one group (new accounts) carries more than its share (Exercise 3).

Workbook self-check, items Q7 to Q12 (learners do them at the end of the block or at home).

Answers: Q7 [ 0.526 10. ]; Q8 D) 0.04; Q9 A) 0.495: resampling first put synthetic frauds, built from frauds in the training folds, into the validation folds, at a 50/50 balance production never has; Q10 405; Q11 C) predict applies the tuned threshold; predict_proba returns the underlying model's probabilities unchanged; Q12 B) The default score of a classifier is accuracy, which hides whether any fraud is found; pass scoring="average_precision" or a cost scorer. Full set with explanations in the Answer Key.

Differentiation

Support: in Lab A, write retention_cost together on the projector (TODO 1 is the step most people get wrong), and give struggling pairs the threshold loop from the demo to adapt. Keep RANK | DECIDE on the board and ask "which side is this?" for every number.

Stretch: the lesson's Exercise 4 (re-tune for losses of $100, $375 and $1,000) and Bonus Challenge (night and night-on-a-new-device features: one good feature moves AP more than every sampler). In Lab A: what threshold fills a capacity of 200 calls, and what does it cost?

Closing

Exit ticket (3 questions, no notes, 4 minutes)

Put the questions on the slide; learners answer in the workbook's Exit Ticket box or on a sticky note. Sort the notes before Session 6: question 1 tells you how much the checkpoint will test what you think it tests.

  1. Why must SelectKBest live inside the pipeline when you cross-validate? Answer: it looks at the labels; outside the pipeline the validation rows help choose the columns, and the score is optimistic (about 80% on pure noise instead of 50%).
  2. A class-weighted fraud model has recall 0.69 where the plain model had 0.31, with the same AP. What changed, and what did it cost? Answer: mostly the cut: more payments cross 0.5 (1,863 flags instead of 44, precision 0.05), and the probabilities are inflated (mean 0.27 against a real 0.009).
  3. A review costs $10 and a missed fraud $375. Which threshold, and on which model's probabilities may you use it? Answer: 10 / 375 ≈ 0.027, on a calibrated model (plain, no weights, no resampling); otherwise tune it with TunedThresholdClassifierCV.

  • Both Quick Quizzes on the lesson pages, and both journal entries.
  • Before the Next Lesson (both pages): keep lean_features.txt and the 2.5 notebook; 2.4: rerun the noise experiment with k=200; 2.5: one problem from your own life with two very different mistake costs, and its formula threshold; rerun 2.5 Step 3 with scoring="roc_auc".
  • Labs not finished in class: lean_fraud TODO 5, vendor_columns, rebalance_tree; optional debug_imbalance.py.

If You Run Long or Short

Running long: what to protect and what to cut first
  • Protect: the noise experiment and the SMOTE leak; 2.5 Steps 4, 8 to 10 (RANK | DECIDE, cost, tuned threshold); RFECV's plateau and the tolerance rule.
  • Cut first (saves 6m): 2.4 Steps 4 and 5 (filters). Show the F/MI table on the lesson page and say: "Filters score columns alone: fast, blind to the model and to teamwork."

Instructor Kit · Facilitator Guide

Session 6: Linear Models and Regularization

How to teach the sixth session of Machine Learning Foundations: Checkpoint 1 on Modules 1 and 2 (audit and fix a leaky churn model, then choose who gets a win-back email from the prices), then linear and logistic regression opened up (least squares by hand, coefficients in units and percent, the sigmoid, odds ratios) and regularization (bias and variance measured, Ridge and Lasso, alpha by cross-validation).

2 lessons + checkpoint 3 hrs session Lessons 3.1–3.2 Session 6 of 11

How to Use This Guide

  • This session has three parts: Checkpoint 1 (30 minutes, right after the warm-up), Lesson 3.1 and Lesson 3.2. The lesson blocks are 60 minutes each instead of 75, so demos are shorter and show charts from the pages instead of typing them.
  • Same rhythm for each lesson: frame → live demo (you type, they follow) → guided lab → start the project. Every code block below was run to produce the output printed under it; the plotting lines are left out (show the charts on regression_linear_logistic.html and regression_regularization.html).
  • Labs live in labs/module-06/; the checkpoint starter and model answer in labs/module-06/checkpoint-1/. Starters are handouts; solutions and the model answer are yours.
  • Why the checkpoint comes first: it covers Modules 1 and 2, the room is fresh, and a lesson that runs long can never eat it.
The #1 risk of Session 6: the clock, then the checkpoint's tone

The lesson pages estimate 100 + 100 minutes; the room has 120 for both. If the checkpoint runs over, take the minutes from the 3.1 demo (cut Steps 2, 3 and 7 to one sentence each), never from the 3.2 bias-variance idea. Say in the first minute that the checkpoint is practice for the final project, open notebook and not graded, and that the rubric is in their hands afterward.

Before the Session

  • ☐ Run the kit check: python3 labs/check_labs.py module-06. Expect 10 passed, 0 failed (four lab pairs and the checkpoint pair), about a minute.
  • ☐ Copy to the shared folder: labs/module-06/l3_1-linear-logistic/*_starter.py, labs/module-06/l3_2-regularization/*_starter.py and labs/module-06/checkpoint-1/checkpoint1_starter.py. Keep checkpoint1_solution.py (the model answer) and every *_solution.py on your machine.
  • ☐ Print the rubric from the Answer Key (one copy per learner, handed out after the checkpoint), or plan to show it on the projector at the wrap-up.
  • ☐ Downloads: none.
  • ☐ Projector machine: two empty notebooks lesson_3_1 and lesson_3_2; browser tabs with both lesson pages scrolled to the charts (Snow scatter, residual fan, S-curve against bands, three-panel bias-variance curves, the U chart).
  • ☐ Board: for the checkpoint, the four verbs PLAN · AUDIT · REBUILD · REPORT and the end time; for 3.1, ŷ = b₀ + b₁x and p = 1 / (1 + e^-z); for 3.2, expected error = bias² + variance + noise.
Known environment traps
SymptomCauseFix
TypeError: LogisticRegression.__init__() got an unexpected keyword argument 'multi_class'Code from an older tutorialDelete the argument: softmax is what happens.
TypeError: Ridge.__init__() got an unexpected keyword argument 'normalize'Older tutorialPut a StandardScaler in front, in the pipeline.
ConvergenceWarning from Lasso or from LogisticRegression(C=np.inf)Tiny penalty, many correlated columnsRaise max_iter, scale, and ask whether so little penalty makes sense. The churn_curves lab silences it on purpose for the unpenalized degree-3 fit.
ValueError: Unknown label type: continuousA classifier given a numeric targetUse LinearRegression or Ridge.

Opening Segment

0:00

Warm-up: Selection and Imbalance

10 min
Timing
6mFive retrieval questions on Lessons 2.4 and 2.5 (workbook warm-up box)
4mReveal; a 30-second homework check by hands (both Session 5 projects: lean_features.txt and fraud_review_queue.csv; the k=200 noise rerun; one learner's own cost-ratio threshold); then one minute on how the checkpoint runs
Questions and answers
  • W1. RFECV's curve is 0.8328 at 6 columns and 0.8333 at 11; the folds disagree by about 0.02. How many columns do you keep, and why? Answer: 6: the difference is far smaller than the noise between folds, and the smaller set is cheaper and sturdier (the tolerance rule). Checkpoint 1 asks for honest comparisons like this one.
  • W2. Review $10, missed fraud $375. What does this print? (code on the slide) Answer: [0 1 1 1] 10 / 375 ≈ 0.027, so every probability from 0.04 up is flagged. Valid only for calibrated probabilities.
  • W3. Where must SMOTE sit when you cross-validate, and what happens otherwise? Answer: Inside an imblearn pipeline, after the preprocessing; resampling first put synthetic near-copies into the validation folds and reported AP 0.91 instead of about 0.49. Same rule as feature selection: a step that learns from y lives inside the folds.
  • W4. Why must an L1 model be scaled before it selects columns? Answer: The penalty charges for the size of each weight, so without scaling it keeps columns in large units and drops columns in small units, whatever their usefulness. Lesson 3.2 today: every penalty needs scaled features.
  • W5. A class-weighted model's probabilities average 0.27 when the real fraud rate is 0.009. What does that change? Answer: Its ranking is about the same, but the probabilities are no longer calibrated: a cost formula applied to them flags almost everything. Tune the threshold with cross-validation instead. Today's 3.1 shows where those probabilities come from: the sigmoid of the log-odds.
0:10

Checkpoint 1: Audit and Fix a Churn Model

30 min
The task (read it aloud; it is also in the workbook)

Pinecrest's marketing team will email a win-back voucher to the customers a churn model flags. An email with a voucher costs $5; a churner nobody reaches costs about $50 of margin. A colleague's notebook (Part A of labs/module-06/checkpoint-1/checkpoint1_starter.py) reports about 95% accuracy. Plan what you will check; audit the colleague's code (one line per problem); rebuild an honest model in a pipeline, cross-validated against a baseline with a metric suited to a rare class, and choose who gets the email from the prices, on the training rows only; look at the test set once; report three sentences for the marketing lead.

Timing
2mOpen checkpoint1_starter.py, run it once: the colleague's accuracy prints
3mPlan, written as comments above the code
5mAudit: one comment line per problem
15mRebuild: pipeline, CV against a baseline, the cost threshold, the test set once
5mReport: three sentences
How to run it
  • Say: "Thirty minutes, alone, open notebook: your Lesson 1.1 to 2.5 notebooks are the best reference. Not graded. Afterward you get the rubric and a model answer. Practice for the final project."
  • Put the four verbs and the end time on the board. Call out time at 0:15 ("you should be auditing"), 0:20 ("rebuild now"), 0:35 ("five minutes: write your three sentences, even if the code is unfinished").
  • Do not help with the audit. Do help with environment trouble (kernel, file not found) at once: that is not what is assessed.
  • Watch for the four habits in the rubric: the leak named, everything that learns inside a pipeline, CV against a baseline with AUC or AP, a threshold from the prices chosen without the test set.
  • At the end: "Keep your file. After the break we move on; the rubric and the model answer come at the wrap-up and in the answer key." Quick read later: the report first, then a pipeline, a baseline line and a threshold line in the code.
What a solid answer reaches (model answer, real run)

CV ROC AUC 0.832 against 0.500 for the dummy, CV AP 0.528 against 0.165; threshold 0.107 (formula 0.10); test ROC AUC 0.826, 654 emails reaching 214 of 248 churners for $4,970, against $7,500 emailing everyone, $9,630 at 0.5 and $12,400 emailing nobody. Full output, rubric and weak answers in the Answer Key.

Lesson Plans

3.1

Linear and Logistic Regression

60 min
Objectives (from the lesson page)
  • Explain what least squares does and fit LinearRegression, reading coef_ and intercept_ in the units of the data
  • Interpret a coefficient in a multiple regression as an effect "holding the other features constant", and spot when that reading breaks down
  • Use a log target with TransformedTargetRegressor when errors grow with the size of the prediction
  • Describe how logistic regression turns a weighted sum into a probability, and read its coefficients as log-odds and odds ratios
  • Fit a multiclass logistic regression and know which settings are current (C, l1_ratio) and which are gone
Timing in the room
8mFrame: the ruler on the scatter plot; least squares; from a line to a probability
22mLive demo: Steps 1 to 9 (Steps 2, 3 and 7 are one-minute steps)
20mGuided lab: daily_sales_line_starter.py (everyone), then fraud_odds_starter.py in pairs
10mStart the lesson project: Steps 1 to 3
Mental model to say

"Plot weekly snowshoe sales against temperature and lay a ruler across the cloud. Tilt it, slide it. Each dot sits some vertical distance from the ruler: its residual. Linear regression finds the one position where the squared residuals add up to the least. That is least squares."

Then the second picture, for yes or no: "Keep the weighted sum, but squash it through an S so it stays between 0 and 1. The sum before squashing is the log-odds; exp of a coefficient is an odds ratio." Write both formulas on the board and add the phrase every coefficient needs: holding the other features constant.

Live demo (you type, they follow)

New notebook lesson_3_1.

  1. A ruler on the Snow scatter (3m). "Snow gear, 2024 to learn from, 2025 to test: a forecast must not see the future." 
    import numpy as np
import pandas as pd
from sklearn.linear_model import LinearRegression
from sklearn.metrics import mean_absolute_error

demand = pd.read_csv("data/weekly_demand.csv")
train, test = demand[demand["year"] == 2024], demand[demand["year"] == 2025]
snow_train = train[train["category"] == "Snow"]
snow_test = test[test["category"] == "Snow"]

line = LinearRegression()
line.fit(snow_train[["avg_high_temp_f"]], snow_train["units_sold"])
print(f"slope {line.coef_[0]:.3f} units per °F, intercept {line.intercept_:.2f}")
print(f"test R2 {line.score(snow_test[['avg_high_temp_f']], snow_test['units_sold']):.3f}")
print(f"test MAE {mean_absolute_error(snow_test['units_sold'], line.predict(snow_test[['avg_high_temp_f']])):.2f} units")
    slope -0.761 units per °F, intercept 60.18
test R2 0.492
test MAE 8.64 units
    Read it in units: about 0.76 fewer snow items per extra °F. The intercept (60 units at 0 °F) is just where the line crosses the axis. Show the lesson's scatter chart.
  2. No magic in fit (2m). "For one feature, the slope is covariance over variance, and the line passes through the averages." 
    x, y = snow_train["avg_high_temp_f"], snow_train["units_sold"]
slope = np.cov(x, y)[0, 1] / x.var()
intercept = y.mean() - slope * x.mean()
print(f"by hand: slope {slope:.3f}, intercept {intercept:.2f}")
    by hand: slope -0.761, intercept 60.18
  3. Where the line goes (1m). "Predict 30, 60 and 85 °F." 
    print(line.predict(pd.DataFrame({"avg_high_temp_f": [30, 60, 85]})).round(1))
    [37.4 14.5 -4.5]
    Minus 4.5 snowshoes in summer: a straight line has no idea sales stop at zero.
  4. Many features, against two baselines (4m). "All 24 products: product and store one-hot, three numeric features, in a pipeline. First the baselines a planner would use without a model." 
    from sklearn.compose import ColumnTransformer
from sklearn.preprocessing import OneHotEncoder
from sklearn.pipeline import make_pipeline

y_train, y_test = train["units_sold"], test["units_sold"]
mean_mae = mean_absolute_error(y_test, np.full(len(y_test), y_train.mean()))
product_means = train.groupby("product_id")["units_sold"].mean()
product_mae = mean_absolute_error(y_test, test["product_id"].map(product_means))
print(f"baseline, overall mean    : MAE {mean_mae:.2f}")
print(f"baseline, product's mean  : MAE {product_mae:.2f}")

num_cols = ["promo", "holiday_week", "avg_high_temp_f"]
cat_cols = ["product_id", "store"]
encode = ColumnTransformer(
    [("num", "passthrough", num_cols),
     ("cat", OneHotEncoder(handle_unknown="ignore", sparse_output=False), cat_cols)]
).set_output(transform="pandas")

linear = make_pipeline(encode, LinearRegression())
linear.fit(train[num_cols + cat_cols], y_train)
pred = linear.predict(test[num_cols + cat_cols])
print(f"linear regression         : MAE {mean_absolute_error(y_test, pred):.2f}, "
      f"R2 {linear.score(test[num_cols + cat_cols], y_test):.3f}")
coefs = pd.Series(linear[-1].coef_, index=linear[-1].feature_names_in_)
print(coefs[["num__promo", "num__holiday_week", "num__avg_high_temp_f"]].round(3))
    baseline, overall mean    : MAE 17.13
baseline, product's mean  : MAE 13.42
linear regression         : MAE 11.77, R2 0.506
num__promo              14.003
num__holiday_week       13.997
num__avg_high_temp_f     0.191
dtype: float64
    11.8 against 13.4 and 17.1. Promo +14 units "holding the others constant": the same product, store and temperature. Then: "One temperature slope for every product: snow and tents averaged into +0.19 that fits nobody."
  5. The fan, and the fix (3m). "Show the residual plot on the lesson page: small sellers within a few units, big sellers off by dozens. Effects act in percent. Model the log." 
    from sklearn.compose import TransformedTargetRegressor

log_linear = TransformedTargetRegressor(
    regressor=make_pipeline(encode, LinearRegression()),
    func=np.log1p,
    inverse_func=np.expm1,
)
log_linear.fit(train[num_cols + cat_cols], y_train)
log_pred = log_linear.predict(test[num_cols + cat_cols])
print(f"log-target linear regression: MAE {mean_absolute_error(y_test, log_pred):.2f}")
print(f"negative predictions: {(log_pred < 0).sum()}")
log_coefs = pd.Series(log_linear.regressor_[-1].coef_, index=log_linear.regressor_[-1].feature_names_in_)
effects = log_coefs[["num__promo", "num__holiday_week", "num__avg_high_temp_f"]]
print(pd.DataFrame({"log_coef": effects, "pct_change": (np.exp(effects) - 1) * 100}).round(3))
    log-target linear regression: MAE 11.16
negative predictions: 0
                      log_coef  pct_change
num__promo               0.420      52.199
num__holiday_week        0.407      50.201
num__avg_high_temp_f     0.005       0.510
    MAE down, no negative predictions, and coefficients as percentages: promo +52% for every product. Write exp(coef) − 1 on the board.
  6. From a line to a probability (3m). "For yes or no, squash the weighted sum through the sigmoid. The sum is the log-odds." 
    from sklearn.model_selection import train_test_split
from sklearn.linear_model import LogisticRegression

customers = pd.read_csv("data/customers_churn.csv")
features = ["tenure_months", "orders_12m", "spend_12m", "avg_order_value", "days_since_last_order",
            "returns_12m", "return_rate", "support_tickets_12m", "discount_share", "app_user"]
Xc_train, Xc_test, yc_train, yc_test = train_test_split(
    customers[features], customers["churned"], test_size=0.25, stratify=customers["churned"], random_state=42
)
one = LogisticRegression().fit(Xc_train[["days_since_last_order"]], yc_train)
b0, b1 = one.intercept_[0], one.coef_[0, 0]
print(f"z = {b0:.3f} + {b1:.5f} * days")
days = pd.DataFrame({"days_since_last_order": [0, 30, 90, 150, 250, 364]})
days["p_churn"] = one.predict_proba(days)[:, 1].round(3)
days["by_hand"] = (1 / (1 + np.exp(-(b0 + b1 * days["days_since_last_order"])))).round(3)
print(days.to_string(index=False))
print(f"p crosses 0.5 at {-b0 / b1:.0f} days")
    z = -2.594 + 0.00928 * days
 days_since_last_order  p_churn  by_hand
                     0    0.070    0.070
                    30    0.090    0.090
                    90    0.147    0.147
                   150    0.231    0.231
                   250    0.432    0.432
                   364    0.687    0.687
p crosses 0.5 at 279 days
    By hand equals predict_proba. Crosses 0.5 only at 279 days, so predict calls few churners.
  7. What really happened (1m). "Actual churn in 30-day bands." 
    bands = pd.cut(Xc_train["days_since_last_order"], bins=[*range(0, 361, 30), 365], right=False)
actual = yc_train.groupby(bands, observed=True).mean()
print(actual.round(3).iloc[[0, 3, 4, 5, 6, 11]].to_string())
    days_since_last_order
[0, 30)       0.088
[90, 120)     0.125
[120, 150)    0.207
[150, 180)    0.295
[180, 210)    0.525
[330, 360)    0.567
    Flat, then a jump between 120 and 210 days. The S-curve can only bend gently: the straight-line-in-log-odds assumption, visible. (Lesson 2.3's 150-day flag is the fix.)
  8. Odds ratios (3m). "All ten features, scaled, so each coefficient is per standard deviation. exp(coef) is an odds ratio." 
    from sklearn.preprocessing import StandardScaler

churn_model = make_pipeline(StandardScaler(), LogisticRegression())
churn_model.fit(Xc_train, yc_train)
print(f"test accuracy {churn_model.score(Xc_test, yc_test):.3f}")
coef = pd.Series(churn_model[-1].coef_[0], index=features)
table = pd.DataFrame({"log_odds": coef, "odds_ratio": np.exp(coef)}).sort_values("log_odds")
print(table.round(2))
    test accuracy 0.848
                       log_odds  odds_ratio
app_user                  -0.34        0.71
orders_12m                -0.31        0.73
tenure_months             -0.12        0.88
spend_12m                 -0.12        0.89
returns_12m               -0.02        0.98
avg_order_value            0.00        1.00
return_rate                0.07        1.07
support_tickets_12m        0.40        1.49
discount_share             0.67        1.96
days_since_last_order      0.88        2.42
    One SD more days since the last order (about 87 days) multiplies the odds by 2.4; app users have 0.71 times the odds. Then the warning box: these describe the model, not causes; tenure's -0.12 hides a U.
  9. The 0.5 rule is z = 0 (2m). "decision_function returns z." 
    z = churn_model.decision_function(Xc_test[:3])
print("log-odds   :", z.round(3))
print("sigmoid(z) :", (1 / (1 + np.exp(-z))).round(3))
print("proba      :", churn_model.predict_proba(Xc_test[:3])[:, 1].round(3))
print("predict    :", churn_model.predict(Xc_test[:3]))
    log-odds   : [-2.691  0.118 -2.145]
sigmoid(z) : [0.063 0.53  0.105]
proba      : [0.063 0.53  0.105]
predict    : [0 1 0]
    Same three customers as Lesson 1.1. Moving the threshold (Lesson 2.5) is moving the line z = 0. If time allows, one sentence on multi_class being gone (TypeError: LogisticRegression.__init__() got an unexpected keyword argument 'multi_class').
Three mistakes to expect, and how to coach each
  • Reading a coefficient without its units or its company. "Promo is 70 times more important than temperature" (14.0 against 0.19), or "price barely matters" from the naive -0.53. Coach: "Per what? Across the feature's range? Holding which other columns constant?"
  • Probability and log-odds mixed up. "0.88 adds 0.88 to the churn probability." Coach: "Which scale is z on? What is exp(0.88)?" Have them compute one sigmoid by hand.
  • Coefficients read as causes, or twins trusted. "Make customers install the app and churn drops 30%"; a promo coefficient that looks absurd (Lab A). Coach: "Did anyone change the app flag and watch?" and "Is this column a copy of another?"
Guided lab
  • Lab A (12m, everyone): labs/module-06/l3_1-linear-logistic/daily_sales_line_starter.py; reference labs/module-06/l3_1-linear-logistic/daily_sales_line_solution.py. Daily store sales: two baselines, Boise's growth line by hand, a multi-feature model with an Asheville Monday in January as the reference, a twin column, a log target.
  • Expected, Lab A: baselines $1,670 and $1,292; Boise +$1,333 a year from $6,037 (hand and sklearn agree); linear MAE $949, Saturday +$3,509, Tuesday -$168, trend +$487 a year, promo +$2,625, Black Friday +$2,083; promo is 1 exactly on sale and holiday-rush days, and dropping it changes no prediction; log target MAE $853, Saturday +74.4%, Tuesday -5.3%, trend +8.1% a year, Black Friday +82.1%.
  • Debrief Lab A: "Promo and the event columns are twins: the split between them is arbitrary, the predictions are not. And a log target turns Saturdays into a percentage that holds for every store."
  • Lab B (8m, pairs): labs/module-06/l3_1-linear-logistic/fraud_odds_starter.py; reference labs/module-06/l3_1-linear-logistic/fraud_odds_solution.py. Odds ratios in an analyst's units (per flag, per $100), the sigmoid by hand, and hour as a line against a night flag.
  • Expected, Lab B: z = -5.984 + 1.515 × velocity (odds ratio 4.55 per extra payment); odds ratios device_new 7.11, night 6.93, billing mismatch 3.91, country mismatch 8.94, per $100 1.31, per card attempt 6.31; hour -0.111 (0.90) against the night flag +2.354 (10.53); predict equals z > 0; 21 flagged at 0.5, 18 fraud; test AP 0.332.
Start the lesson project

The lesson project asks what a discount really sells: a log-log price elasticity that changes as the model compares like with like. In the room, Steps 1 to 3 (10 minutes).

  • Step 1 together (log columns, crossed categories). Step 2: naive elasticity: -0.53. Step 3: within-product elasticity: -1.50. Ask: "Why did it triple?"
  • Goes home: Step 4 (season, store, promo, trend: -1.606, a 10% cut sells +18.4%), Step 5 (2024 only, test 2025: MAE 8.27 against 13.42) and Step 6 (the tent what-if: +45% units, +16% revenue).
Checks for understanding
  • What does ŷ = 60.18 - 0.761 × temp predict at 85 °F, and what does that tell you? Answer: -4.5 units: lines extrapolate without limits.
  • Log target coefficient 0.42: in words? Answer: about 52% more, for every product.
  • decision_function returns 0: probability and prediction? Answer: 0.5, exactly on the boundary.

Workbook self-check, items Q1 to Q6.

Answers: Q1 [35.]; Q2 B) A promo week sells about 52% more, for every product, whatever its size; Q3 D) A new device multiplies the odds of fraud by about 2, holding the other features constant; Q4 A) The naive slope compared different products (cheap socks sell more units than tents), so price stood in for product type; Q5 0.25; Q6 C) No error: every distinct count becomes its own class, which is wrong and slow. Full set with explanations in the Answer Key.

Differentiation

Support: in Lab A, do TODO 1 and 2 together on the projector; give pairs the coefficient-printing line. Stretch: the lesson's Exercise 4 (a 150-day flag helps the line) and Bonus Challenge (an elasticity per category).

3.2

Regularization and the Bias-Variance Trade-off

60 min
Objectives (from the lesson page)
  • Explain underfitting and overfitting in terms of bias and variance, and recognize each from training and test errors
  • Add polynomial and interaction features with PolynomialFeatures and predict how many columns they create
  • Fit Ridge, Lasso and ElasticNet in a pipeline with scaling, and describe how their penalties differ
  • Choose the penalty strength alpha by cross-validation on the training data only, with RidgeCV and LassoCV
  • Build a regularized demand forecast with interactions and judge it against baselines on a later year
Timing in the room
8mFrame: three students and a practice exam; the flexibility dial
22mLive demo: Steps 1 to 10
20mGuided lab: churn_curves_starter.py (everyone), then variance_without_truth_starter.py in pairs
10mStart the lesson project: Steps 1 and 2
Mental model to say

"Three students prepare with last year's practice exam. One learns a single rule, 'demand is about 25', and is always a bit wrong: high bias. One memorizes every answer, typos included: perfect on the practice exam, lost on the real one, and a different practice exam would have made a different student: high variance. The third learns the patterns and ignores details too specific to trust. Regularization turns the memorizer into the third student: learn many things, but pay for every strong opinion."

Write expected error = bias² + variance + noise and draw the U. "Every model in this course has a dial like this: tree depth, k in kNN next week, boosting rounds. Today's dial is alpha."

Live demo (you type, they follow)

New notebook lesson_3_2.

  1. Powers and products (2m). "A linear model can only add columns, so give it new ones." 
    import numpy as np
import pandas as pd
from sklearn.preprocessing import PolynomialFeatures

tiny = pd.DataFrame({"a": [1, 2, 3], "b": [10, 20, 30]})
poly = PolynomialFeatures(degree=2, include_bias=False).set_output(transform="pandas")
print(poly.fit_transform(tiny))
         a     b  a^2   a b    b^2
0  1.0  10.0  1.0  10.0  100.0
1  2.0  20.0  4.0  40.0  400.0
2  3.0  30.0  9.0  90.0  900.0
    Squares and an interaction. Still linear in its weights.
  2. A curve where we know the truth (1m). "A sine wave plus noise with SD 0.3: even a perfect model has MSE about 0.09." 
    rng = np.random.default_rng(42)

def true_curve(x):
    return np.sin(2 * np.pi * x)

def make_sample(n=25):
    x = rng.uniform(0, 1, n)
    return x.reshape(-1, 1), true_curve(x) + rng.normal(0, 0.3, n)

X_small, y_small = make_sample()
X_new, y_new = make_sample(500)
print(X_small.shape, X_new.shape)
    (25, 1) (500, 1)
  3. Too simple, too flexible (3m). "Predict which degree has the best TEST error." 
    from sklearn.linear_model import LinearRegression
from sklearn.metrics import mean_squared_error
from sklearn.pipeline import make_pipeline
from sklearn.preprocessing import StandardScaler

def poly_model(degree, regressor=None):
    if regressor is None:
        regressor = LinearRegression()
    return make_pipeline(PolynomialFeatures(degree, include_bias=False), StandardScaler(), regressor)

for degree in [1, 4, 15]:
    m = poly_model(degree).fit(X_small, y_small)
    train_mse = mean_squared_error(y_small, m.predict(X_small))
    test_mse = mean_squared_error(y_new, m.predict(X_new))
    print(f"degree {degree:2d}   train MSE {train_mse:.3f}   test MSE {test_mse:.3f}")
    degree  1   train MSE 0.331   test MSE 0.283
degree  4   train MSE 0.025   test MSE 0.133
degree 15   train MSE 0.016   test MSE 0.187
    Degree 15 has the lowest training error, below the noise floor, and a worse test error than degree 4. Write the diagnostic: high/high = too simple; low/much higher = too flexible.
  4. Bias and variance, measured (3m). "Refit each degree on 100 fresh samples. Show the lesson's three-panel chart of 20 curves while it runs." 
    samples = [make_sample() for _ in range(100)]
x_eval = np.linspace(0.05, 0.95, 50).reshape(-1, 1)

def bias_variance(make_model):
    preds = np.array([make_model().fit(X_s, y_s).predict(x_eval) for X_s, y_s in samples])
    bias_sq = ((preds.mean(axis=0) - true_curve(x_eval.ravel())) ** 2).mean()
    variance = preds.var(axis=0).mean()
    return bias_sq, variance

rows = []
for degree in [1, 3, 4, 8, 15]:
    bias_sq, variance = bias_variance(lambda: poly_model(degree))
    rows.append({"degree": degree, "bias^2": bias_sq, "variance": variance, "expected MSE": bias_sq + variance + 0.09})
print(pd.DataFrame(rows).set_index("degree").round(3))
            bias^2  variance  expected MSE
degree                                
1        0.155     0.026         0.271
3        0.004     0.015         0.108
4        0.003     0.024         0.117
8        0.055     4.050         4.194
15       6.780   307.451       314.321
    Bias falls, variance explodes. Expected error = bias² + variance + noise. "You can only do this when you know the truth." (Lab B shows what you CAN measure without it.)
  5. Ridge tames the wild one (2m). "Same 15 degrees, same 25 points, a penalty on big weights." 
    from sklearn.linear_model import Ridge

for name, regressor in [("least squares", LinearRegression()), ("ridge alpha=0.1", Ridge(alpha=0.1))]:
    m = poly_model(15, regressor).fit(X_small, y_small)
    weights = m[-1].coef_
    test_mse = mean_squared_error(y_new, m.predict(X_new))
    print(f"{name:16s} largest |weight| {np.abs(weights).max():9.1f}   test MSE {test_mse:.3f}")
    least squares    largest |weight|    1044.5   test MSE 0.187
ridge alpha=0.1  largest |weight|       1.4   test MSE 0.132
    Largest weight from about 1,000 to 1.4; test error at degree-4 level. The rubber-band picture.
  6. Real data: the season depends on the category (2m). "Pinecrest's weekly demand." 
    demand = pd.read_csv("data/weekly_demand.csv")
demand["month"] = pd.to_datetime(demand["week_start"]).dt.month
train = demand[demand["year"] == 2024]
test = demand[demand["year"] == 2025]
print(train.shape, test.shape)
print(demand.groupby(["category", "month"])["units_sold"].mean().unstack().loc[["Snow", "Tents", "Apparel"], [1, 4, 7, 10, 12]].round(1))
    (5088, 16) (4896, 16)
month       1     4     7     10    12
category                              
Snow      32.6   2.7   0.3  10.1  41.5
Tents      5.1  16.9  32.4  10.2   9.2
Apparel   26.3  19.3  15.6  27.7  41.7
    Snow in July is 0.3. One weight for July and one for Snow cannot say "Snow in July".
  7. 489 columns (2m). "Products of pairs of numeric, category, store and month." 
    from sklearn.compose import ColumnTransformer, TransformedTargetRegressor
from sklearn.preprocessing import OneHotEncoder

numeric = ["price", "discount_pct", "promo", "holiday_week", "avg_high_temp_f"]
grouping = ["category", "store", "month"]
columns = numeric + grouping + ["product_id"]
interactions = make_pipeline(
    ColumnTransformer([
        ("num", StandardScaler(), numeric),
        ("cat", OneHotEncoder(sparse_output=False, handle_unknown="ignore"), grouping),
    ]),
    PolynomialFeatures(degree=2, interaction_only=True, include_bias=False),
)
features = ColumnTransformer([
    ("product", OneHotEncoder(sparse_output=False, handle_unknown="ignore"), ["product_id"]),
    ("inter", interactions, numeric + grouping),
])
print(features.fit(train[columns]).transform(train[columns].head()).shape)
    (5, 489)
  8. A 400-row pilot (3m). "About as many rows as useful columns. Predict the largest forecast of least squares." 
    from sklearn.metrics import mean_absolute_error

def demand_model(regressor):
    return TransformedTargetRegressor(make_pipeline(features, regressor), func=np.log1p, inverse_func=np.expm1)

pilot = train.sample(400, random_state=42)
for name, regressor in [("least squares", LinearRegression()), ("ridge alpha=1", Ridge(alpha=1)),
                        ("ridge alpha=10", Ridge(alpha=10)), ("ridge alpha=100", Ridge(alpha=100))]:
    m = demand_model(regressor).fit(pilot[columns], pilot["units_sold"])
    pred = m.predict(test[columns])
    print(f"{name:16s} train MAE {mean_absolute_error(pilot['units_sold'], m.predict(pilot[columns])):5.2f}"
          f"   test MAE {mean_absolute_error(test['units_sold'], pred):6.2f}   largest forecast {pred.max():8.0f}")
    least squares    train MAE  3.43   test MAE  42.17   largest forecast    15297
ridge alpha=1    train MAE  4.16   test MAE  11.97   largest forecast      315
ridge alpha=10   train MAE  5.58   test MAE  10.00   largest forecast      181
ridge alpha=100  train MAE  9.03   test MAE  12.43   largest forecast      135
    15,297 units for a product that never sold more than a few hundred: variance. alpha = 10 cuts the test error by three quarters; alpha = 100 underfits again.
  9. Lasso, and twins (3m). "Lasso charges absolute size and sets weights to exactly zero." 
    from sklearn.linear_model import Lasso

for alpha in [0.003, 0.01, 0.03]:
    m = demand_model(Lasso(alpha=alpha, max_iter=20_000)).fit(pilot[columns], pilot["units_sold"])
    kept = (m.regressor_[-1].coef_ != 0).sum()
    print(f"lasso alpha={alpha:<6} weights kept {kept:3d} of 489   test MAE {mean_absolute_error(test['units_sold'], m.predict(test[columns])):5.2f}")

from sklearn.linear_model import ElasticNet

snow = train[train["category"] == "Snow"]
twins = snow[["avg_high_temp_f"]].assign(avg_high_temp_c=(snow["avg_high_temp_f"] - 32) * 5 / 9)
y_snow = np.log1p(snow["units_sold"])
for name, regressor in [("ridge", Ridge(alpha=1)), ("lasso", Lasso(alpha=0.05)),
                        ("elastic net", ElasticNet(alpha=0.05, l1_ratio=0.5))]:
    m = make_pipeline(StandardScaler(), regressor).fit(twins, y_snow)
    print(f"{name:12s} weights (F, C): {m[-1].coef_.round(3)}")
    lasso alpha=0.003  weights kept 120 of 489   test MAE  9.57
lasso alpha=0.01   weights kept  52 of 489   test MAE  9.92
lasso alpha=0.03   weights kept  19 of 489   test MAE 11.98
ridge        weights (F, C): [-0.607 -0.607]
lasso        weights (F, C): [-1.164 -0.   ]
elastic net  weights (F, C): [-0.589 -0.586]
    Fewer columns, similar error. Then the twins: Lasso keeps one at random. "Never read 'Lasso dropped it' as 'it does not matter'."
  10. Choose alpha by cross-validation (1m). "Choosing by the test score made the test set training data." 
    from sklearn.linear_model import LassoCV, RidgeCV
from sklearn.model_selection import KFold

cv = KFold(n_splits=5, shuffle=True, random_state=42)
searches = {
    "RidgeCV": RidgeCV(alphas=np.logspace(-1, 3, 9), cv=cv),
    "LassoCV": LassoCV(alphas=np.logspace(-3.5, -1, 11), cv=cv, max_iter=20_000),
}
for name, regressor in searches.items():
    m = demand_model(regressor).fit(pilot[columns], pilot["units_sold"])
    chosen = m.regressor_[-1]
    mae = mean_absolute_error(test["units_sold"], m.predict(test[columns]))
    print(f"{name:8s} chose alpha={chosen.alpha_:.4f}   weights kept {(chosen.coef_ != 0).sum():3d}   test MAE {mae:.2f}")
    RidgeCV  chose alpha=3.1623   weights kept 377   test MAE 10.35
LassoCV  chose alpha=0.0056   weights kept  83   test MAE 9.50
    Close to the best values seen by peeking, without touching 2025.
Three mistakes to expect, and how to coach each
  • Judging flexibility by training error. "Degree 15 is best, lowest error." Coach: "On which rows?" Point at the diagnostic on the board.
  • A penalty on unscaled features. Results change with units (dollars against thousands). Coach: "What does the penalty add up? Is a weight of 3.8 per $1,000 the same as 0.0038 per dollar?"
  • Choosing alpha with the test set, or C read the wrong way. Coach: "Which rows chose alpha?" and, for logistic regression, "C is the inverse: small C, strong penalty."
Guided lab
  • Lab A (12m, everyone): labs/module-06/l3_2-regularization/churn_curves_starter.py; reference labs/module-06/l3_2-regularization/churn_curves_solution.py. The same ideas for a classifier: polynomial churn features on a 1,000-customer pilot, train against CV AUC, a validation curve over C, then all 4,500 customers, then one test.
  • Expected, Lab A: pilot train AUC 0.823 / 0.899 / 1.000 and CV AUC 0.807 / 0.807 / 0.706 for degrees 1 to 3; degree 3 by C: CV AUC 0.808, 0.810, 0.809, 0.776, 0.730 for C = 0.001 to 10; on 4,500 customers degree 2 reaches 0.851 with or without a penalty while degree 3 needs C = 0.1 (0.851 against 0.827); the chosen degree-2 model scores test AUC 0.853 against 0.823 for the plain model.
  • Debrief Lab A: "Real interactions exist in churn (degree 2 wins), but a pilot cannot afford them without a penalty. More data is also a regularizer."
  • Lab B (8m, pairs): labs/module-06/l3_2-regularization/variance_without_truth_starter.py; reference labs/module-06/l3_2-regularization/variance_without_truth_solution.py. Variance needs no truth: refit on 30 pilot samples of daily sales and measure how much the same day's forecast moves.
  • Expected, Lab B: 406 columns; spread / mean MAE over 30 pilots of 200 rows: least squares $1,217 / $1,351, alpha 1 $526 / $1,089, alpha 10 $275 / $1,122, alpha 100 $167 / $1,527; 500-row pilots: spread $486 (least squares) and $384 (alpha 1).
  • Debrief Lab B: "The spread falls steadily with alpha (variance); the error falls, then rises (bias returns). You measured one half of the U on real data."
Start the lesson project

The lesson project is the interaction forecast for 2025. In the room, Steps 1 and 2 (10 minutes).

  • Step 1: baselines 17.13 (overall mean) and 13.42 (product mean). Step 2: ridge, main effects MAE 10.62.
  • Goes home: Step 3 (489 columns), Step 4 (RidgeCV chooses alpha 1.0), Step 5 (2025 MAE 7.92, R² 0.754) and Step 6 (the six strongest interactions all involve Snow; demand_forecast_2025.csv, 4,896 rows).
Checks for understanding
  • Training MSE 0.016, test 0.187, noise floor 0.09: which problem? Answer: high variance (overfitting).
  • What happens to Ridge weights as alpha grows very large? Answer: all shrink toward zero; predictions approach the mean.
  • Why choose alpha with RidgeCV and not with the 2025 MAE? Answer: choosing with the test set makes it training data; the final number becomes optimistic.

Workbook self-check, items Q7 to Q12.

Answers: Q7 C) Increase the Ridge penalty (alpha), chosen by cross-validation; Q8 14; Q9 A) Ridge shares the effect between the twins; Lasso keeps one twin and zeros the other, somewhat arbitrarily; Q10 D) In thousands the price needs a weight 1,000 times larger, and the penalty shrinks it hard; Q11 [0.] 13.0; Q12 B) More rows reduce variance on their own, so less penalty is needed to control it. Full set with explanations in the Answer Key.

Differentiation

Support: in Lab A give the cross_validate call for TODO 1 and let pairs change only the degree. Stretch: the lesson's Exercise 4 (more data or more penalty?) and the rolling-origin Bonus Challenge.

Closing

Exit ticket (3 questions, no notes, 4 minutes)
  1. A churn model gives discount_share (scaled) a coefficient of 0.67. Say what it means in one sentence, and one thing it does not mean. Answer: one standard deviation more discount reliance multiplies the odds of churning by exp(0.67) ≈ 1.96, other features held constant; it does not mean discounts cause churn.
  2. Training error tiny, test error large: name the problem and two fixes. Answer: high variance; more penalty (alpha chosen by CV), fewer columns or more data.
  3. Why must features be scaled before Ridge or Lasso? Answer: the penalty charges for weight size, and units decide how large a weight must be.

  • Both Quick Quizzes and both journal entries.
  • Checkpoint 1: if unfinished, finish it at home, then compare with the rubric (handed out at the wrap-up). Bring one question about it to Session 7.
  • Before the Next Lesson: 3.1: count the project model's one-hot columns; refit churn with C=0.01. 3.2: rerun Step 4 with np.logspace(-2, 3, 11); define bias and variance in one sentence each.
  • Labs not finished in class: any TODO of the four labs.

Instructor Kit · Facilitator Guide

Session 7: Nearest Neighbors and Naive Bayes

How to teach the seventh session of Machine Learning Foundations: k-nearest neighbors (distance as similarity, why scaling decides everything, the k dial, lookalikes as explanations, the curse of dimensionality) and Naive Bayes (Bayes' rule from counts, bag of words, smoothing, the variants, and a model that ranks well while its probabilities are too confident).

2 lessons 3 hrs session Lessons 4.1–4.2 Session 7 of 11

How to Use This Guide

  • Same rhythm for both lessons: frame → live demo → guided lab → start the project. Exercises, the quiz and the journal are homework.
  • The two lessons are opposites worth contrasting out loud: kNN has no weights and lives on distances (scaling is everything, more columns hurt); Naive Bayes counts and multiplies (no scaling, thrives on thousands of word columns, but double-counts duplicates).
  • Every code block below was run to produce the output printed under it; plotting lines are left out. Show the decision maps (4.1) and the bell-curve and calibration charts (4.2) from classification_knn.html and classification_naive_bayes.html.
  • Labs live in labs/module-07/. Starters are handouts; solutions are yours.
The #1 risk of Session 7: kNN's lookalikes and Naive Bayes' probabilities taken at face value

Both models produce something that looks like an explanation or a confidence: "these 15 customers" and "99% sure". Learners leave trusting both. Protect two moments: the noise-columns step (4.1 Step 10: lookalikes are only as good as the distance) and the overconfidence check (4.2 Step 10: above 90% sure, 61% churned).

Before the Session

  • ☐ Run the kit check: python3 labs/check_labs.py module-07. Expect 8 passed, 0 failed (debug_knn.py is not run), about a minute.
  • ☐ Copy the starters (labs/module-07/l4_1-knn/*_starter.py, debug_knn.py, labs/module-07/l4_2-naive-bayes/*_starter.py) to the shared folder; keep the solutions.
  • ☐ Downloads: none; data/reviews.csv and data/products_ml.csv are in the data folder.
  • ☐ Projector: notebooks lesson_4_1 and lesson_4_2; lesson tabs scrolled to the decision maps, the bell curves and the calibration chart. The 4.1 CV loop takes about 10 seconds: start it, then talk.
  • ☐ Board: the comps picture with two questions (what counts as similar? how many comps?); Bayes' rule P(class | clue) = P(clue | class) × P(class) / P(clue).
  • ☐ Checkpoint 1 feedback: read a few checkpoint reports; pick the two most common rubric gaps for the warm-up.
Known environment traps
SymptomCauseFix
ValueError: Expected n_neighbors <= n_samples_fit, ...k larger than the rows (a tiny subset, a small fold)Keep k well below the smallest training fold.
ValueError: Input X contains NaN.email_opens_90d has gaps; kNN cannot measure a distance to a gapSimpleImputer in the pipeline.
ValueError: Negative values in data passed to MultinomialNB (input X).A scaler in front of a count modelNo scaler for Multinomial/Complement/Bernoulli NB; GaussianNB for continuous features.
TypeError: Sparse data was passed for X, but dense data is required.CountVectorizer output into GaussianNBUse MultinomialNB for text.
kNN predictions take a few secondsEvery prediction searches all stored rowsExpected: kNN trains instantly and predicts slowly.
