Opening Segment

0:00

Warm-up: Linear Models and Regularization

10 min
Timing
6mFive retrieval questions on Lessons 3.1 and 3.2
4mReveal; two minutes on the two most common Checkpoint 1 gaps in the reports you read (likely: preprocessing fitted before the split, and a threshold chosen on the test set), answering one or two of the checkpoint questions learners were asked to bring
Questions and answers
  • W1. A log-target demand model gives holiday_week a coefficient of 0.405. In words? Answer: A holiday week sells about exp(0.405) − 1 ≈ 50% more, for every product. Percent effects come back today as likelihood ratios that multiply.
  • W2. A logistic regression flag has coefficient 0.69. What does this print, and what does it mean? (code on the slide) Answer: 1.99 The odds ratio: the flag about doubles the odds. Naive Bayes today also multiplies odds, one clue at a time.
  • W3. Degree 15 has training MSE 0.016 and test MSE 0.187 (noise floor 0.09). Which problem, and which dial helps? Answer: High variance (overfitting); a penalty such as Ridge's alpha, fewer features or more data. Today's dial is k: k = 1 is the degree-15 model of nearest neighbors.
  • W4. Temperature in °F and °C: Ridge gives [-0.607, -0.607], Lasso [-1.164, 0]. What does Lasso's zero mean? Answer: Only that the column was redundant given its twin, not that it has no effect. Naive Bayes has the opposite problem with twins: it counts both.
  • W5. Why must features be scaled before Ridge or Lasso? Answer: The penalty charges for weight size, and a feature's units decide how big its weight must be. For kNN scaling is even more basic: it defines what "similar" means.

Lesson Plans

4.1

k-Nearest Neighbors

75 min
Objectives (from the lesson page)
  • Explain how k-nearest neighbors classifies a row, and compute Euclidean and Manhattan distances by hand
  • Show why unscaled features wreck distances, and fix it with a scaler inside a pipeline
  • Choose k with cross-validation and connect small and large k to variance and bias
  • Recognize the curse of dimensionality and why irrelevant features hurt kNN more than a linear model
  • Use kneighbors to show the lookalike customers behind a prediction
Timing in the room
10mFrame: the real-estate agent's comps; distance and k
30mLive demo: Steps 1 to 10
20mGuided lab: fraud_neighbors_starter.py (everyone), then lookalike_days_starter.py in pairs
15mStart the lesson project: Steps 1 to 3
Mental model to say

"Ask an experienced real-estate agent what a house will sell for. No formula: they pull up the comps, the five or six recently sold homes most like this one, and average their prices. That is kNN. Two questions decide whether the estimate is any good: what counts as similar (is one extra bedroom a bigger difference than ten years of age?) and how many comps (one is a gamble; fifty drag in houses that are not alike)."

Write the two dials on the board: distance (which columns, what scale) and k (variance at 1, bias at 500). "Nothing is learned in advance: fit stores the table, predict does the work. A lazy learner."

Live demo (you type, they follow)

New notebook lesson_4_1.

  1. The churn split again (1m). "Ten numeric features from Lesson 1.1, the canonical split." 
    import numpy as np
import pandas as pd
from sklearn.model_selection import train_test_split

customers = pd.read_csv("data/customers_churn.csv")
features = ["tenure_months", "orders_12m", "spend_12m", "avg_order_value", "days_since_last_order",
            "returns_12m", "return_rate", "support_tickets_12m", "discount_share", "app_user"]
X = customers[features]
y = customers["churned"]
X_train, X_test, y_train, y_test = train_test_split(X, y, test_size=0.25, stratify=y, random_state=42)
print(X_train.shape, X_test.shape)
    (4500, 10) (1500, 10)
  2. A distance by hand (3m). "Subtract, square, add, square root. Then ask where the distance comes from." 
    a, b = X_train.iloc[0], X_train.iloc[1]
diff = a - b
parts = pd.DataFrame({"customer A": a, "customer B": b, "squared diff": diff ** 2})
parts["share of total"] = parts["squared diff"] / parts["squared diff"].sum()
print(parts.round(3).iloc[[0, 1, 2, 3, 9]])
print(f"Euclidean distance: {np.sqrt((diff ** 2).sum()):.2f}")
                     customer A  customer B  squared diff  share of total
tenure_months          1.00       44.00      1849.000           0.026
orders_12m             2.00       10.00        64.000           0.001
spend_12m            630.30      578.82      2650.190           0.037
avg_order_value      315.15       57.88     66187.853           0.935
app_user               0.00        1.00         1.000           0.000
Euclidean distance: 266.00
    94% from average order value. A brand-new customer with 2 orders and a 44-month app user with 10 count as close, because they spend about the same per order.
  3. Scaled, every column has a say (2m). "After StandardScaler one standard deviation counts the same in every column." 
    from sklearn.preprocessing import StandardScaler

scaler = StandardScaler().set_output(transform="pandas").fit(X_train)
a_s, b_s = scaler.transform(X_train.iloc[[0, 1]]).to_numpy()
share = pd.Series((a_s - b_s) ** 2, index=features)
print((share / share.sum()).round(3).sort_values(ascending=False).head(4))
print(f"Euclidean distance (scaled): {np.sqrt(((a_s - b_s) ** 2).sum()):.2f}")
    avg_order_value    0.306
orders_12m         0.301
app_user           0.205
tenure_months      0.097
dtype: float64
Euclidean distance (scaled): 4.52
  4. Does it matter for predictions? (3m). "Predict the AUC gap." 
    from sklearn.metrics import roc_auc_score
from sklearn.neighbors import KNeighborsClassifier
from sklearn.pipeline import make_pipeline

for name, model in [("unscaled", KNeighborsClassifier(n_neighbors=15)),
                    ("scaled", make_pipeline(StandardScaler(), KNeighborsClassifier(n_neighbors=15)))]:
    model.fit(X_train, y_train)
    auc = roc_auc_score(y_test, model.predict_proba(X_test)[:, 1])
    print(f"{name:9s} accuracy {model.score(X_test, y_test):.3f}   ROC AUC {auc:.3f}")
    unscaled  accuracy 0.851   ROC AUC 0.774
scaled    accuracy 0.858   ROC AUC 0.836
    0.774 against 0.836, same algorithm, same k. Accuracy barely moves: Lesson 1.4 again.
  5. Probabilities are vote shares (2m). "With 15 neighbors there are only 16 possible probabilities." 
    knn = make_pipeline(StandardScaler(), KNeighborsClassifier(n_neighbors=15)).fit(X_train, y_train)
proba = knn.predict_proba(X_test)[:, 1]
print("first five churn probabilities:", proba[:5].round(3))
print("as counts out of 15:", (proba[:5] * 15).round().astype(int))
print("distinct probability values:", np.unique(proba).size)
    first five churn probabilities: [0.133 0.467 0.133 0.067 0.133]
as counts out of 15: [2 7 2 1 2]
distinct probability values: 15
  6. Ask for the lookalikes (4m). "kneighbors lives on the classifier, so transform the customer with the pipeline's scaler first." 
    riskiest = int(np.argmax(proba))
customer = X_test.iloc[[riskiest]]
distances, positions = knn[-1].kneighbors(knn[:-1].transform(customer))
lookalikes = X_train.iloc[positions[0]].assign(churned=y_train.iloc[positions[0]].to_numpy(),
                                              distance=distances[0].round(2))
print(customer[["tenure_months", "days_since_last_order", "discount_share", "app_user"]])
print(lookalikes[["days_since_last_order", "discount_share", "app_user", "churned", "distance"]].head(4))
print(f"lookalikes who churned: {lookalikes['churned'].sum()} of 15 -> probability {proba[riskiest]:.3f}")
          tenure_months  days_since_last_order  discount_share  app_user
4050             65                    364           0.432         0
      days_since_last_order  discount_share  app_user  churned  distance
4799                    364           0.231         0        1      1.70
4997                    220           0.484         0        1      2.00
2361                    254           0.558         0        1      2.09
2385                    364           0.110         0        1      2.10
lookalikes who churned: 14 of 15 -> probability 0.933
    14 of 15 churned: a long gap and no app. "These 15 customers looked like her" is an explanation a retention agent understands. Privacy caveat: in a real system summarize, do not print other customers.
  7. The k dial (4m). "Watch training and test AUC." 
    for k in [1, 5, 15, 51, 151, 501]:
    m = make_pipeline(StandardScaler(), KNeighborsClassifier(n_neighbors=k)).fit(X_train, y_train)
    train_auc = roc_auc_score(y_train, m.predict_proba(X_train)[:, 1])
    test_auc = roc_auc_score(y_test, m.predict_proba(X_test)[:, 1])
    print(f"k={k:3d}   train AUC {train_auc:.3f}   test AUC {test_auc:.3f}")
    k=  1   train AUC 1.000   test AUC 0.645
k=  5   train AUC 0.932   test AUC 0.789
k= 15   train AUC 0.893   test AUC 0.836
k= 51   train AUC 0.865   test AUC 0.842
k=151   train AUC 0.848   test AUC 0.837
k=501   train AUC 0.833   test AUC 0.823
    k = 1: training AUC 1.000 because every customer is its own nearest neighbor. Show the lesson's three decision maps (k = 1, 25, 301): islands, the 150-day cliff, broad strokes.
  8. Choose k by cross-validation (4m). "Training rows only, as with alpha." 
    from sklearn.model_selection import StratifiedKFold, cross_val_score

cv = StratifiedKFold(n_splits=5, shuffle=True, random_state=42)
for k in [5, 15, 25, 51, 101, 151]:
    scores = cross_val_score(make_pipeline(StandardScaler(), KNeighborsClassifier(n_neighbors=k)),
                             X_train, y_train, cv=cv, scoring="roc_auc")
    print(f"k={k:3d}   CV AUC {scores.mean():.3f} ± {scores.std():.3f}")
    k=  5   CV AUC 0.778 ± 0.012
k= 15   CV AUC 0.826 ± 0.016
k= 25   CV AUC 0.834 ± 0.023
k= 51   CV AUC 0.839 ± 0.018
k=101   CV AUC 0.838 ± 0.021
k=151   CV AUC 0.835 ± 0.021
    A flat plateau from 25 to 151: choose a range, not a magic number. Tuned kNN beats logistic regression's 0.823 on these columns, because it can follow the 150-day cliff.
  9. The curse of dimensionality (3m). "Random points: nearest against farthest distance." 
    from sklearn.metrics import pairwise_distances

rng = np.random.default_rng(42)
for n_dims in [2, 10, 100, 1000]:
    points = rng.uniform(size=(1000, n_dims))
    d = pairwise_distances(points[:1], points[1:])[0]
    print(f"{n_dims:4d} dimensions: nearest / farthest distance = {d.min() / d.max():.3f}")
       2 dimensions: nearest / farthest distance = 0.010
  10 dimensions: nearest / farthest distance = 0.317
 100 dimensions: nearest / farthest distance = 0.715
1000 dimensions: nearest / farthest distance = 0.890
    In 1,000 dimensions the nearest point is only 10% closer than the farthest: "nearest" stops meaning "similar".
  10. Noise hurts kNN, not the weighted model (4m). "Add useless columns to both." 
    from sklearn.linear_model import LogisticRegression

for n_noise in [0, 5, 20, 50]:
    noise = pd.DataFrame(rng.normal(size=(len(X), n_noise)), index=X.index).add_prefix("noise_")
    X_noisy = pd.concat([X, noise], axis=1)
    Xn_train, Xn_test = X_noisy.loc[X_train.index], X_noisy.loc[X_test.index]
    aucs = []
    for clf in [KNeighborsClassifier(n_neighbors=51), LogisticRegression()]:
        m = make_pipeline(StandardScaler(), clf).fit(Xn_train, y_train)
        aucs.append(roc_auc_score(y_test, m.predict_proba(Xn_test)[:, 1]))
    print(f"{n_noise:2d} noise columns   kNN AUC {aucs[0]:.3f}   logistic AUC {aucs[1]:.3f}")
     0 noise columns   kNN AUC 0.842   logistic AUC 0.823
 5 noise columns   kNN AUC 0.823   logistic AUC 0.824
20 noise columns   kNN AUC 0.790   logistic AUC 0.820
50 noise columns   kNN AUC 0.755   logistic AUC 0.816
    Logistic regression gives noise near-zero weights; kNN cannot. "For kNN, feature selection is part of the model": the project tests a lean set for that reason.
Three mistakes to expect, and how to coach each
  • No scaler, or a scaler fitted outside the pipeline. Symptom: AUC near 0.77, neighbors that share only an order value. Coach: "Which column decides the distance? Print the shares."
  • k chosen by training AUC, or k = 5 left at the default. Symptoms: "k = 1 is perfect"; a ranking full of ties. Coach: "On which rows? How many distinct probabilities can k = 5 give?"
  • Lookalikes searched with an unscaled row, or too many columns (kneighbors). Symptom: lookalikes that look nothing like the customer. Coach: "Did the customer go through the same scaler as the stored rows?" (debug_knn.py has exactly this bug.)
Guided lab
  • Lab A (12m, everyone): labs/module-07/l4_1-knn/fraud_neighbors_starter.py; reference labs/module-07/l4_1-knn/fraud_neighbors_solution.py. kNN on fraud, where dollars, days and 0/1 flags meet in one distance.
  • Expected, Lab A: account age makes 99.7% of the first two payments' unscaled distance; unscaled kNN AUC 0.659, AP 0.056; scaled AUC 0.812, AP 0.397; logistic regression AUC 0.848, AP 0.444; CV AP 0.362, 0.448, 0.468 for k = 5, 51, 201; k = 5 gives 6 distinct probabilities (14,588 test payments at 0) and test AP 0.329; k = 201 gives 43 and test AP 0.419.
  • Debrief Lab A: "Scaling multiplied AP by seven. Tuned kNN still trails logistic regression on fraud: nine columns, some weak, every one with an equal vote."
  • Lab B (8m, pairs): labs/module-07/l4_1-knn/lookalike_days_starter.py; reference labs/module-07/l4_1-knn/lookalike_days_solution.py. KNeighborsRegressor forecasts Boise's 2025 days from lookalike days in 2023 and 2024.
  • Expected, Lab B: weekday mean $1,669; raw dow and day-of-year unscaled $1,889, scaled $1,327; cyclical calendar plus events: k = 1 $1,389, k = 5 $1,244, k = 15 $1,256, always under-forecasting ($+517 to $+1,016); Boise grew $6,831 → $7,821 → $8,342 a day; trained on 2024 only, k = 5: $1,202 (+$458).
  • Debrief Lab B: "An average of past days can never forecast a level it has never seen. kNN cannot extrapolate a trend; a linear model can (Session 6)."
  • Fast finishers: labs/module-07/l4_1-knn/debug_knn.py: two loud bugs (ValueError: Expected n_neighbors <= n_samples_fit, but n_neighbors = 51, n_samples_fit = 40, n_samples = 3; ValueError: Input X contains NaN.) and two silent ones (no scaler: CV AUC 0.787; kneighbors on an unscaled row: 39 of 51 lookalikes with a median of 364 days). Fixed: CV AUC 0.854, 3 of 51.
Start the lesson project

The lesson project is lookalike churn scoring: a shortlist where every name comes with the lookalikes that justify it. In the room, Steps 1 to 3.

  • Step 1: 4500 1500 test churn rate 0.165. Step 2: baseline AUC 0.500, logistic regression 0.823.
  • Step 3: features and k by CV: the lean six with k = 51 and distance weights wins (CV AUC 0.8552).
  • Goes home: Step 4 (test AUC 0.855; 31 churners in the top 50), Step 5 (a reason per customer from a summary of lookalikes) and Step 6 (lookalike_shortlist.csv).
Checks for understanding
  • Why is the k = 1 training AUC exactly 1.000? Answer: every training customer is its own nearest neighbor.
  • What does predict_proba return for kNN with k = 15? Answer: the share of the 15 neighbors in each class.
  • Name two things kNN needs that logistic regression does not. Answer: scaled features (distance is the model) and the stored training rows at prediction time.

Workbook self-check, items Q1 to Q6.

Answers: Q1 [[0.333 0.667]]; Q2 A) Variance falls and bias rises: predictions drift toward the overall churn rate; Q3 C) Account age runs up to 7,030 days, so it alone decided who the neighbors were; the flags that signal fraud had no say; Q4 B) kNN gives every scaled column an equal say in the distance; logistic regression learns near-zero weights for useless columns; Q5 [[5.]]; Q6 D) The scaler and every training customer, because each prediction searches them. Full set with explanations in the Answer Key.

Differentiation

Support: do TODO 1 of Lab A together; keep the two dials on the board and ask which one each step touches. Stretch: the lesson's Exercise 4 (backward elimination by hand) and the Bonus Challenge (feature weights before the distance).

4.2

Naive Bayes

75 min
Objectives (from the lesson page)
  • Compute a posterior probability by hand with Bayes' rule, starting from counts in a table
  • Explain the "naive" independence assumption and show what it costs when features repeat each other
  • Turn review text into word counts with CountVectorizer and classify it with MultinomialNB inside a pipeline
  • Explain what the smoothing parameter alpha prevents, and choose between the Multinomial, Bernoulli, Complement and Gaussian variants
  • Tell a model that ranks well apart from one whose probabilities you can take at face value
Timing in the room
10mFrame: the seasoned store manager; prior, clues, posterior; the naive habit
30mLive demo: Steps 1 to 10
20mGuided lab: fraud_flags_bayes_starter.py (everyone), then review_category_starter.py in pairs
15mStart the lesson project: Steps 1 to 3
Mental model to say

"A store manager has read every comment card for years. Before reading a new one, they have a hunch: three cards in four are happy. That is the prior. Then they read clue by clue. 'Returned' nudges them strongly toward unhappy; 'love' nudges them back. Each clue multiplies the odds. At the end the hunch has become a judgment: the posterior. The manager has one habit: they weigh each word on its own, as if words did not influence each other. That is the naive part. Often wrong, still surprisingly good."

Write Bayes' rule and, under it, the odds form: posterior odds = prior odds × likelihood ratio × likelihood ratio × ...

Live demo (you type, they follow)

New notebook lesson_4_2.

  1. Reviews, a clean yes or no (2m). "Drop the mixed 3-star reviews; split as always." 
    import pandas as pd
from sklearn.model_selection import train_test_split

reviews = pd.read_csv("data/reviews.csv")
print(reviews.shape)
print(reviews["rating"].value_counts().sort_index().to_dict())

labeled = reviews[reviews["rating"] != 3].copy()
labeled["positive"] = (labeled["rating"] >= 4).astype(int)
train, test = train_test_split(labeled, test_size=0.25, stratify=labeled["positive"], random_state=42)
X_train, y_train = train["text"], train["positive"]
X_test, y_test = test["text"], test["positive"]
print(len(labeled), "labeled reviews,", f"{labeled['positive'].mean():.1%} positive")
print(len(train), "train,", len(test), "test")
    (4000, 8)
{1: 230, 2: 558, 3: 905, 4: 1093, 5: 1214}
3095 labeled reviews, 74.5% positive
2321 train, 774 test
  2. Bayes' rule from counts (4m). "Prior, likelihood, posterior, for one word." 
    has_word = X_train.str.contains(r"\breturned\b", case=False)
is_neg = y_train == 0
p_neg = is_neg.mean()
p_word_given_neg = has_word[is_neg].mean()
p_word_given_pos = has_word[~is_neg].mean()
p_word = has_word.mean()
posterior = p_word_given_neg * p_neg / p_word
print(f"prior P(negative)          {p_neg:.3f}")
print(f"P(word | negative)         {p_word_given_neg:.4f}")
print(f"P(word | positive)         {p_word_given_pos:.4f}")
print(f"posterior P(negative|word) {posterior:.3f}")
print(f"check by direct counting   {is_neg[has_word].mean():.3f}")
    prior P(negative)          0.255
P(word | negative)         0.0406
P(word | positive)         0.0029
posterior P(negative|word) 0.828
check by direct counting   0.828
    The likelihood ratio (about 14) lifts 0.25 to 0.83. With one clue, Bayes' rule equals counting. With 20 words no combination repeats: that is why we need the naive assumption.
  3. A bag of words (2m). "CountVectorizer: one column per word, word order thrown away." 
    from sklearn.feature_extraction.text import CountVectorizer

demo = ["The tent leaked. Returned it.", "Great tent, great price."]
demo_vec = CountVectorizer()
demo_counts = demo_vec.fit_transform(demo)
print(demo_vec.get_feature_names_out())
print(demo_counts.toarray())
print(type(demo_counts).__name__)
    ['great' 'it' 'leaked' 'price' 'returned' 'tent' 'the']
[[0 1 1 0 1 1 1]
 [2 0 0 1 0 1 0]]
csr_matrix
  4. Sparse on purpose (1m). "333 words, about 18 per review." 
    vec = CountVectorizer()
counts_train = vec.fit_transform(X_train)
print(counts_train.shape)
print(f"non-zero cells: {counts_train.nnz:,} of {counts_train.shape[0] * counts_train.shape[1]:,}")
    (2321, 333)
non-zero cells: 40,981 of 772,893
  5. MultinomialNB against the baseline (3m). "Vectorizer and model in one pipeline, so the vocabulary is learned from training reviews only." 
    from sklearn.pipeline import make_pipeline
from sklearn.naive_bayes import MultinomialNB
from sklearn.dummy import DummyClassifier

baseline = DummyClassifier(strategy="most_frequent").fit(X_train, y_train)
nb = make_pipeline(CountVectorizer(), MultinomialNB())
nb.fit(X_train, y_train)
print(f"baseline accuracy    {baseline.score(X_test, y_test):.3f}")
print(f"Naive Bayes accuracy {nb.score(X_test, y_test):.3f}")

from sklearn.metrics import classification_report, roc_auc_score

pred = nb.predict(X_test)
print(classification_report(y_test, pred, target_names=["negative", "positive"], digits=3))
print(f"ROC AUC {roc_auc_score(y_test, nb.predict_proba(X_test)[:, 1]):.3f}")
    baseline accuracy    0.745
Naive Bayes accuracy 0.857
              precision    recall  f1-score   support

    negative      0.692     0.787     0.736       197
    positive      0.924     0.880     0.902       577

    accuracy                          0.857       774
   macro avg      0.808     0.834     0.819       774
weighted avg      0.865     0.857     0.859       774

ROC AUC 0.921
    Recall 0.787 for negative reviews, AUC 0.92: a strong ranking for a triage list.
  6. What it counted (3m). "feature_log_prob_ row 1 minus row 0: each word's nudge." 
    vocab = nb.named_steps["countvectorizer"].get_feature_names_out()
mnb = nb.named_steps["multinomialnb"]
print(mnb.classes_, mnb.class_count_)
log_ratio = pd.Series(mnb.feature_log_prob_[1] - mnb.feature_log_prob_[0], index=vocab).sort_values()
print("most negative:", log_ratio.head(5).round(2).to_dict())
print("most positive:", log_ratio.tail(5).round(2).to_dict())
    [0 1] [ 591. 1730.]
most negative: {'save': -3.4, 'your': -3.4, 'very': -3.29, 'disappointed': -3.21, 'regret': -3.2}
most positive: {'stars': 3.43, 'from': 3.43, 'regrets': 3.46, 'no': 3.46, 'love': 3.58}
    "save" and "your" from "save your money"; "no" is POSITIVE because of "no regrets". The model knows counts, not meaning.
  7. Try your own reviews (3m). "Predict before running." 
    new_reviews = [
    "Leaked on the first night and the zipper broke. Returned it.",
    "Light, warm and packs down small. Would buy again.",
    "Not bad at all for the price.",
]
for text, p in zip(new_reviews, nb.predict_proba(new_reviews)[:, 1]):
    print(f"P(positive) = {p:.3f}  {text}")
    P(positive) = 0.063  Leaked on the first night and the zipper broke. Returned it.
P(positive) = 0.302  Light, warm and packs down small. Would buy again.
P(positive) = 0.745  Not bad at all for the price.
    "Would buy again" reads negative word by word ("would not buy again" is common). Bag-of-words cannot see negation.
  8. Smoothing: never say never (4m). "'love' never appears in a negative review." 
    love = counts_train[:, vec.vocabulary_["love"]].toarray().ravel()
print("'love' in negative reviews:", love[y_train.to_numpy() == 0].sum())
print("'love' in positive reviews:", love[y_train.to_numpy() == 1].sum())
tricky = ["Leaked at the seams, fell apart after three trips and I returned it. Love the color though."]
for alpha in [1e-10, 1.0]:
    model = make_pipeline(CountVectorizer(), MultinomialNB(alpha=alpha)).fit(X_train, y_train)
    print(f"alpha={alpha:g}  P(negative), P(positive) = {model.predict_proba(tricky).round(3)[0]}")
    'love' in negative reviews: 0
'love' in positive reviews: 106
alpha=1e-10  P(negative), P(positive) = [0. 1.]
alpha=1  P(negative), P(positive) = [0.973 0.027]
    Without smoothing one word vetoes all the evidence. Write the formula: (count + alpha) / (total + alpha × vocabulary).
  9. Four flavors (2m). "Counts, presence, complement." 
    from sklearn.naive_bayes import BernoulliNB, ComplementNB

variants = {
    "MultinomialNB": make_pipeline(CountVectorizer(), MultinomialNB()),
    "ComplementNB": make_pipeline(CountVectorizer(), ComplementNB()),
    "BernoulliNB": make_pipeline(CountVectorizer(binary=True), BernoulliNB()),
}
for name, model in variants.items():
    model.fit(X_train, y_train)
    auc = roc_auc_score(y_test, model.predict_proba(X_test)[:, 1])
    print(f"{name:14s} accuracy {model.score(X_test, y_test):.3f}  AUC {auc:.3f}")
    MultinomialNB  accuracy 0.857  AUC 0.921
ComplementNB   accuracy 0.835  AUC 0.921
BernoulliNB    accuracy 0.859  AUC 0.923
    All within a couple of points on short reviews; try two or three.
  10. GaussianNB, and the cost of being naive (6m). "A bell curve per feature per class on churn." 
    from sklearn.naive_bayes import GaussianNB

customers = pd.read_csv("data/customers_churn.csv")
features = ["tenure_months", "orders_12m", "spend_12m", "avg_order_value", "days_since_last_order",
            "returns_12m", "return_rate", "support_tickets_12m", "discount_share", "app_user"]
Xc_train, Xc_test, yc_train, yc_test = train_test_split(
    customers[features], customers["churned"], test_size=0.25, stratify=customers["churned"], random_state=42
)
gnb = GaussianNB().fit(Xc_train, yc_train)
print("priors:", gnb.class_prior_.round(3))
learned = pd.DataFrame({"mean_stayed": gnb.theta_[0], "mean_churned": gnb.theta_[1],
                        "sd_stayed": gnb.var_[0] ** 0.5, "sd_churned": gnb.var_[1] ** 0.5}, index=features)
print(learned.loc[["days_since_last_order", "discount_share", "tenure_months"]].round(2))

from sklearn.linear_model import LogisticRegression
from sklearn.preprocessing import StandardScaler

logreg = make_pipeline(StandardScaler(), LogisticRegression()).fit(Xc_train, yc_train)
for name, model in [("GaussianNB", gnb), ("logistic regression", logreg)]:
    auc = roc_auc_score(yc_test, model.predict_proba(Xc_test)[:, 1])
    print(f"{name:20s} accuracy {model.score(Xc_test, yc_test):.3f}  AUC {auc:.3f}")

independent = [f for f in features if f not in ("spend_12m", "avg_order_value", "returns_12m", "return_rate")]
gnb_lean = GaussianNB().fit(Xc_train[independent], yc_train)
auc = roc_auc_score(yc_test, gnb_lean.predict_proba(Xc_test[independent])[:, 1])
print(f"GaussianNB, {len(independent)} features: accuracy {gnb_lean.score(Xc_test[independent], yc_test):.3f}  AUC {auc:.3f}")

p_gnb = gnb.predict_proba(Xc_test)[:, 1]
confident = p_gnb > 0.9
print(f"GaussianNB says above 90% for {confident.sum()} customers; {yc_test[confident].mean():.1%} of them churned")
    priors: [0.835 0.165]
                       mean_stayed  mean_churned  sd_stayed  sd_churned
days_since_last_order        68.98        156.94      71.21      117.90
discount_share                0.19          0.29       0.17        0.21
tenure_months                42.26         37.53      29.45       35.24
GaussianNB           accuracy 0.815  AUC 0.778
logistic regression  accuracy 0.848  AUC 0.823
GaussianNB, 6 features: accuracy 0.845  AUC 0.813
GaussianNB says above 90% for 102 customers; 60.8% of them churned
    Churners averaged 157 days since their last order against 69; show the bell-curve chart on the page. Fewer columns, better model: duplicated evidence was counted twice. And above 90% sure, only 61% churned: rank with it, recalibrate before quoting it.
Three mistakes to expect, and how to coach each
  • The wrong variant for the input. Raw text into the model (could not convert string to float), a scaler in front of MultinomialNB (Negative values), word counts into GaussianNB (Sparse data). Coach: "What does this variant expect: counts, flags or measurements?"
  • alpha = 0 or tiny. One word vetoes the rest. Coach: "What is P(word | class) for a word never seen in that class?"
  • Probabilities quoted as facts, or stop words removed by habit. Coach: "Above 90% sure: how many really churned?" and "Is 'not' a stop word?"
Guided lab
  • Lab A (12m, everyone): labs/module-07/l4_2-naive-bayes/fraud_flags_bayes_starter.py; reference labs/module-07/l4_2-naive-bayes/fraud_flags_bayes_solution.py. Bayes' rule on fraud warning signs, the naive product checked against direct counts, BernoulliNB on nine flags.
  • Expected, Lab A: prior 0.0088; country mismatch 0.137 against 0.0121, posterior 0.091 by both routes; likelihood ratios from 11.3 (country mismatch) to 1.9 (new account); night + new device: naive 0.280 against counted 0.425 (193 payments); big amount + gift card: naive 0.185 against 0.508 (59); BernoulliNB AUC 0.810, AP 0.439 (21 payments above 0.9, all fraud); logistic regression AUC 0.810, AP 0.435 (8 above 0.9).
  • Debrief Lab A: "Here the naive answer is too LOW: among frauds these flags come together (night on a new device), so treating them as independent misses that they reinforce each other. Independence can fail in either direction. Nine 0/1 flags, and Naive Bayes ranks as well as logistic regression."
  • Lab B (8m, pairs): labs/module-07/l4_2-naive-bayes/review_category_starter.py; reference labs/module-07/l4_2-naive-bayes/review_category_solution.py. Ten classes: which product category is a review about?
  • Expected, Lab B: baseline 0.237; MultinomialNB 0.586, ComplementNB 0.592, logistic regression 0.605; CV accuracy 0.579 to 0.583 for alpha 0.1 to 3; Tents' telling words start with canopy, camp, tent; most frequent mix-ups Camp Kitchen → Accessories (20), Apparel ↔ Paddling (20, 19); lowest recall Packs 0.39.
Start the lesson project

The lesson project is review triage for customer care. In the room, Steps 1 to 3.

  • Step 1: 2321 774 905 ['R02194', 'R02522', 'R03559']. Step 2: word pairs help: CV AUC 0.899 → 0.912. Step 3: alpha 0.3 (0.9123), barely different from the others.
  • Goes home: Step 4 (test accuracy 0.868, AUC 0.934, negative recall 0.843), Step 5 (rank the 905 three-star reviews; 457 read as negative) and Step 6 (60 possible star/text mismatches; two CSV files).
Checks for understanding
  • Prior 0.25, likelihood ratio 3: posterior? Answer: odds 1/3 × 3 = 1, so 0.5.
  • Why does MultinomialNB refuse scaled data? Answer: it models counts; scaling makes negative numbers.
  • AUC 0.81 but above 0.9 only 61% churned: use it how? Answer: for ranking; recalibrate before quoting probabilities.

Workbook self-check, items Q7 to Q12.

Answers: Q7 D) About 0.71; Q8 [[1 2] [1 0]]; Q9 A) "love" never appeared in a negative training review, so with almost no smoothing its probability in that class is about 0, and one zero factor vetoes everything else; Q10 C) GaussianNB, unscaled: it models continuous measurements with a bell curve per class; Q11 B) The dropped columns repeat information already present, and Naive Bayes counts each one as a new, independent clue; Q12 True False. Full set with explanations in the Answer Key.

Differentiation

Support: do Lab A's TODO 1 on the projector with the odds form of Bayes' rule on the board. Stretch: the lesson's Exercise 4 (GaussianNB by hand) and the Bonus Challenge (CalibratedClassifierCV and the Brier score).

Closing

Exit ticket (3 questions, no notes, 4 minutes)
  1. Why does kNN need scaled features, and why does MultinomialNB refuse them? Answer: kNN's distance adds raw differences, so units decide similarity; MultinomialNB models counts, and scaling creates negative values.
  2. k = 1 gives training AUC 1.000 and test AUC 0.645. Name the problem and the fix. Answer: high variance (each customer is its own neighbor); choose a larger k by cross-validation.
  3. Prior P(negative) = 0.25; a word appears in 8% of negative and 2% of positive reviews. Posterior? Answer: odds 1/3 × 4 = 4/3, so P ≈ 0.57.
Homework (about 2 to 3 hours)
  • Finish both lesson projects. 4.1 Steps 4 to 6 (test AUC 0.855, a reason for every name, lookalike_shortlist.csv); 4.2 Steps 4 to 6 (accuracy 0.868, AUC 0.934; 3-star ranking; mismatches; triage_three_star.csv, rating_mismatches.csv).
  • Lesson exercises. 4.1: Exercises 1 to 3 (distances by hand, Manhattan or Euclidean, kNN for regression); 4.2: Exercises 1 to 3 (Bayes for "regret", how small can the training set be, fool the model). Exercise 4 of each is a stretch.
  • Both Quick Quizzes and both journal entries.
  • Before the Next Lesson: 4.1: MinMaxScaler instead of StandardScaler; predict, then check, a duplicated recency column. 4.2: read five mismatches; predict BernoulliNB on long manuals.
  • Labs not finished in class; optional debug_knn.py.

Instructor Kit · Facilitator Guide

Session 8: Support Vector Machines and Decision Trees

How to teach Session 8 of Machine Learning Foundations: support vector machines (the widest street, support vectors, the C and gamma dials, the kernel trick, scaling, class weights, calibration) and decision trees (signposts and campsites, Gini, leaves as probabilities, overfitting and its controls, importances and instability). Learners leave with a tuned SVM shortlist started, a churn rule book started, and a fraud rule book of their own.

2 lessons 3 hrs session Lessons 4.3–5.1 Session 8 of 11

How to Use This Guide

  • Same rhythm for both lessons: frame (one mental model) → live demo (you type, they follow) → guided lab (kit labs, new practice) → start the lesson project. Exercises, quizzes and journals are homework.
  • Every code block below was run to produce the output printed under it (Python 3.12, pandas 3.0, scikit-learn 1.9.1, 4 CPU threads). The demo code is the lesson pages' code with the plotting lines left out: show the pictures from the lesson page (the street with C = 0.01 and 100, the ring with both kernels, the moons with three gammas, the tree drawn with plot_tree, the depth curve).
  • Keep classification_svm.html and trees_decision.html open in browser tabs. Labs live in labs/module-08/: hand out the _starter.py and debug_*.py files, keep the _solution.py files.
  • Session 8 is the hinge of the course: the last of Module 4's four classifiers, then the first tree. Today's tree is the building block of Session 9's forests and boosting, so protect the 5.1 demo.
The #1 risk of Session 8: an SVM demo that drowns in math

The lesson needs no formulas beyond the RBF similarity, and the room does not either. Teach with the street picture, the two dials (C, gamma) and the ring. If someone asks about Lagrange multipliers or the dual problem, give one sentence ("the training only ever needs the similarity of each pair of points; that is why kernels work") and offer to talk at the break. The second risk is time: the 4.3 project's grid search (45 fits) takes 30 to 60 seconds on a laptop. Start it, then talk about what the table will show while it runs.

Before the Session

  • ☐ Run the kit check: python3 labs/check_labs.py module-08 from this kit's folder. Every line PASS (8 files, about 30 seconds).
  • ☐ Downloads: none. Everything today uses the Pinecrest CSV files already in data/.
  • ☐ Projector machine: (.venv) active in your own pinecrest-ml, JupyterLab started there, two empty notebooks: lesson_4_3 and lesson_5_1. Browser tabs: classification_svm.html, trees_decision.html, and the Session 7 lesson pages for the warm-up.
  • ☐ Board, before learners arrive: a street between two neighborhoods with its two curbs (dashed) and a few circled curb houses; under it "C = price of a violation · gamma = how far each point reaches". On the right, an empty trail map with a first signpost.
  • ☐ Print or share: the Session 8 pages of the workbook; sticky notes for the exit ticket.
  • ☐ Pre-run the 4.3 project grid once on the projector machine so you know its time (in the kit's measurements, under a minute).
Known environment traps
SymptomCauseFix
AttributeError: This 'Pipeline' has no attribute 'predict_proba'An SVM has no probabilitiesdecision_function for ranking and AUC; CalibratedClassifierCV(SVC(), ensemble=False) for probabilities.
FutureWarning about probability=TrueCode copied from an older tutorialRemove probability=True; calibrate as above.
ValueError: Invalid parameter 'C' for estimator Pipeline(...)Grid key without the step namesvc__C, svc__gamma (lowercase class name, two underscores).
A grid search that seems frozen45 kernel-SVM fits; or n_jobs=-1 on a busy laptopWait up to a minute; check that the scaler is in the pipeline (unscaled SVMs can be much slower to converge).
InvalidParameterError: The 'max_depth' parameter ... Got 0 instead.max_depth=0Use 1 or more, or None.
ValueError: could not convert string to float: 'Bend'A text column given to a treeOne-hot encode it (the 5.1 project's tree_prep).
Tree rules that look backwards (big safe leaf labeled churned)class_names not in the order of classes_Build the names from model.classes_.
plot_tree text overlapping or tinyFigure too small for the depthfigsize=(10, 5) and depth 2 or 3 for display; use export_text for bigger trees.

Warm-up: Nearest Neighbors and Naive Bayes

10 min
Run it

Put the five questions on the slide. Two minutes alone, one minute with a neighbor, then cold-call gently. While they work, walk the room for the Session 7 homework: the lookalike_shortlist.csv (Lesson 4.1 project) and the review triage files (Lesson 4.2 project).

  • W1 Lesson 4.1: a kNN churn model scores a perfect 1.000 on its training data with k=1. Why, and what do you do about it? Answer: Each training customer is its own nearest neighbor, so k=1 is right by construction (high variance). Choose a larger k by cross-validation; the 4.1 project chose k=51. Ask for the word: overfitting. Today's two models have their own versions: gamma for SVMs, depth for trees.
  • W2 Two unscaled features: spend differs by $300 and days since last order by 120. What share of the squared distance comes from spend? Predict, then check. Answer: 0.86 (86%). 300² = 90,000 against 120² = 14,400: spend takes 86% of the distance purely because of its units. The same reason SVMs need a scaler today.
  • W3 Lesson 4.2: a word appears in 6% of negative reviews and 0.5% of positive ones; 25% of reviews are negative. After seeing the word, about how likely is the review to be negative? Answer: About 0.8: the odds go from 1:3 to (1 × 6) : (3 × 0.5) = 6 : 1.5 = 4 : 1. Prior odds times the likelihood ratio (12). Anyone stuck: draw the 1,000-review table.
  • W4 Why does MultinomialNB need alpha above 0? Answer: Without smoothing a word never seen in one class gives that class a probability of exactly 0, which vetoes all the other evidence. Then bridge: today's SVM on reviews (Lab A) needs no smoothing, but it needs scaling, in its text form (TF-IDF).
  • W5 Your Naive Bayes model ranks customers well (AUC 0.81) but its probabilities pile up near 0 and 1. Can you quote them to the care team? Answer: Rank with it, but recalibrate (CalibratedClassifierCV) before quoting probabilities. Today's SVM gives no probabilities at all, and the same tool fixes it.
Bridge to today

"kNN asked the closest past customers, Naive Bayes multiplied clues. Today: a model that draws the widest possible boundary, and a model that asks yes-or-no questions. By the end of the day you will have five classifiers and an honest way to compare them."

Lesson Plans

4.3

Support Vector Machines

75 min
Objectives (from the lesson page)
  • Explain the maximum-margin idea and find the support vectors of a fitted model
  • Predict how changing C and gamma changes the margin, the number of support vectors and the risk of overfitting
  • Explain the kernel trick and use the RBF kernel to learn a curved boundary
  • Build a scaled SVC pipeline, rank with decision_function, handle imbalance with class_weight, and tune C and gamma with cross-validation
  • Decide when an SVM is a sensible choice and when its training time rules it out
Timing in the room
10mFrame: the widest street; support vectors; C and gamma
30mLive demo: Steps 1 to 12
25mGuided lab: review_svm_starter.py (13m), then kernel_by_hand_starter.py in pairs (12m)
10mStart the lesson project: Steps 1 to 3
Mental model to say

Point at the board: "Two neighborhoods, red and blue, and a city planner who must build a straight street between them. Many streets would work. The planner picks the widest one, because a wide street leaves room for error: a new house built near either edge still lands on the right side."

Circle the curb houses: "Only these decided where the street went. The houses deep inside could move and nothing would change. They are the support vectors, and they are all the model remembers."

Then the two extensions, one per dial: "Real neighborhoods overlap, so we allow some houses on the street, at a price: that price is C. Real boundaries curve, so we bend the street with a kernel; for the usual RBF kernel, gamma says how far each house's influence reaches."

Live demo (you type, they follow)

New notebook lesson_4_3. Ask for a prediction before every run that prints a number: the street gets narrower as C rises, and gamma's test score collapses while its training score climbs.

  1. The widest street (3m). "Many lines separate these two clouds. The SVM picks the widest street that fits, and only the points on its curbs decide where it goes." Show the lesson's first picture, then run. 
    import numpy as np
import pandas as pd
from sklearn.datasets import make_blobs
from sklearn.svm import SVC

X_toy, y_toy = make_blobs(n_samples=80, centers=[(-2, 0), (2, 0)], cluster_std=1.3, random_state=42)

svm = SVC(kernel="linear", C=1).fit(X_toy, y_toy)
print("support vectors per class:", svm.n_support_)
print("margin width:", round(2 / np.linalg.norm(svm.coef_[0]), 2))
print("training accuracy:", svm.score(X_toy, y_toy))
    support vectors per class: [6 5]
margin width: 1.34
training accuracy: 0.9625
    80 points, 11 support vectors: the other 69 could move freely and nothing would change. Ask: "Why not 100% training accuracy?" (the clouds overlap; no straight line separates them).
  2. C prices the violations (3m). "C is what a point inside the street costs. Cheap violations: a wide street held up by many points. Expensive: a narrow street that bends toward individual points." 
    for C in [0.01, 0.1, 1, 100]:
    model = SVC(kernel="linear", C=C).fit(X_toy, y_toy)
    width = 2 / np.linalg.norm(model.coef_[0])
    print(f"C={C:<6} support vectors {model.n_support_.sum():2d}   margin width {width:.2f}")
    C=0.01   support vectors 42   margin width 4.06
C=0.1    support vectors 18   margin width 2.11
C=1      support vectors 11   margin width 1.34
C=100    support vectors  7   margin width 0.75
    Write on the board: small C = strong regularization = wide street. It is the same C as logistic regression's, the inverse of Lesson 3.2's alpha.
  3. A problem no line can solve (2m). "One class makes a ring around the other. Predict the linear SVM's accuracy." 
    from sklearn.datasets import make_circles
from sklearn.model_selection import train_test_split

X_ring, y_ring = make_circles(n_samples=300, noise=0.08, factor=0.5, random_state=42)
Xr_train, Xr_test, yr_train, yr_test = train_test_split(X_ring, y_ring, test_size=0.3, stratify=y_ring, random_state=42)

linear_ring = SVC(kernel="linear").fit(Xr_train, yr_train)
print(f"linear SVM, test accuracy {linear_ring.score(Xr_test, yr_test):.3f}")
    linear SVM, test accuracy 0.478
    Worse than a coin flip.
  4. Lift it by hand (3m). "What separates them? Distance from the center. Add it as a third feature and a flat plane in 3-D separates them perfectly." 
    def add_radius(X):
    return np.column_stack([X, (X ** 2).sum(axis=1)])

lifted = SVC(kernel="linear").fit(add_radius(Xr_train), yr_train)
print(f"linear SVM on (x, y, x²+y²), test accuracy {lifted.score(add_radius(Xr_test), yr_test):.3f}")
    linear SVM on (x, y, x²+y²), test accuracy 1.000
    Draw the bowl: inner points low, outer points high, a flat sheet between them. Projected back, the flat sheet is a circle.
  5. Let the kernel do it (3m). "With ten customer features you cannot see which feature to add. The kernel trick never builds the features: it only needs how similar each pair of points is, as if they had been lifted." Write the RBF similarity: exp(-gamma × squared distance). 
    rbf_ring = SVC(kernel="rbf").fit(Xr_train, yr_train)
print(f"RBF SVM, test accuracy {rbf_ring.score(Xr_test, yr_test):.3f}, support vectors {rbf_ring.n_support_.sum()}")
    RBF SVM, test accuracy 1.000, support vectors 42
    Lab B (kernel_by_hand) does exactly this comparison on the churn table: 66 hand-built columns against a kernel that builds none.
  6. gamma is how far each point reaches (4m). "Small gamma: each support vector's influence spreads wide. Huge gamma: a tiny bubble around each point." Ask for a prediction of the test score at gamma=1000 before you run it. 
    from sklearn.datasets import make_moons

X_moon, y_moon = make_moons(n_samples=400, noise=0.3, random_state=42)
Xm_train, Xm_test, ym_train, ym_test = train_test_split(X_moon, y_moon, test_size=0.3, stratify=y_moon, random_state=42)

for gamma in [0.1, 1, 10, 100, 1000]:
    model = SVC(kernel="rbf", gamma=gamma).fit(Xm_train, ym_train)
    print(f"gamma={gamma:<5} train {model.score(Xm_train, ym_train):.3f}  test {model.score(Xm_test, ym_test):.3f}"
          f"  support vectors {model.n_support_.sum()}")
    gamma=0.1   train 0.814  test 0.908  support vectors 141
gamma=1     train 0.882  test 0.925  support vectors 96
gamma=10    train 0.921  test 0.900  support vectors 141
gamma=100   train 0.975  test 0.867  support vectors 259
gamma=1000  train 1.000  test 0.650  support vectors 280
    Training climbs to 1.000, test collapses to 0.650, and the support-vector count rises at the end: almost every point must be remembered. Same overfitting picture as Lesson 3.2, new dial.
  7. Scaling is not optional (3m). "The RBF kernel is distances, and Lesson 4.1 showed what unscaled distances do." Note decision_function in the AUC line: AUC only needs a ranking score. 
    from sklearn.pipeline import make_pipeline
from sklearn.preprocessing import StandardScaler
from sklearn.metrics import roc_auc_score

customers = pd.read_csv("data/customers_churn.csv")
features = ["tenure_months", "orders_12m", "spend_12m", "avg_order_value", "days_since_last_order",
            "returns_12m", "return_rate", "support_tickets_12m", "discount_share", "app_user"]
X_train, X_test, y_train, y_test = train_test_split(
    customers[features], customers["churned"], test_size=0.25, stratify=customers["churned"], random_state=42
)

for name, model in [("unscaled SVC", SVC()), ("scaled SVC", make_pipeline(StandardScaler(), SVC()))]:
    model.fit(X_train, y_train)
    auc = roc_auc_score(y_test, model.decision_function(X_test))
    print(f"{name:13s} accuracy {model.score(X_test, y_test):.3f}  ROC AUC {auc:.3f}")
    unscaled SVC  accuracy 0.839  ROC AUC 0.767
scaled SVC    accuracy 0.859  ROC AUC 0.814
    0.767 to 0.814 from one line. The scaler goes INSIDE the pipeline (Lesson 2.2) so cross-validation never peeks.
  8. A fair comparison on churn (4m). "Baseline, logistic regression, an RBF SVM, and the same SVM told that a missed churner costs more." 
    from sklearn.dummy import DummyClassifier
from sklearn.linear_model import LogisticRegression
from sklearn.metrics import average_precision_score, recall_score

def report(name, model):
    model.fit(X_train, y_train)
    if hasattr(model, "decision_function"):
        scores = model.decision_function(X_test)
    else:
        scores = model.predict_proba(X_test)[:, 1]
    print(f"{name:22s} accuracy {model.score(X_test, y_test):.3f}  AUC {roc_auc_score(y_test, scores):.3f}"
          f"  AP {average_precision_score(y_test, scores):.3f}  recall {recall_score(y_test, model.predict(X_test)):.3f}")

report("baseline", DummyClassifier(strategy="most_frequent"))
report("logistic regression", make_pipeline(StandardScaler(), LogisticRegression()))
report("SVC (RBF)", make_pipeline(StandardScaler(), SVC()))
report("SVC (RBF, balanced)", make_pipeline(StandardScaler(), SVC(class_weight="balanced")))
    baseline               accuracy 0.835  AUC 0.500  AP 0.165  recall 0.000
logistic regression    accuracy 0.848  AUC 0.823  AP 0.497  recall 0.266
SVC (RBF)              accuracy 0.859  AUC 0.814  AP 0.540  recall 0.258
SVC (RBF, balanced)    accuracy 0.792  AUC 0.850  AP 0.495  recall 0.774
    Three things: the plain SVM has the best accuracy and a WORSE AUC than logistic regression; class_weight="balanced" jumps recall from 0.258 to 0.774 and AUC to 0.850; accuracy falls because it flags many more stayers. Ask: "Which number would the care team care about?"
  9. Probabilities, when you need them (2m). "An SVM scores distances. When the team wants a probability, for revenue at risk, calibrate." 
    from sklearn.calibration import CalibratedClassifierCV

svm_proba = make_pipeline(StandardScaler(), CalibratedClassifierCV(SVC(class_weight="balanced"), ensemble=False))
svm_proba.fit(X_train, y_train)
p = svm_proba.predict_proba(X_test)[:, 1]
print("first five churn probabilities:", p[:5].round(3))
print(f"average predicted churn {p.mean():.3f} vs real churn rate {y_test.mean():.3f}")
print(f"ROC AUC {roc_auc_score(y_test, p):.3f}")
    first five churn probabilities: [0.066 0.484 0.105 0.148 0.046]
average predicted churn 0.164 vs real churn rate 0.165
ROC AUC 0.850
    Average 0.164 against a real 0.165, AUC unchanged. Mention once: older code uses SVC(probability=True); it is deprecated now (FutureWarning in scikit-learn 1.9).
  10. Error 1: asking for probabilities (1m). "Read the last line." 
    make_pipeline(StandardScaler(), SVC()).fit(X_train, y_train).predict_proba(X_test)
    AttributeError: This 'Pipeline' has no attribute 'predict_proba'
    The pipeline passes the question to its last step, hence "Pipeline".
  11. Error 2: a grid key without the step name (1m). "The grid applies to the pipeline, and the pipeline has no C." 
    from sklearn.model_selection import GridSearchCV

GridSearchCV(make_pipeline(StandardScaler(), SVC()), {"C": [1, 10]}).fit(X_train, y_train)
    ValueError: Invalid parameter 'C' for estimator Pipeline(steps=[('standardscaler', StandardScaler()), ('svc', SVC())]). Valid parameters are: ['memory', 'steps', 'transform_input', 'verbose'].
    Fix: svc__C. pipe.get_params().keys() lists every valid key. The project needs this in ten minutes.
  12. The silent one (1m). "No error, and that is the problem." 
    silent = make_pipeline(StandardScaler(), SVC(kernel="linear")).fit(X_train, y_train)
print(f"accuracy {silent.score(X_test, y_test):.3f}")
print("customers predicted to churn:", (silent.predict(X_test) == 1).sum())
    accuracy 0.835
customers predicted to churn: 0
    Accuracy exactly the baseline's, zero churners predicted. Judge with AUC or recall, use class_weight, or choose a threshold. Skip the timing block live; say the rule: kernel SVMs up to about 10,000 rows, LinearSVC beyond (the lesson's table).
Three mistakes to expect, and how to coach each
  • Judging an SVM by accuracy on imbalanced data. The linear SVM that predicts nobody churns scores 0.835, exactly the baseline. Coach: "How many churners did it flag?" Then AUC, recall, or class_weight.
  • Tuning by the training score. gamma=10 on churn: training 0.996, test AUC 0.699, 4,491 of 4,500 customers as support vectors. Coach: "What share of the customers are support vectors? What does that say?" Choose C and gamma by CV.
  • Probabilities from an SVM. predict_proba raises; probability=True warns. Coach: "Do you need a probability or a ranking?" Ranking: decision_function. Probability: calibrate.
Guided lab
  • Lab A (13m, everyone): labs/module-08/l4_3-svm/review_svm_starter.py; reference labs/module-08/l4_3-svm/review_svm_solution.py. Session 7's reviews and split, now with a linear SVM: rank with decision_function, "scale" word counts with TF-IDF, choose C by CV with the key linearsvc__C, read the weights, count reviews inside the street, calibrate.
  • Expected, Lab A: raw counts accuracy 0.859, AUC 0.921 (worse than Naive Bayes' 0.868 / 0.934); TF-IDF 0.884 / 0.936 with 1,856 features; CV AUC peaks at C=1 (0.9269); strongest words not (-2.51) and would buy (1.93); 445 of 774 reviews inside the street, 90 on the wrong side; calibrated average 0.734 vs 0.745.
  • Circulate in Lab A for: roc_auc_score(y_test, svm.predict(X_test)) (0/1 labels: a much lower AUC; ask "how many distinct scores does that give?"); the key typed LinearSVC__C (case matters: the step name is lowercase). Debrief: "Raw counts lost to Naive Bayes; TF-IDF won. For text, TF-IDF is the scaler." And the odd weight: late it comes from "It arrived late. It ...": word pairs ignore sentence boundaries, so read weights skeptically.
  • Lab B (12m, pairs): labs/module-08/l4_3-svm/kernel_by_hand_starter.py; reference labs/module-08/l4_3-svm/kernel_by_hand_solution.py. The ring's lift, on churn: PolynomialFeatures(2) builds 66 columns for a straight LinearSVC; a degree-2 polynomial kernel reaches the same AUC without building any.
  • Expected, Lab B: LinearSVC 0.826; lift + LinearSVC 0.852; poly kernel 0.852 (2,293 support vectors); 66 / 286 / 1,001 / 3,003 columns for degrees 2 to 5; RBF 0.850. Fit times differ per machine. Stretch: the biggest lifted weights include tenure_months^2 (+0.31): the U-shape in tenure that a straight line could not draw.
  • Fast finishers: labs/module-08/l4_3-svm/debug_svm.py: two loud bugs (grid key C; predict_proba) and two silent ones (no scaler; the top 50 taken with np.argsort(scores)[:50], the LEAST risky). Fixed: best C 1, CV 0.848, test 0.850, 32 churners in the top 50.

The tuned SVM retention list (lesson page, "Project: A Tuned SVM Retention List"). In the room: Steps 1 to 3 (10 minutes).

  • Step 1: split the whole table, keep the IDs. Check: 4500 1500 ['C102112', 'C103063', 'C101159'].
  • Step 2: logistic regression CV AUC 0.829 ± 0.021, the bar to beat.
  • Step 3: start the grid (C × gamma, 45 fits) and talk while it runs: which corner will overfit? Done = best {'svc__C': 10, 'svc__gamma': 0.01}, CV AUC 0.854.

Checks for understanding

Ask these during the demo and the labs:

  • You delete a training point far from the boundary and refit. What changes? Answer: nothing: it was not a support vector.
  • Which way do you move C to get a simpler boundary? Answer: down: cheaper violations, wider street.
  • Why does roc_auc_score accept decision_function output? Answer: AUC only needs scores that rank; the signed distance ranks.
  • When would you NOT use a kernel SVM? Answer: many rows (training time grows roughly with the square of the rows): use LinearSVC, logistic regression or trees.

Workbook self-check, items Q1 to Q6.

Answers: Q1 B) A wider street held up by many more support vectors; Q2 ['svc__gamma']; Q3 D) The kernel and the margin are built on distances, and unscaled spend_12m and days_since_last_order would drown out the 0-to-1 columns; Q4 B) AttributeError: This 'Pipeline' has no attribute 'predict_proba'; Q5 A) Recall of churners jumped (0.258 to 0.774) while accuracy fell (0.859 to 0.792); Q6 0.67 0.0. Full set with explanations in the Answer Key.

Differentiation

Support: draw the street for every question; let pairs copy the pipeline lines from the demo and spend the lab time on the numbers. In Lab A, a struggling pair can stop after TODO 3.

Stretch: Lab A's RBF stretch (no gain on text: 0.928); the lesson's Exercise 2 (polynomial kernels on the rings) and the Nystroem bonus on 50,000 transactions.

5.1

Decision Trees

75 min
Objectives (from the lesson page)
  • Explain how a classification tree chooses each split by reducing Gini impurity, and compute the impurity of a node by hand
  • Train DecisionTreeClassifier and DecisionTreeRegressor, and read their rules with export_text and plot_tree
  • Explain where a tree's probabilities come from and why trees do not need scaled features
  • Control overfitting with max_depth, min_samples_leaf, max_leaf_nodes and cost-complexity pruning, chosen by cross-validation
  • Read a tree's feature importances, including what they cannot tell you
Timing in the room
10mFrame: trail junction signposts; Gini
30mLive demo: Steps 1 to 12
25mGuided lab: fraud_rules_starter.py (13m), then sales_tree_starter.py in pairs (12m)
10mStart the lesson project: Steps 1 to 3
Mental model to say

Draw on the trail map: "At the trailhead a signpost asks: has it been more than about 170 days since your last order? Each answer sends the customer down a different trail, to the next signpost. Every trail ends at a campsite with a sign: the share of past customers who walked that way and then left."

"Training a tree means choosing which question to post at each junction: it tries every feature and every threshold and keeps the one that makes the two piles purest. Predicting means walking a new customer down the trails and reading the campsite's sign." Write Gini = chance that two customers picked from a node disagree next to the map.

Live demo (you type, they follow)

New notebook lesson_5_1. The demo uses the 1.1 split and ten features, so every number can be compared with the SVM's.

  1. A tree that asks two questions (3m). "Every model so far did arithmetic. A tree asks one yes-or-no question, splits the pile, and asks again in each pile." Draw the trail map with its signposts while it runs. 
    import numpy as np
import pandas as pd
from sklearn.model_selection import train_test_split
from sklearn.tree import DecisionTreeClassifier, export_text

customers = pd.read_csv("data/customers_churn.csv")
features = ["tenure_months", "orders_12m", "spend_12m", "avg_order_value", "days_since_last_order",
            "returns_12m", "return_rate", "support_tickets_12m", "discount_share", "app_user"]
X = customers[features]
y = customers["churned"]
X_train, X_test, y_train, y_test = train_test_split(X, y, test_size=0.25, stratify=y, random_state=42)

small_tree = DecisionTreeClassifier(max_depth=2, random_state=42)
small_tree.fit(X_train, y_train)
print(export_text(small_tree, feature_names=features, show_weights=True))
    |--- days_since_last_order <= 172.50
|   |--- discount_share <= 0.35
|   |   |--- weights: [2845.00, 175.00] class: 0
|   |--- discount_share >  0.35
|   |   |--- weights: [620.00, 245.00] class: 0
|--- days_since_last_order >  172.50
|   |--- tenure_months <= 5.50
|   |   |--- weights: [31.00, 127.00] class: 1
|   |--- tenure_months >  5.50
|   |   |--- weights: [260.00, 197.00] class: 0
    Read the weights as [stayed, churned]. Ask: "Which campsite would you call first?" (lapsed and brand new: 127 of 158 churned, 80%). Nobody typed 172.5: the tree found it.
  2. Gini by hand (3m). "Pick two customers from a node at random; Gini is the chance they disagree. A good split makes purer children." 
    def gini(counts):
    p = np.asarray(counts) / np.sum(counts)
    return 1 - np.sum(p ** 2)

root = np.bincount(y_train)                                        # [stayed, churned]
left = np.bincount(y_train[X_train["days_since_last_order"] <= 172.5])
right = root - left
weighted = (left.sum() * gini(left) + right.sum() * gini(right)) / root.sum()

print("root", root, " left", left, " right", right)
print(f"Gini root {gini(root):.4f}  left {gini(left):.4f}  right {gini(right):.4f}")
print(f"weighted Gini of the children {weighted:.4f}, decrease {gini(root) - weighted:.4f}")

t = small_tree.tree_
print("scikit-learn's values:", t.impurity[[0, t.children_left[0], t.children_right[0]]].round(4))
    root [3756  744]  left [3465  420]  right [291 324]
Gini root 0.2760  left 0.1928  right 0.4986
weighted Gini of the children 0.2346, decrease 0.0414
scikit-learn's values: [0.276  0.1928 0.4986]
    Your arithmetic matches tree_.impurity exactly. The right child is MORE mixed than the root, but it holds only 615 customers, so it counts for less.
  3. Every threshold, tried (2m). "Why 172.5 and not 150? It tried them all." 
    recency = X_train["days_since_last_order"].to_numpy()
thresholds = np.arange(0, 364) + 0.5
decrease = []
for cut in thresholds:
    left = np.bincount(y_train[recency <= cut], minlength=2)
    right = root - left
    weighted = (left.sum() * gini(left) + right.sum() * gini(right)) / root.sum()
    decrease.append(gini(root) - weighted)

best = thresholds[np.argmax(decrease)]
print(f"best recency threshold: {best}  (impurity decrease {max(decrease):.4f})")
    best recency threshold: 172.5  (impurity decrease 0.0414)
    Show the lesson's decrease-by-threshold chart: flat below 100 days, a peak at 172.5.
  4. Every feature competes (3m). "The same scan for each feature; the winner becomes the root." 
    rows = []
for col in features:
    stump = DecisionTreeClassifier(max_depth=1, random_state=42).fit(X_train[[col]], y_train)
    s = stump.tree_
    children = (s.n_node_samples[1] * s.impurity[1] + s.n_node_samples[2] * s.impurity[2]) / s.n_node_samples[0]
    rows.append({"feature": col, "threshold": s.threshold[0], "decrease": s.impurity[0] - children})

stumps = pd.DataFrame(rows).sort_values("decrease", ascending=False)
print(stumps.round(4).head(5).to_string(index=False))
                  feature  threshold  decrease
days_since_last_order   172.5000    0.0414
       discount_share     0.3505    0.0142
        tenure_months     5.5000    0.0125
            spend_12m   443.2250    0.0114
           orders_12m     6.5000    0.0108
    Recency wins by a factor of three. Then the search repeats inside each child: that is why the two second-level questions differ (Lab A asks the same of fraud). Say "greedy": it never reconsiders a split.
  5. Probabilities come from leaves (3m). "A probability is the churn share of the campsite the customer reaches. Four leaves, four possible numbers." 
    leaf_of = small_tree.apply(X_test)
proba = small_tree.predict_proba(X_test)[:, 1]

per_leaf = pd.DataFrame({"leaf": leaf_of, "p_churn": proba, "churned": y_test.to_numpy()})
print(per_leaf.groupby("leaf").agg(customers=("churned", "size"),
                                   predicted=("p_churn", "first"),
                                   actual=("churned", "mean")).round(3))
          customers  predicted  actual
leaf                              
2          1011      0.058   0.057
3           286      0.283   0.308
5            42      0.804   0.786
6           161      0.431   0.429
    Test churn rates match the training shares: the groups are real. A rule book is fine with four ties; a top-50 list is not.
  6. No scaling needed (2m). "A split asks 'above this value?'. Rescaling moves the threshold, not the customers." 
    from sklearn.preprocessing import StandardScaler

scaler = StandardScaler().set_output(transform="pandas").fit(X_train)
scaled_tree = DecisionTreeClassifier(max_depth=2, random_state=42).fit(scaler.transform(X_train), y_train)

same = np.array_equal(scaled_tree.predict_proba(scaler.transform(X_test)), small_tree.predict_proba(X_test))
print("identical predictions:", same)
print("root threshold, raw vs scaled:", small_tree.tree_.threshold[0], scaled_tree.tree_.threshold[0].round(3))
    identical predictions: True
root threshold, raw vs scaled: 172.5 1.021
  7. Grow too far (4m). "Left alone, a tree splits until every leaf is pure. Watch training and validation AUC as it grows." Predict first: where does the validation AUC peak? 
    from sklearn.model_selection import StratifiedKFold, cross_validate, cross_val_score

cv = StratifiedKFold(n_splits=5, shuffle=True, random_state=42)
rows = []
for depth in [1, 2, 3, 4, 5, 6, 8, 10, None]:
    res = cross_validate(DecisionTreeClassifier(max_depth=depth, random_state=42), X_train, y_train,
                         cv=cv, scoring="roc_auc", return_train_score=True)
    rows.append({"max_depth": str(depth), "train_auc": res["train_score"].mean(), "cv_auc": res["test_score"].mean()})

depth_table = pd.DataFrame(rows)
print(depth_table.round(3).to_string(index=False))
    max_depth  train_auc  cv_auc
        1      0.682   0.678
        2      0.791   0.783
        3      0.834   0.823
        4      0.857   0.839
        5      0.878   0.839
        6      0.897   0.834
        8      0.938   0.744
       10      0.973   0.674
     None      1.000   0.663
    Peak at depth 4 to 5, then down to 0.663 while training reaches 1.000. Bias-variance from Lesson 3.2, in its clearest form.
  8. Leaf size is often the better control (3m). "Require enough customers in every leaf." 
    for leaf in [1, 10, 25, 50, 100, 200]:
    model = DecisionTreeClassifier(min_samples_leaf=leaf, random_state=42)
    auc = cross_val_score(model, X_train, y_train, cv=cv, scoring="roc_auc").mean()
    n_leaves = model.fit(X_train, y_train).get_n_leaves()
    print(f"min_samples_leaf={leaf:3d}  leaves {n_leaves:3d}  CV AUC {auc:.3f}")
    min_samples_leaf=  1  leaves 523  CV AUC 0.663
min_samples_leaf= 10  leaves 186  CV AUC 0.800
min_samples_leaf= 25  leaves 100  CV AUC 0.831
min_samples_leaf= 50  leaves  58  CV AUC 0.848
min_samples_leaf=100  leaves  30  CV AUC 0.844
min_samples_leaf=200  leaves  16  CV AUC 0.828
    50 per leaf: 58 leaves, CV AUC 0.848, the best so far. Mention ccp_alpha (pruning) in one sentence; the lesson page has it.
  9. What the tree used (3m). "Importances add up each feature's impurity decreases." 
    from sklearn.metrics import roc_auc_score

pruned = DecisionTreeClassifier(min_samples_leaf=50, random_state=42).fit(X_train, y_train)
print(f"test ROC AUC {roc_auc_score(y_test, pruned.predict_proba(X_test)[:, 1]):.3f}, leaves {pruned.get_n_leaves()}")

importance = pd.Series(pruned.feature_importances_, index=features).sort_values(ascending=False)
print(importance.round(3))
    test ROC AUC 0.863, leaves 58
days_since_last_order    0.450
tenure_months            0.250
discount_share           0.169
app_user                 0.049
support_tickets_12m      0.034
return_rate              0.020
avg_order_value          0.016
spend_12m                0.012
orders_12m               0.001
returns_12m              0.000
dtype: float64
    Then the three cautions: a low score is not "no effect" (spend overlaps with others), MDI favors many-valued columns (Lesson 5.2 shows a noise column winning), and it describes the model, not the customers.
  10. A single tree is unstable (2m). "Three trees, three random 80% slices of the same customers." 
    rng = np.random.default_rng(42)
subsets = [rng.choice(len(X_train), size=3600, replace=False) for _ in range(3)]

for label, settings in [("unlimited tree", {}), ("pruned tree (min_samples_leaf=50)", {"min_samples_leaf": 50})]:
    preds = []
    for rows in subsets:
        model = DecisionTreeClassifier(random_state=42, **settings).fit(X_train.iloc[rows], y_train.iloc[rows])
        preds.append(model.predict(X_test))
    preds = np.array(preds)
    disagree = (preds.min(axis=0) != preds.max(axis=0)).mean()
    print(f"{label:34s} the three trees disagree on {disagree:.1%} of test customers")
    unlimited tree                     the three trees disagree on 24.6% of test customers
pruned tree (min_samples_leaf=50)  the three trees disagree on 10.3% of test customers
    One customer in four gets a different answer from an unlimited tree. Pruning helps; Lesson 5.2 turns the weakness into a strength.
  11. Mistake: class names in the wrong order (1m). "No error. Read the first leaf: 175 churners of 3,020, labeled churned." 
    print(export_text(small_tree, feature_names=features, class_names=["churned", "stayed"]))
    |--- days_since_last_order <= 172.50
|   |--- discount_share <= 0.35
|   |   |--- class: churned
|   |--- discount_share >  0.35
|   |   |--- class: churned
|--- days_since_last_order >  172.50
|   |--- tenure_months <= 5.50
|   |   |--- class: stayed
|   |--- tenure_months >  5.50
|   |   |--- class: churned
    Build names from classes_: ["stayed" if c == 0 else "churned" for c in model.classes_].
  12. Mistake: depth zero (1m). "The smallest tree is a stump." 
    DecisionTreeClassifier(max_depth=0).fit(X_train, y_train)
    InvalidParameterError: The 'max_depth' parameter of DecisionTreeClassifier must be an int in the range [1, inf) or None. Got 0 instead.
Three mistakes to expect, and how to coach each
  • Reporting the training score. An unlimited tree: training 1.000, test 0.822, baseline 0.835. Coach: "Which customers was that 1.000 measured on?"
  • Ranking with an unpruned tree. Its probabilities are all 0 or 1 (AUC 0.683): you cannot choose a top 50 from ties. Coach: "How many distinct probabilities does it give?" (np.unique). Fix: min_samples_leaf, or a forest next session.
  • Reading importance as truth. spend_12m 0.012, yet churn falls from 24% to 8% across spend quartiles. Coach: "Did the model need it, or does it not matter?"
Guided lab
  • Lab A (13m, everyone): labs/module-08/l5_1-decision-trees/fraud_rules_starter.py; reference labs/module-08/l5_1-decision-trees/fraud_rules_solution.py. A rule book for the payments team: rules with weights, the root's Gini by hand, an unlimited tree judged honestly, size by CV average precision (fraud is rare), a segment table, a zero importance.
  • Expected, Lab A: leaf "velocity_24h > 3.5 and account_age_days <= 366" holds 40 frauds of 42; Gini root 0.0174 (tiny: 0.88% fraud), decrease 0.0018; unlimited tree 433 leaves, training AUC 1.000, test AUC 0.684, AP 0.129; CV AP 0.178 / 0.259 / 0.287 / 0.295 for 4 / 8 / 12 / 16 leaves; the 8-rule book test AUC 0.756, AP 0.253, riskiest segment 15 of 19; items importance 0.000 although frauds buy more items (2.39 vs 1.89).
  • Circulate in Lab A for: bincount without minlength=2 on a child with no frauds; CV by accuracy (every size scores 0.991: ask what accuracy means at 0.88% fraud). Debrief: read the rule book aloud as a reviewer would, especially the night + new device + young account rule (38 of 68 fraud in training): that is an interaction a straight line cannot express.
  • Lab B (12m, pairs): labs/module-08/l5_1-decision-trees/sales_tree_starter.py; reference labs/module-08/l5_1-decision-trees/sales_tree_solution.py. A regression tree for daily store sales: calendar features, a time split, TimeSeriesSplit for the depth, leaves as averages, and the trend a tree cannot follow.
  • Expected, Lab B: baselines $1,967 / $1,670; first question day_of_week <= 4; biggest leaf Boise weekends from May on, $10,059; CV MAE $1,374 (depth 2) to $901 (depth 10); depth 10 test MAE $976; Boise 2025 actual $8,342 vs predicted $7,339 (2024 actual $7,821): a tree predicts averages of past days, so it cannot follow growth.
  • Fast finishers: labs/module-08/l5_1-decision-trees/debug_tree.py: max_depth=0, a text column, class names reversed, training accuracy reported as accuracy on new customers. Fixed: accuracy on new customers 0.851 (baseline 0.835).

The churn rule book (lesson page, "Project: A Churn Rule Book"). In the room: Steps 1 to 3 (10 minutes).

  • Step 2: tree_prep passes numbers through and one-hot encodes categories. Check: 35 columns after encoding.
  • Step 3: baselines by CV: do-nothing 0.500, logistic regression 0.832 ± 0.023.

Checks for understanding

Ask these during the demo and the labs:

  • Why do the two second-level questions differ? Answer: the search repeats inside each child, using only that child's customers.
  • A depth-2 tree gives how many distinct probabilities? Answer: at most four, one per leaf.
  • Does a tree need scaled features? Answer: no: scaling moves thresholds, not customers.
  • Which tells you the right tree size: training score, test score, or CV on the training set? Answer: CV on the training set; the test set once at the end.

Workbook self-check, items Q7 to Q12.

Answers: Q7 0.375; Q8 C) The share of churners among the training customers in the leaf the new customer lands in; Q9 B) 5.0; Q10 D) Choose a size control such as min_samples_leaf by cross-validation on the training set; Q11 A) The tree did not need spend: other features it split on first carried the same information; Q12 C) InvalidParameterError: max_depth must be at least 1 (or None). Full set with explanations in the Answer Key.

Differentiation

Support: have strugglers read export_text aloud as sentences ("if recency is at most 172 days and discount share at most 0.35, 6% churn"); give them the gini function ready-made.

Stretch: the lesson's Exercise 4 (a monotonic constraint on recency) and the Bonus Challenge (write best_split yourself); in Lab B, add ccp_alpha to the depth search.

Closing

Exit ticket (3 questions, no notes, 4 minutes)

Questions on the slide; answers in the workbook's Exit Ticket box or on a sticky note. Sort into "got it" and "not yet": question 1's pile tells you how much of the Session 9 warm-up goes to gamma, question 3's whether the readability-versus-ranking idea landed.

  1. Your SVM scores 0.996 on the training customers and AUC 0.699 on the test set, with 4,491 of 4,500 customers as support vectors. Which dial is set wrong, and how do you choose it properly? Answer: gamma is far too large (each customer's influence is a tiny bubble, so the model memorized the training set; nearly every point is a support vector). Choose C and gamma together with cross-validation on the training set (keys svc__C, svc__gamma), never with the training score.
  2. A node holds 40 stayers and 10 churners. What is its Gini impurity, and what churn probability does a tree give a customer who lands there? Answer: Gini = 1 - (0.8² + 0.2²) = 0.32. The probability is the leaf's churn share: 10 / 50 = 0.2.
  3. The care team wants rules they can pin to the wall AND the best possible top-50 list. What do you give them from today's two models, and why not one model for both? Answer: A small tree (about 12 leaves, size chosen by CV) as the rule book: readable, but its probabilities come in a few big ties. For the list, a model that scores every customer individually: today the tuned SVM (34 of 50; AUC 0.848), and next session a forest (41 of 50). Readability and ranking pull in different directions.

  • Lesson exercises: 4.3 Exercises 1 to 3 (support-vector share, polynomial kernels on the rings, LinearSVC vs SVC); 5.1 Exercises 1 to 3 (impurity by hand, Gini or entropy, a regression tree for Snow gear). Exercise 4 of each is a stretch.

  • Unfinished labs from today: each starter's "Expected when done" block lets them check themselves.

Instructor Kit · Facilitator Guide

Session 9: Random Forests and Gradient Boosting

How to teach Session 9 of Machine Learning Foundations: bagging and random forests (the bootstrap, averaging away variance, decorrelated trees, out-of-bag validation, honest importances) and gradient boosting (small trees that correct each other, the learning rate, early stopping, native categories, XGBoost and LightGBM). Learners leave able to say which error each ensemble fixes, and with a forest call list and a demand forecaster started.

2 lessons 3 hrs session Lessons 5.2–5.3 Session 9 of 11

How to Use This Guide

  • Same rhythm as every session: frame → live demo → guided labs → start the project. Exercises, quizzes and journals are homework.
  • Every code block below was run to produce the output under it (Python 3.12, scikit-learn 1.9.1, XGBoost 3.4, LightGBM 4.7, 4 CPU threads). The demos are the lesson pages' code without the plotting lines: show the pictures from trees_random_forest.html (bagging diagram, the trees curve, the noise-column bars) and trees_gradient_boosting.html (1 / 10 / 300 trees, the validation U-turn).
  • XGBoost and LightGBM outputs may differ in the last digit on another machine (multithreaded floating-point sums); say so once, when you run them.
  • Labs are in labs/module-09/. The two fraud labs continue the kit's payments thread: logistic regression found 53 frauds in its top 100 in Session 1; the eight-rule tree in Session 8 scored AP 0.253; today the forest and the boosters.
The #1 risk of Session 9: two ensemble lessons blur into one

Learners leave saying "forests and boosting are both lots of trees". Keep the two pictures apart all day: a panel of independent, quirky experts whose average cancels their quirks (variance), and a golfer whose every putt aims at what is left (bias). Put both on the board, side by side, and make every exit-ticket answer use one of them. The second risk is CPU time: Step 2 of 5.2 fits 100 trees in a Python loop and Step 9 runs permutation importance; both take a few seconds on a laptop. Start them and talk.

Before the Session

  • ☐ Run the kit check: python3 labs/check_labs.py module-09 (8 files, about 50 seconds). Every line PASS.
  • ☐ Check the libraries on the projector machine: python -c "import xgboost, lightgbm; print(xgboost.__version__, lightgbm.__version__)" (3.4 and 4.7 in the course environment). check_environment.py from Session 1 lists both.
  • ☐ Downloads: none.
  • ☐ Projector machine: (.venv) active in your own pinecrest-ml, JupyterLab started there, notebooks lesson_5_2 and lesson_5_3. Browser tabs: trees_random_forest.html, trees_gradient_boosting.html.
  • ☐ Board, before learners arrive: left, the panel of new hires with an averaging arrow and variance of an average = ρσ² + (1 − ρ)σ²/n; right, a golf green with three putts and F = F + ν · tree.
  • ☐ Print or share: the Session 9 workbook pages; sticky notes for the exit ticket.
