Running it
  • Say: "The final project is one supervised model, built end to end the way this course has taught: frame, leak-safe split, baseline, pipelines, fair comparison, tuning, one look at the test set, a decision in business terms, and an honest report. You choose the problem today; Session 11 is a studio for it; you submit one week after Session 11."
  • The plan template (workbook): the decision the model informs; the target and when the prediction is made; the split (copy from the table, no inventions); the deciding metric, chosen now; the bar to beat; two candidate models; one leakage risk specific to this problem; what result would change your recommendation.
  • Two framing questions for each plan read aloud: "On the day you make a prediction, which of your columns would you not know yet?" and "Which number decides between two models, and why that one?" Praise plans that name a leak and a metric; gently fix any plan that proposes its own random split for demand or fraud.
  • Balance: no quota per problem. Steer learners who struggled with Sessions 5 and 6 toward churn (the best-trodden path); suggest fraud to anyone who wants the hardest evaluation and demand to anyone who enjoyed 5.3. Write each learner's choice on your class list: it decides who you visit first in the studio.

Closing

Exit ticket (3 questions, no notes, 4 minutes)
  1. Why must a stack's meta-model learn from out-of-fold predictions? Answer: in-sample predictions are overconfident (the booster scores 0.999 on its own training rows, 0.859 honestly), so the meta-model would over-trust the most flexible member and fail on new data.
  2. Two models: CV AUC 0.867 and 0.860; paired differences mean +0.007, std 0.008, the first wins 9 of 10 folds. Real? What else decides? Answer: small but consistent (wins nearly every fold; the mean is well above 0.008 / √10 = 0.0025). Then weigh cost, speed and explainability, and confirm once on the test set.
  3. Your final project: which problem, which deciding metric, and what does doing nothing score? Answer: for example "churn, average precision, 0.165 (contact everyone: -$7,620)"; "demand, MAE, per-product mean 13.42"; "fraud, average precision then cost, review nothing $24,000".

  • Lesson exercises: 5.4 Exercises 1 to 3 (weights chosen out-of-fold, a k-NN member, blending by hand); 6.1 Exercises 1 to 3 (the metric changes the winner, a k-NN validation curve, the lapsed flag). Exercise 4 of each is a stretch.
  • Both Quick Quizzes and both journal entries.
  • Before the Next Lesson (from 6.1): keep the project notebook (Lesson 6.2 starts from the same split and make_preprocess); list three boosting hyperparameter names with sorted(make_pipeline(make_preprocess(), HistGradientBoostingClassifier()).get_params()); write down a guess for which settings would close boosting's training-validation gap.
  • Final project, milestone 1: type the plan into the first Markdown cell of final_project.ipynb; run labs/module-11/l6_2-hyperparameter-tuning/final_project_starter.py with PROBLEM set to your choice and copy its finished cells (the split, the bar to beat, a first cross-validated model) into the notebook.
  • Optional: demand_blend_starter.py and demand_curves_starter.py if you did not reach them.
Bring list for Session 11

  • The pinecrest-ml folder with data/ and labs/; Session 11's starters are in labs/module-11/.

Instructor Kit · Facilitator Guide

Session 11: Hyperparameter Tuning and Final Project Studio

How to teach the last session of Machine Learning Foundations: tune models with grid search, random search, successive halving and Optuna, and report a tuned model honestly (nested cross-validation, the test set once); then run a final-project studio on the clock and a lightning-talk showcase. The final project (churn, weekly demand or fraud) is submitted one week later and scored with the rubric in final-assessment.html.

1 lesson + studio 3 hrs session Lesson 6.2 Session 11 of 11

How to Use This Guide

  • Session 11 has two halves. The first is Lesson 6.2 in the usual rhythm (frame, live demo, guided lab); the lesson's own project goes home. The second is the final-project studio and showcase: learners work on the project they chose in Session 10, you coach.
  • Every code block below was run to produce the output printed under it (Python 3.12, scikit-learn 1.9.1, Optuna 5.0, 4 CPU threads). The demo is the lesson's own code, so learners can check every number against hyperparameter_tuning.html.
  • Keep final-assessment.html (kit root) open for the studio: the rubric, the deliverables and the instructor answer notes with reference numbers for all three problems. The studio coaching below gives the short version.
  • This is the last session of the Foundations kit: the wrap-up closes the course (submission logistics, what comes next).
The #1 risk of Session 11: the demo eats the studio

Lesson 6.2 runs several searches; on a slow laptop the grid, the random search, halving and Optuna take minutes each. The studio is the only supervised time learners get on their project, so it must start at 1:20. Before: run the whole demo notebook at home and keep it open: on a slow projector machine, show Optuna's and the nested search's saved output instead of rerunning them. In the room: learners type along only the cells marked in the timing table; they rerun the rest at home. Studio: a learner without milestone 1 does it first (final_project_starter.py, 15 minutes) before anything else.

Before the Session

  • ☐ Run the kit check: python3 labs/check_labs.py module-11: 6 PASS lines (about two minutes).
  • ☐ Run the 6.2 demo once in a notebook lesson_6_2 on the projector machine and keep its outputs; note how long Optuna takes there.
  • ☐ Read the instructor answer notes in final-assessment.html for all three problems (the traps and the reference numbers); print the rubric page and the scoring sheet.
  • ☐ Class list: each learner's chosen problem from Session 10 and whether they emailed milestone 1. Plan your studio rounds: learners without milestone 1 first, then fraud (the hardest evaluation), then demand, then churn.
  • ☐ Board: the milestone ladder (1 split and bar to beat · 2 fair leaderboard · 3 tuned on training rows · 4 test once and a decision · 5 one-page report), a column per learner for a sticker, and the showcase card (decision · bar to beat · best CV ± spread · one trap avoided · what is left).
  • ☐ Print: peer feedback cards (two lines: one strength, one question), one per learner per talk they watch, or use sticky notes.
Known environment traps
SymptomCauseFix
ValueError: Invalid parameter 'C' for estimator Pipeline(...)Hyperparameter name without the stepmodel__C; sorted(pipe.get_params()) lists the names.
TypeError: Parameter grid for parameter 'model__C' needs to be a list ...A single value, or a scipy distribution, in GridSearchCV[1.0], or use RandomizedSearchCV for distributions.
ValueError: For multi-metric scoring, the parameter refit must be set ...Two scorers and no deciderrefit="average_precision" (the deciding metric).

Warm-up

0:00

Warm-up: Ensembles and Model Selection

10 min
Five retrieval questions (answers after each)
  1. Five 70%-accurate classifiers always make exactly the same predictions. How accurate is their majority vote? Answer: 70%: identical voters are one voter repeated.
  2. Where must a stacking meta-model's training features come from? Answer: Out-of-fold predictions on the training rows (cross_val_predict, or StackingClassifier's cv).
  3. Why pass one splitter object with a fixed random_state to every candidate? Answer: So all are scored on the same folds and can be compared fold by fold (paired differences).
  4. A learning curve's training and validation scores meet at 0.83 and stay flat. Next step? Answer: Better features or a more flexible model (high bias); more rows of the same data will not help.
  5. Boosting's learning curve: training 1.0, validation still rising to 0.861. What would close the gap? Answer: Regularization (smaller trees, larger leaves, a smaller learning rate) or more data. Today's lesson tests it: max_leaf_nodes 7 lifts CV AUC to 0.8795.
Milestone-1 check (3 minutes)
  • Hands up per problem: churn, demand, fraud. Then: "Who has a split, the bar to beat and a first cross-validated score?" Note the learners without it: they start the studio with final_project_starter.py.
  • Quick sanity numbers to listen for: churn 4,500 / 1,500 and random-order AP 0.165; demand 5,088 / 4,896 and per-product mean MAE 13.42; fraud 41,738 / 8,262 with 374 / 64 frauds and review-nothing $24,000. A different split size means a different split: fix it first.

Lesson Plans

6.2

Hyperparameter Tuning

60 min
Objectives (from the lesson page)
  • Tune a whole pipeline with GridSearchCV and RandomizedSearchCV, naming its hyperparameters as step__param, and read cv_results_
  • Choose sensible search ranges (log scales, distributions from scipy.stats) and explain why random search usually beats a grid of the same size
  • Speed up a search with successive halving (HalvingRandomSearchCV)
  • Run a seeded Optuna study that learns where to search next
  • Explain why best_score_ is optimistic, estimate a tuned model honestly with nested cross-validation, and use the test set once
Timing in the room
10mFrame: parameters versus hyperparameters; the camp stove; space, budget, objective
30mLive demo: default, grid for C, 3 × 3 grid, random search, halving, Optuna, nested CV, test once, three errors (learners type Steps 1 to 4 and 8)
20mGuided lab: tune_fraud_starter.py
Mental model to say

"A camp stove has two knobs: fuel flow and air. You cannot calculate the best setting from the manual; you try a setting, boil a pot, time it, and adjust. Every trial costs fuel, so a smart camper tries a few spread-out settings, notices which knob matters, and spends the remaining fuel near the best results."

Map it on the board: a trial = one full cross-validation run; the fuel = computing time; the strategy = which settings get tried. Grid tries every combination; random samples; halving runs a tournament on growing data; Optuna learns from past trials. Then the honesty rule: "The best-looking setting looks better than it is: the best of many noisy scores. Report a nested estimate or the test score, once."

Live demo (you type, they follow)

Notebook lesson_6_2, same split and make_preprocess as 6.1 (learners can copy them from their 6.1 project notebook).

  1. The number to beat (3m). "Lesson 6.1 left a puzzle: default boosting lost narrowly to a forest and its learning curve showed a perfect training score. Its settings control exactly that. First: what does the default score, and what are its settings called?" Short step names, prep and model, keep the names short. 
    import numpy as np
import pandas as pd
import matplotlib.pyplot as plt
from sklearn.model_selection import train_test_split, StratifiedKFold, cross_val_score
from sklearn.compose import ColumnTransformer
from sklearn.pipeline import Pipeline, make_pipeline
from sklearn.impute import SimpleImputer
from sklearn.preprocessing import StandardScaler, OneHotEncoder
from sklearn.ensemble import HistGradientBoostingClassifier

customers = pd.read_csv("data/customers_churn.csv")
numeric = ["tenure_months", "orders_12m", "spend_12m", "avg_order_value", "days_since_last_order",
           "returns_12m", "return_rate", "support_tickets_12m", "discount_share", "app_user",
           "email_opens_90d"]
categorical = ["home_store", "region", "age_group", "loyalty_tier", "preferred_category"]
X_train, X_test, y_train, y_test = train_test_split(
    customers[numeric + categorical], customers["churned"],
    test_size=0.25, stratify=customers["churned"], random_state=42)

def make_preprocess():
    return ColumnTransformer([
        ("num", make_pipeline(SimpleImputer(strategy="median"), StandardScaler()), numeric),
        ("cat", make_pipeline(SimpleImputer(strategy="most_frequent"),
                              OneHotEncoder(handle_unknown="ignore")), categorical),
    ])

hgb = Pipeline([("prep", make_preprocess()),
                ("model", HistGradientBoostingClassifier(random_state=42))])
cv = StratifiedKFold(n_splits=5, shuffle=True, random_state=42)

default_auc = cross_val_score(hgb, X_train, y_train, cv=cv, scoring="roc_auc")
print(f"default boosting, 5-fold AUC: {default_auc.mean():.4f} +/- {default_auc.std():.4f}")
print([name for name in hgb.get_params() if name.startswith("model__")][:8])
    default boosting, 5-fold AUC: 0.8615 +/- 0.0132
['model__categorical_features', 'model__class_weight', 'model__early_stopping', 'model__interaction_cst', 'model__l2_regularization', 'model__learning_rate', 'model__loss', 'model__max_bins']
    Write 0.8615 on the board: the number to beat. model__learning_rate is step name, two underscores, parameter.
  2. A grid for one knob (3m). "Logistic regression has one knob that matters, C. Regularization works across factors of ten, so the values are log-spaced." 
    from sklearn.linear_model import LogisticRegression
from sklearn.model_selection import GridSearchCV

logit = Pipeline([("prep", make_preprocess()), ("model", LogisticRegression(max_iter=1000))])
logit_search = GridSearchCV(logit, {"model__C": [0.001, 0.01, 0.1, 1, 10, 100]},
                            cv=cv, scoring="roc_auc")
logit_search.fit(X_train, y_train)

results = pd.DataFrame(logit_search.cv_results_)
print(results[["param_model__C", "mean_test_score", "std_test_score", "rank_test_score"]].round(4).to_string(index=False))
     param_model__C  mean_test_score  std_test_score  rank_test_score
          0.001           0.8267          0.0218                6
          0.010           0.8336          0.0240                1
          0.100           0.8330          0.0235                2
          1.000           0.8324          0.0229                3
         10.000           0.8320          0.0228                4
        100.000           0.8320          0.0228                5
    From 0.01 upward the scores differ by less than their standard deviations: logistic regression is not overfitting (its flat learning curve said so), and no C lifts it to boosting's level.
  3. A 3 × 3 grid for boosting (4m). "Now a model where settings matter." Start it; while it runs, write 9 × 5 = 45 on the board and add a third knob with five values: 225. 
    grid = {"model__learning_rate": [0.05, 0.1, 0.2],
        "model__max_leaf_nodes": [7, 15, 31]}
grid_search = GridSearchCV(hgb, grid, cv=cv, scoring="roc_auc", n_jobs=-1)
grid_search.fit(X_train, y_train)

n_settings = len(grid_search.cv_results_["params"])
print(f"{n_settings} settings x {cv.get_n_splits()} folds = {n_settings * cv.get_n_splits()} fits, plus 1 refit")
print("best:", grid_search.best_params_)
print(f"best CV AUC: {grid_search.best_score_:.4f}")
    9 settings x 5 folds = 45 fits, plus 1 refit
best: {'model__learning_rate': 0.05, 'model__max_leaf_nodes': 7}
best CV AUC: 0.8795
    Small trees win (7 leaves instead of 31): the overfitting cure, and 0.8795 beats the default by well over a standard error.
  4. Random search (4m). "Same budget idea, sampled from distributions: loguniform for rates, randint for sizes. And two more knobs a grid could not afford." 
    from scipy.stats import loguniform, randint
from sklearn.model_selection import RandomizedSearchCV

space = {"model__learning_rate": loguniform(0.01, 0.3),
         "model__max_leaf_nodes": randint(4, 64),
         "model__min_samples_leaf": randint(10, 200),
         "model__l2_regularization": loguniform(1e-3, 10)}
random_search = RandomizedSearchCV(hgb, space, n_iter=12, cv=cv, scoring="roc_auc",
                                   random_state=42, n_jobs=-1)
random_search.fit(X_train, y_train)

print(f"best CV AUC: {random_search.best_score_:.4f}")
print({k: round(float(v), 4) if isinstance(v, float) else int(v)
       for k, v in random_search.best_params_.items()})
    best CV AUC: 0.8764
{'model__l2_regularization': 0.0292, 'model__learning_rate': 0.0472, 'model__max_leaf_nodes': 50, 'model__min_samples_leaf': 199}
    50 leaves but at least 199 customers per leaf and a slow rate: a different route to the same cure. Several settings are about equally good; that is normal.
  5. Successive halving (4m). "A tournament: 27 candidates on 500 rows, the best third go through with three times the rows." Remind them of the experimental import line. 
    from sklearn.experimental import enable_halving_search_cv  # noqa: F401  (switches the feature on)
from sklearn.model_selection import HalvingRandomSearchCV

halving = HalvingRandomSearchCV(hgb, space, n_candidates=27, factor=3, min_resources=500,
                                cv=cv, scoring="roc_auc", random_state=42, n_jobs=-1)
halving.fit(X_train, y_train)

rounds = pd.DataFrame(halving.cv_results_).groupby("iter").agg(
    candidates=("n_resources", "size"), rows_per_candidate=("n_resources", "first"),
    best_auc=("mean_test_score", "max"))
print(rounds.round(4))
print(f"best CV AUC: {halving.best_score_:.4f}")
          candidates  rows_per_candidate  best_auc
iter                                          
0             27                 500    0.8192
1              9                1500    0.8555
2              3                4500    0.8729
best CV AUC: 0.8729
    40,500 rows fitted in total, the cost of 9 full candidates, yet 27 screened. The catch: a slow starter can be knocked out early.
  6. Optuna learns where to look (5m). "Grid and random search never learn from their own results. Optuna's TPE does." On a slow machine, run it before the session and show the saved output; it is 100 fits. 
    import optuna

optuna.logging.set_verbosity(optuna.logging.WARNING)   # hide one log line per trial

def objective(trial):
    params = {
        "model__learning_rate": trial.suggest_float("learning_rate", 0.01, 0.3, log=True),
        "model__max_leaf_nodes": trial.suggest_int("max_leaf_nodes", 4, 64),
        "model__min_samples_leaf": trial.suggest_int("min_samples_leaf", 10, 200),
        "model__l2_regularization": trial.suggest_float("l2_regularization", 1e-3, 10, log=True),
    }
    model = hgb.set_params(**params)
    return cross_val_score(model, X_train, y_train, cv=cv, scoring="roc_auc", n_jobs=-1).mean()

study = optuna.create_study(direction="maximize", sampler=optuna.samplers.TPESampler(seed=42))
study.optimize(objective, n_trials=20)

print(f"best CV AUC: {study.best_value:.4f} (trial number {study.best_trial.number}, counting from 0)")
print({k: round(v, 4) for k, v in study.best_params.items()})
    best CV AUC: 0.8793 (trial number 12, counting from 0)
{'learning_rate': 0.0609, 'max_leaf_nodes': 5, 'min_samples_leaf': 183, 'l2_regularization': 0.0094}
    Level with the grid. The differences between searches are a few thousandths, far smaller than the fold spread; what they agree on matters more: every search beats the default.
  7. Nested cross-validation (3m). "best_score_ is the best of many noisy numbers. To estimate the whole procedure honestly, cross-validate the search itself." 
    inner = RandomizedSearchCV(hgb, space, n_iter=6, scoring="roc_auc", random_state=42, n_jobs=-1,
                           cv=StratifiedKFold(n_splits=3, shuffle=True, random_state=0))
outer_cv = StratifiedKFold(n_splits=3, shuffle=True, random_state=1)
nested = cross_val_score(inner, X_train, y_train, cv=outer_cv, scoring="roc_auc")
print("outer-fold AUCs:", nested.round(4))
print(f"nested estimate of the whole tuning procedure: {nested.mean():.4f}")
    outer-fold AUCs: [0.8609 0.8528 0.8907]
nested estimate of the whole tuning procedure: 0.8681
    0.868: about 0.01 below the best scores, still above the default's 0.8615. The gain is real, a little smaller than best_score_ claims.
  8. The test set, once (2m). "Build the model with Optuna's settings, fit on all training rows, and compare with the default for reference." 
    from sklearn.base import clone
from sklearn.metrics import roc_auc_score

tuned = clone(hgb).set_params(**{f"model__{k}": v for k, v in study.best_params.items()})
default = clone(hgb).set_params(model__learning_rate=0.1, model__max_leaf_nodes=31,
                                model__min_samples_leaf=20, model__l2_regularization=0.0)
for name, model in [("default boosting", default), ("tuned boosting", tuned)]:
    model.fit(X_train, y_train)
    print(f"{name:17s} test AUC {roc_auc_score(y_test, model.predict_proba(X_test)[:, 1]):.3f}")
    default boosting  test AUC 0.860
tuned boosting    test AUC 0.874
    0.874 against 0.860: the gain survives unseen customers. clone matters because the Optuna objective changed hgb in place.
  9. Three error messages (2m). "Read the last line each time." Run the three cells one after another. 
    GridSearchCV(logit, {"C": [0.1, 1, 10]}, cv=cv).fit(X_train, y_train)

GridSearchCV(logit, {"model__C": 1.0}, cv=cv).fit(X_train, y_train)

GridSearchCV(logit, {"model__C": [0.1, 1]}, cv=cv,
             scoring=["roc_auc", "average_precision"]).fit(X_train, y_train)
    ValueError: Invalid parameter 'C' for estimator Pipeline(steps=[('prep', ... Valid parameters are: ['memory', 'steps', 'transform_input', 'verbose'].
TypeError: Parameter grid for parameter 'model__C' needs to be a list or a numpy array, but got 1.0 (of type float) instead. Single values need to be wrapped in a list with one element.
ValueError: For multi-metric scoring, the parameter refit must be set to a scorer key or a callable to refit an estimator with the best parameter sett...
    No step name → model__C. A single value → [1.0] (and distributions belong to RandomizedSearchCV). Two metrics → refit="average_precision".
Three mistakes to expect, and how to coach each
  • Tuning on the test set. A loop that scores settings on X_test, or early stopping on it. Coach: "Which rows chose this setting? Then which rows are left to grade it?" Every search runs on the training rows only.
  • Linear grids for log-scale knobs. [1, 2, 3, 4, 5] for C: the scores agree to three decimals and nothing was explored. Coach: "From the model's point of view, are 1 and 5 far apart?" Use np.logspace or loguniform.
  • Promising best_score_. "The model scores 0.8795." Coach with the three numbers on the slide: best_score_ 0.8795, nested 0.868, test 0.874. "Which one did the search choose with?"
Guided lab
  • Lab A (20m): labs/module-11/l6_2-hyperparameter-tuning/tune_fraud_starter.py; reference labs/module-11/l6_2-hyperparameter-tuning/tune_fraud_solution.py. The lesson's moves on fraud: a written space, a budgeted random search on January to May, the leaderboard, June once for default and tuned, the settings saved as JSON. It takes about 40 seconds to run when finished: start it, then read the leaderboard code.
  • Expected: default 3-fold AP 0.443 +/- 0.032; 24 fits; best CV AP 0.515 (learning_rate 0.0186, max_leaf_nodes 24, min_samples_leaf 170); 5 of 8 settings within one std of the best, all with small rates and large leaves, while the one rate of 0.254 collapses to 0.262 +/- 0.169; June AP 0.477 default vs 0.544 tuned, 32 vs 38 frauds in the top 100.
  • Circulate for: a model__ prefix on a bare booster (no pipeline here, so no prefix); scoring the search on June; reading the 0.254 row as "bad luck" (it is a rate so large that some folds blow up: the std says so). Debrief (2m): "Tuning helps a lot here because the default overfits 374 frauds. Same cure as churn: smaller steps and bigger leaves. And notice how many settings are about as good as the winner: what they share matters more than the exact winner."
  • Fast finishers: labs/module-11/l6_2-hyperparameter-tuning/winners_curse_starter.py: tune a tree on shuffled labels and watch best_score_ climb to 0.537 on pure noise while the nested estimate stays at 0.501.
Checks for understanding

Ask these out loud:

  • A grid of 4 × 5 × 3 values with cv=5: how many fits? Answer: 300, plus one refit.
  • Why loguniform for the learning rate? Answer: it matters across orders of magnitude; loguniform samples as many values between 0.01 and 0.03 as between 0.1 and 0.3.
  • What does nested cross-validation estimate? Answer: how well the whole procedure (search, then refit the winner) does on unseen data.
  • Tuning did not beat the default on your project. Is that a failure? Answer: no: an honest negative result. The workbook's demand example: the best of 8 settings is worse than the default (CV 8.73 vs 8.49; test 8.20 vs 8.05). Report the default.

Workbook self-check, items Q1 to Q12 (project skills; learners do them at home).

Answers: Q1 C) Which customers should get the $15 retention offer next quarter, judged by profit on held-out customers, against offering to everyone (-$7,620)?; Q2 A) The per-product 2024 mean: MAE 13.42; Q3 D) On the planning date, last week's sales are unknown for almost every 2025 week: it is a framing leak; Q4 60; Q5 B) 0.614, the one-time test score (or a nested CV estimate), with best_score_ described only as what chose the settings; Q6 C) It tries 9 different values of the important hyperparameter instead of 3; Q7 A) Train January to May, test June, never shuffling months together; Q8 [1.e-03 1.e-02 1.e-01 1.e+00 1.e+01]; Q9 D) 0.25; Q10 B) Tuned boosting: test ROC AUC 0.874 against 0.860 for the default, on the same 1,500 test customers, after a search on the training rows only; the nested CV estimate of the procedure is 0.868; Q11 C) $5,265: the threshold was chosen without the test set, so its test profit is an honest estimate; Q12 415. Full set with explanations in the Answer Key.

Differentiation

Support: learners who are behind type only Steps 1, 3 and 8 and read the rest; in the lab, give them the space dict from the demo (drop the model__ prefix) so they can focus on reading the leaderboard.

Stretch: the lesson's Exercise 4 (halving over boosting rounds) and Bonus Challenge (tune the forest with Optuna and rerun 6.1's paired comparison); winners_curse_starter.py.

Final Project Studio

1:20

Final Project Studio

55 min
Timing
5mMilestone board: every learner puts a sticker on their current milestone; learners without milestone 1 start final_project_starter.py now
20mMilestone 2: a fair leaderboard on the training rows (baseline + at least two real candidates, one splitter, the deciding metric, mean ± std, paired check of the top two)
25mMilestone 3: a budgeted search on the training rows (10 to 15 settings, log scales); churn and fraud also tune the decision threshold for the business cost with TunedThresholdClassifierCV
5mEach learner writes their three numbers so far on the board: bar to beat, best CV ± std, what is next
How to run the studio
  • Say: "The test set stays locked today. Everything you choose, you choose on the training rows. You open the test set once, at home, after the choice, and you report what it says."
  • Circulate by problem, with the coaching notes below. Spend at most four minutes per learner per round; ask questions rather than fixing code: "Which rows chose this?", "What does doing nothing score?", "Is that gap bigger than the fold spread?"
  • Pair learners on the same problem for a five-minute check after Milestone 2: compare split sizes and baselines (they must match exactly) and each other's leaderboard.
  • A learner who finishes Milestone 3 early: write the report skeleton (Question, Answer, Evidence, Caveats, Method) and the README, or try one feature idea from Lesson 2.3 on the same folds.
Coaching notes by problem (reference numbers from the instructor's own run; full notes in final-assessment.html)
ProblemWhere learners should landTraps to look for in the studio
A · ChurnLogistic CV AP 0.528 ± 0.042, default boosting 0.608, forest 0.619; tuned boosting best CV AP 0.636 (test 0.614 once); logistic with four shape features CV AP 0.643. Threshold tuned on training ≈ 0.222, test profit $5,235 with 395 offers (everyone: -$7,620).cancellation_reason in X (test AUC 0.939: too good to be true); accuracy as the metric (doing nothing scores 0.835); a threshold picked on the test set (best possible $5,535 at 0.27: optimistic); reporting best_score_.
B · Weekly demandPer-product mean MAE 13.42; Poisson boosting default 8.05 on 2025; CV on blocks of 2024 weeks 8.49 ± 1.34. A 15-setting search is NOT better (CV 8.57, test 8.22): an honest negative.A shuffled split (MAE 7.36: flattering) or shuffled CV folds (7.6); last week's sales as a feature for a start-of-year forecast; forgetting that 2024 has 53 week rows (5,088, not 4,992); ignoring that the forecast is about 6% low overall (2025 grew: 121,456 forecast vs 128,970 sold).
C · FraudLogistic CV AP 0.506 ± 0.054; default boosting 0.443 (overfits); gentle or tuned boosting about 0.528 to 0.529. Threshold tuned on January to May: June cost $9,900 (tuned booster) or $10,285 (logistic), against $24,000 for reviewing nothing.A stratified random split over all months; accuracy (doing nothing scores 0.9923 in June); a threshold tuned on June (tuned booster $9,395: optimistic); claiming one model beats another by a few hundred dollars (one fraud is $375; June has only 64).

Showcase

2:15

Showcase: Lightning Talks

35 min
Running the showcase
  • Format: two minutes each, from the notebook or one slide, then 30 seconds for one question. The card on the board: the decision · the bar to beat · the best CV score with its spread · one trap avoided · what is left. Keep strict time: a phone timer on the projector.
  • Groups larger than 12: split into two rooms or tables with a peer timekeeper, or run a gallery walk (each learner leaves the notebook open on the card's five lines; others rotate and leave feedback cards).
  • Peer cards: each listener writes one strength and one question for two talks; the speaker keeps them for the report's Caveats and Next questions.
  • Your role: one sentence of praise per talk, aimed at a habit ("you showed the spread", "you named the leak"). Write down one strength per learner for the written feedback. The showcase is practice and is not scored.
  • Listen for: a test score already opened (ask whether anything was changed after it: if yes, the report must say so); "accuracy 99%" for fraud; a demand forecaster scored on shuffled folds. Raise these privately after the session, not in front of the room.

Closing

Exit ticket (3 questions, 4 minutes)
  1. Why is a search's best_score_ not the number to promise? Answer: it is the best of many noisy scores (the winner's curse), so it is biased upward; promise a nested estimate or the test score from one look.
  2. Write the three numbers your report will lead with. Answer (example): churn: random-order AP 0.165 → CV AP 0.636 ± 0.04 → test AP once; plus the profit at a threshold chosen on training rows.
  3. What is the one thing left before you submit? Answer: any honest answer; sort the notes to plan the follow-up emails (learners with "test set" or "report" left are on track; "leaderboard" or "split" left need a check-in this week).
Homework (about 2 to 3 hours, plus the final project)
  • Lesson 6.2 project (tune the retention model, Steps 1 to 6: default CV AP 0.608, best 0.636, test 0.600 → 0.614, top 50: 40 → 43 churners, best_params.json).
  • Lesson 6.2 exercises 1 to 3 (which knob mattered, a k-NN grid, two metrics one decider); Exercise 4 is a stretch. The Quick Quiz and the journal entry.
  • Final project: milestones 4 and 5: open the test set once, make the decision in business terms, write the one-page report.md and the README; submit the folder within one week (details and rubric: final-assessment.html).
  • Optional: winners_curse_starter.py if you did not reach it.
Closing the course (5 minutes of the wrap-up)
  • Thank the group and name, out loud, the habits they now have: a baseline first, leak-safe splits, pipelines, shared folds, the test set once, decisions in dollars, honest negatives.
  • Submission logistics: the project folder (notebook that reruns, report.md, README.md, results files) within one week, by the channel you use; written feedback within two weeks, with one strength per rubric row.
  • What next: the Advanced Machine Learning kit starts from exactly here (Lesson 7.1 opens up a gradient-boosting churn model and asks what it learned). Keep the final-project notebook and best_params.json: the Advanced kit's churn case study (Lesson 13.1) tunes the same kind of booster again, so their own numbers make a useful comparison, and its capstone is the next step up from this project.
  • Bring list for the next course (if they continue): the same laptop and environment, pinecrest-ml with data/, the final-project notebook, and the image data and pretrained weights (about 175 MB) downloaded at home before its Session 5 (check_environment.py --download, as its setup guide says).
  • Ask for course feedback (two questions: what helped most, what to change).

If You Run Long or Short

Running long
  • Protect: the studio start at 1:20 and at least 20 minutes of showcase.
  • Cut first: demo Step 5 (halving), then Step 6 (show Optuna's saved output), then the lab's TODO 3 to 5 (they go home with the expected output).
  • Showcase under time pressure: one-minute talks with the five-line card, or a gallery walk.
Running short
  • Run winners_curse_starter.py for everyone: the most memorable picture of why best_score_ is optimistic.
  • Extend the studio: Milestone 4 rehearsal (write the report's first three sentences: answer, evidence, caveat).
